Trump likely to lash out in foreign policy if Democrats take Congress, warns ex-US ambassador to EU

Support us

Good morning.

Cary is a small town in North Carolina. Downtown has a library, an Amtrak train station, a beautiful park with a fountain, a post office, an art house cinema, and a single main street with wide sidewalks that divides it in two.

Taipei 101, a Taiwanese takeout on East Chatham Street serves up generous portions of tasty food. I've been there myself a few times.

The town is also around a 30-minute drive to the state's capital city, Raleigh, where former North Carolina governor Roy Cooper is facing Donald Trump's hand-picked Michael Whatley for a Senate seat in the US mid-term elections.

I first came across Cooper's name (as a candidate) in March when I saw it posted on somebody's fence at a stoplight in Cary. Some of the pro-Cooper slogans included "Truth over Lies, Hope over Fear", "Pro-Woman, Pro-Child, Pro-Choice", and "ICE out for Good"...

Continue reading this article on euobserver.com or subscribe here for free to get our daily editorial delivered to your inbox in full.

Nikolaj Nielsen, home affairs editor

What else you need to know

Serbia’s Student List has been inspirational, but the big test will be forming a stable anti-corruption government

The Serbian campaign looked exceptional. I cannot think of many cases of an opposition that was so decentralised and so democratic internally. The closest comparison may be the early German Greens that had a strong Basisdemokratie [“Grassroots Democracy”] out of conviction; or the faceless and fluid democratic movement in Hong Kong (“Be Water”) out of necessity. This is inspirational.

Czech public broadcasters hold historic strike following government vote to abolish license fees

Thousand of Czechs joined public TV and radio workers in their walkout over the Czech government’s moves to bring public broadcasting under its political control.

Microsoft cloud in Ireland under fire for hosting Israeli app used to monitor Palestinians in occupied West Bank

Microsoft hosts key software used to monitor movements of Palestinians in the occupied West Bank. Some of this is being hosted in Ireland.

Firefighters from 19 countries protest EU budget cuts in Brussels

Around 1,000 firefighters from 19 countries gathered in Brussels Tuesday to demand more equipment, more staff, and adequate healthcare, only weeks before key EU budget negotiations.

Merz, Meloni and the EU: How political attacks on rescue charities are leaving migrants to drown

Berlin, Rome and Brussels have played a role in the decline in an increasingly vital force in the Mediterranean. Search-and-rescue ships have been hampered by legal hurdles and funding cuts, while the central Mediterranean has become a grave for tens of thousands of people.

Czech MEP Markéta Gregorová stopped at Chinese border in ‘pseudo-scare tactics’

MEP Markéta Gregorová claims to have been stopped in Beijing in what she called “pseudo-scare tactics” over the arrest of a Chinese national accused of spying in Czech Republic.

When adults take over youth protests, EU children’s own demands vanish

As youth protests spread from France to Belgium, Spain, Italy, Greece and Albania, Europe is confronting a stark test of whether under‑18s are treated as political actors in their own right, or merely as props in adult power struggles.

Andrew Tate’s toxic legacy in Romania is warping its democracy

While prosecutors chase the Tates across borders, their macho fantasy of domination is already shaping how a frustrated generation of Romanian men sees women and democracy itself.

Russia faces a strategic crisis and record losses, but thanks to Trump it sees things positively (Ukraine Battlefield update, Day 1,713)

Russia is dropping bombs on Ukrainian schools and hospitals, while losing tens of thousands of soldiers in just a few weeks.

Listen: What’s at stake for Europe in the US midterms?

Americans are voting today in the midterm elections. All 435 seats in the House of Representatives are up for grabs, along with a third of the Senate and according to the polls, the Republicans are expected to lose seats.

Whisper it, but the UK’s path back to the EU is beginning to take shape

Kaja Kallas and Wes Streeting set the scene for a process of improving EU-UK relations on Monday. Their logical conclusion is the UK rejoining, some day.

A fight over Poland’s top court is fuelling tensions ahead of next year’s election, with judicial independence, EU relations and the country’s future in the bloc all caught up in the dispute.

On an Italian dock, the EU’s new asylum pact gives rescued migrants a rushed ‘tick-box’ border screening

The impact of the EU’s new pact on migration and asylum is clearly visible from an Italian port quay on the day of a migrant disembarkation. First touching land after having survived a sail aboard an overcrowded dinghy in a storm, 28 migrants were trooped through a sped-up “tick-boxing” screening, which experts worry will not accurately measure the vulnerabilities they’re up against.

Scandal-hit EU innovation agency disputes auditors’ finding that €8.7m may be unrecoverable

The European Institute of Innovation & Technology is under renewed scrutiny after an audit suggested “a material level of error” and flagged that it could fail to recover €8.7m from its beneficiaries. This comes after several investigations into the agency were launched in recent years.

The Polls Are Closed. Here’s What Comes Next.

The Polls Are Closed. Here’s What Comes Next.

Q&A with former U.S. Attorney Joyce Vance on the work that made the midterms possible and the legal challenges that could still lie ahead.

Jessica Yellin

Nov 4

READ IN APP

For daily news, updates, and analysis, be sure to follow us on Instagram. If you are able to support independent journalism, please make a tax-deductible donation to News Not Noise here.

Election Day has been surprisingly calm. But does that mean the threats to the results are behind us?

Former U.S. Attorney and legal analyst Joyce Vance joins me to break down the legal battles that could still lie ahead, from the Justice Department’s reported targeting of election lawyer Marc Elias to concerns over federal poll monitors and potential challenges to election certification.

Vance explains why she’s feeling more optimistic than expected, how lawyers and election officials have prepared for potential interference, and why she believes the courts are ready to protect the results.

News Not Noise is a reader-supported publication. To receive exclusive content and support our work, please consider becoming a paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

Jessica:

Hi everyone! Republican Ashley Moody has won Florida’s Senate race. That is a Republican hold. No change in the vote totals there. In North Carolina, Democrat Roy Cooper has won, flipping a Republican held seat. That is a pickup for Democrats. Tight now between those two seats, one Republican holds one Democratic pickup. It’s plus one for Dems.

We’re going to turn our conversation a bit away from counting votes and analyzing the map, to considering Donald Trump’s attempts to undermine this election and what might come next. So far, today has been relatively calm, but not without incident altogether. I can’t tell you how pleased I am that we have such a wonderful person joining us to talk about this.

Joyce Vance is joining. She’s the former U.S. attorney for the Northern District of Alabama and a legal analyst. You know her if you watch any news at all. She will help us understand what happens next and what we can actually do to protect the results.

Jessica:

Joyce, first of all, how are you? How are you feeling tonight?

Joyce:

I am upbeat and optimistic. It’s a great day to be a Democrat in America — and someone who’s pro-democracy.

Jessica:

I think part of the reason things have gone smoothly and well today is because people like you helped everybody get very, very prepared for all eventualities.

Joyce:

I don’t get any credit for today. But there is just such an amazing conglomeration of lawyers and activists, union leaders — you know, just the pro-democracy movement in this country, that has made it clear to Donald Trump that they don’t intend to sit down and take it any longer. As an American citizen, I find that to just be a wonderful sort of a vibe for the day. I don’t think I was expecting to feel this good. I thought that there would be more drama and trauma before we got to this point.

Jessica:

Me too. I think we all have a lot of election PTSD from past years. Like, is it really okay? Things are okay?

So, though the day has been largely uneventful, it is the case that the Justice Department sent letters to Democratic election lawyer Mark Elias and others. Can you help us understand: What are they demanding? What do you make of this timing?

Joyce:

We know very little about this, other than the fact that the letters were sent, but they appear to be what in Justice Department parlance is called “target letters.” These are letters that prosecutors send to people that they are preparing to indict shortly before they indict them, to give them the opportunity to make their final case or to testify in the grand jury if they want to. What they really end up being, in virtually all cases, is something that motivates plea dealing before there is an indictment — really the best time that a defendant can come in and make a deal.

I don’t think that we will see Mark Elias cutting a plea deal with the federal government for nonexistent crimes. It’s worth noting, by the way, that even if there were crimes here, which I just think it’s very hard to contemplate — this is around the situation with the Alaska race, where there’s an allegation of ballot stacking, which may be awful, but it’s also lawful.

The best case for the government is a vindictive prosecution motion, because Donald Trump has been out in public and has been on social media saying that Mark Elias should be locked up, that he should be prosecuted. That’s the same sort of argument that led [James] Comey’s defense team to make an argument for vindictive prosecution.

Jessica:

Since you brought up the Alaska case, and this is about that: Where do you think that heads? If Mary Portola ultimately wins, will they be able to drag this out in the courts for a while?

Joyce:

The problem for the Justice Department in that situation is that Alaska’s highest court has already ruled that the ballot was proper, that the inclusion on the ballot was proper. They didn’t find any sort of criminal misconduct. That’s a big hurdle for the DOJ to overcome. But even if they decide they want to go ahead and bring criminal charges, that doesn’t really have anything to do with the election win. Those would be two separate and distinct entities. So I would expect to see that election certified in fairly short order.

Then, you know, DOJ can, I guess, continue with Donald Trump’s revenge tour, indicting people who have not committed crimes in a terrible abuse of the power of the prosecution, for as long as the attorney general is willing to be a part of that right.

Jessica:

Which is probably as long as he’s in that office. We have also heard reports today of aggressive poll monitors — poll monitors asking voters questions, which they’re not supposed to do. What has been happening that you’re aware of? Are you seeing a broader pattern? What could those poll monitors be up to that could lead to some fallout in the coming weeks?

Joyce:

Frankly, there was a lot of concern about these poll monitors before the election. It’s very routine for the DOJ to send poll monitors out. But they typically go to jurisdictions, to polling places where voters have had problems exercising their right to vote in the past, to make sure that they watch very carefully to see what happens. The DOJ’s rule is never to interfere in elections that are occurring. The DOJ plays a very different role. States administer elections. The Justice Department doesn’t. There were concerns this year because of statements made by Harmeet Dhillon, the head of the Civil Rights Division, that this would be different — that they would be targeting polling places with, for instance, language minorities, in an effort to determine whether or not some of those voters were non-citizens.

Overnight, Monday night, the NAACP, the Legal Defense Fund, and others were in court. They persuaded a judge in the District of Columbia not to give them an injunction to stop some of these practices, but to issue stern warnings about the behavior that was expected from these poll monitors. For instance, they were not permitted to talk to voters at the polling places.

So we heard throughout the day instances where that sort of behavior was occurring. You know, it’s more concerning, I think, after the election than during. It doesn’t look like there was any intimidation factor. Americans are brave. We showed up and voted no matter what today. The issue, I think, will be in some of the very close races where there may be challenges in court, whether or not the DOJ has used these monitors to collect evidence. But given that they were operating under a very restrictive sort of set of rules here, it’s not at all clear that they made any progress using these folks.

Jessica:

It just doesn’t seem like the crisis people were expecting materialized.

Joyce:

Well, the big issue was whether or not they would send law enforcement officers in with arrest power who would, in some sense, have an obligation to make arrests. The concern was that they would abuse that. But it was very clear that they were not permitted to send in law enforcement officers. My understanding is that, by and large, the department did not do that.

Jessica:

I’ve seen some reporting tonight, I think it was from CBS News at the White House, that Trump has said internally that he is not planning to aggressively contest the results. I don’t put much stock in that. He could change his mind in two minutes, tomorrow, whatever. I’m just sharing that with the audience to keep your calm.

I also want to ask you, Joyce, after January 6th — if Trump does decide to do something about unfavorable election results and to try to challenge the certification: Is there any law or legal precedent that’s been stood up since 2020 that would prevent them from refusing to certify a new Congress?

Joyce:

You know, this certification question comes up persistently. I think it’s a nonstarter for a lot of reasons. A big one is the fact that we are also focused on the shenanigans in ways that maybe as a country, we were not as prepared for when January 6th, 2021, happened. I think it’s a different scenario, but also it’s going to be a very different Congress. By the time it comes to certifying and seating, we will very likely have Speaker Hakeem Jeffries or a Democrat in that role. That changes the dynamic entirely.

Jessica:

Well, that’s for ‘28. But what if they try not to seat this Congress?

Joyce:

I think the same thing is true. If they try to prevent certification. There are a lot of measures that are in between this and not certifying. Because the lawyers have prepared for that at every step, I think there’s very little play in the joints for Republicans to block seating.

Jessica:

That’s great to hear. I just don’t want to cover another crazy episode.

Joyce:

I mean, look. I think what we have to accept is that they will try the crazy — that they will not be successful with the crazy. One of the big unsung heroes in this whole, horrible episode for democracy has been federal district judges — who, regardless of the political party of the president who appointed them to their judgeship, have simply looked at the facts and the law and made decisions on that basis. I think that they’re the folks that get us through this, along with people like the poll workers and state election officials.

Jessica:

It’s really true. The judiciary has largely held. I want to ask you to take off your legal analyst hat and be for a moment a political analyst. You were in Alabama, where voters can cast a straight party ballot. Would you explain what that means in practice and what you think it could tell us about the results we’ll see tonight.

Joyce:

You know, this is the damnedest thing, and it’s the curse of the Democratic Party in Alabama, although they sort of brought it upon themselves. Alabama used to be an all-Democratic state, and then around 1990, it shifted. A lot of Republican money was injected. Karl Rove came down for some visits. And we’re now an all-Republican state.

When you go to vote in Alabama, at the very top of your ballot there’s one little blip and you can fill it in with your black ink pen. We used to call it pulling the lever. You used to have to pull a lever. Now you just circle that one little blip and you have voted a straight ticket ballot. You have voted for every Republican or every Democrat on the ballot, depending on what you designate. An enormous number of people in Alabama do that, as opposed to looking at each race individually.

As somebody who has never voted straight ticket, I just don’t really get it. That’s very attractive, apparently, to some people — it saves time. That’s the high hurdle that Doug Jones will have to overcome if he’s going to have a shot at the governor’s race. So it’ll be very interesting. He talked about it a lot. He campaigned on that issue. There’s also a very interesting House race in Alabama. One of the districts that was heavily gerrymandered, supposedly to give Republicans the advantage. It looks like maybe it was a dummymander instead, because the Democrat figure is looking very strong going into tonight. So we’ll see.

Jessica:

Wow. Well, there’s nothing you can’t do. Political analysis. Legal analysis. Reassuring.

Joyce:

I knit. I cook, I mean, you name it, I’m here for it.

Jessica:

I am impressed. Joyce, your voice is such a valuable one, especially on a night like tonight. I am such a fan of yours. Thank you for helping us to separate the real legal risks from the noise.

Joyce:

Oh, it’s my pleasure. Thank you so much for having me.

If Democrats Win Big, What Should They Do With It?

Q&A with Democratic strategist Lis Smith on the party’s divisions and what it can learn from its most competitive candidates.

Jessica Yellin

Nov 3

READ IN APP

For daily news, updates, and analysis, be sure to follow us on Instagram. If you are able to support independent journalism, please make a tax-deductible donation to News Not Noise here.

As the first results came in, Democrats were picking up seats and seeing signs of a strong night. But a wave can give a party power without settling what it should do with it. If Democrats take Congress, what should they do first? And how do they deliver on the cost of living while holding the Trump administration accountable?

I spoke with Democratic strategist Lis Smith, who helped launch Pete Buttigieg’s 2020 presidential campaign, about what she’s seeing in the early returns and what she wants her party to do next. We talked about tariffs, a congressional stock trading ban, and whether Democrats can investigate the administration while keeping voters’ everyday concerns front and center.

We also got into the party’s own divisions and what its most competitive candidates might teach Democrats about 2028. Lis’s argument: the party has spent years giving voters reasons to oppose Trump. It needs to give them a reason to vote for Democrats.

News Not Noise is a reader-supported publication. To receive exclusive content and support our work, please consider becoming a paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

Jessica:

Hi, I want to start off with some big wins I can share with you now in Georgia. John Ossoff, the Democrat, has won his race there. He was the one Democratic incumbent senator who faced a real challenge in this race, and he handily won it. That is quite a bit of a surprise.

We’re looking in Florida, where Representative Kathy Castor, a Democrat, has won in the 14th district. Now, I’m spotlighting this because this seat was gerrymandered by Republicans to make it easy for Republicans to win and the Republican lost. They’re calling it a dummymander because the gerrymander is some rigging that didn’t work. There are a few more of those we’ll be talking about in a moment.

Senator Chris Pappas will win in New Hampshire. That’s a Democratic hold. He will be the first openly gay man in the U.S. Senate. CNN is currently reporting that right now Democrats are currently leading in 50 districts that Republicans held. That does not mean they’ve already won them, but they’re leading in 50 districts, according to CNN count. Republicans are leading in only two districts Democrats held. Now, keep in mind that’s a 48 seat gap. And Democrats only needed to pick up three seats to take control of the House. So guess what’s happening after tonight.

Joining us now to talk about all of this and what comes next is Democratic strategist and political commentator Lis Smith, who has worked on some of the biggest Democratic campaigns in the country, including Pete Buttigieg’s 2020 presidential run.

First of all, Lis, how are you feeling right now?

Lis:

After 2024, it’s hard not to feel pretty nervous and pretty burned. But I’m feeling pretty good because of some of the results that we’re about to talk about.

Jessica:

So let’s talk about some of them right now. It looks like Democrats are poised to have a very good night, even a blue wave. We’ll talk about that.

What are some of the races that you’re watching?

Lis:

The first Democratic pickup of the night was Florida’s 22nd district. You were talking about the dummymander in Florida. I first heard it this cycle actually, and I’ve worked in politics for 22-ish years.

Florida’s 22nd district is one of those districts that was created and didn’t have an incumbent. It was created because they thought it would give them a new seat and that they would win the seat. It’s about 27% Latino. Pia Dandiya just won it. She’s a 36-, 37-year-old Indian American woman. She was a teacher, a principal who then went into the private sector and is a first time candidate, which is going to be a trend tonight. You’re going to hear about a lot of first time Democratic candidates. It’s a district that is a Trump plus-ten district. So that is a big, big district.

I think it’s going to be interesting as we get to Texas, where, again, they tried to gerrymander a lot of districts, in part because of Republican strength with Latino voters in 2024. If this tells us anything, it’s that maybe Republicans were taking the Hispanic Latino vote for granted.

Jessica:

Yes. Perceived strength, not real strength.

Lis:

Correct.

Jessica:

If Democrats do have as big a night as it seems it’s shaping up to be. You know, winning elections is one thing, governing is another. So if we look at a scenario in which the Democrats do take the House and the Senate, what do you think they need to do immediately after to capitalize on the momentum?

Lis:

I get it. A lot of people are going to say, throw out the subpoenas, do the investigations, etc. Of course, people in the administration need to be held accountable. I agree with that. But most importantly, from my perspective, as someone who has advised The Bench, which worked with a couple dozen candidates this cycle, is that we’ve got to deliver on some of the things that we promised, or at least try to deliver on some of the things that we campaigned on.

Democrats should really, really push on: Trying to end the war in Iran. Voting to roll back these tariffs. Making data centers pay for the energy that they’re using. Ethics reforms. Corruption was a huge, huge theme in so many of these races. And one thing that they can do is try to pass a stock ban, that bans members of the House, Senate, executive branch, their family members, their staff from trading in stocks.

You might say, “Oh, well, okay, Donald Trump’s going to veto all these things.” With the stock ban, what they could even do is just in the House, pass it so that House members themselves can’t trade in stocks. That would be a big thing. But even if the president vetoes this, it still sends a message: We are fighting for this. And it gets every Republican in the country on the record. It gets the president on the record on whether you are actually serious about that promise you made in 2024 about lowering costs. Are you actually serious about no more dumb wars?

So I don’t think that we should just throw up our hands and say, well, if he is going to veto everything, we shouldn’t do anything. Let’s try to show the American people something that they think Democrats don’t do anymore, which is that we can fight and we will fight for them, and we’ll fight for them on the priorities that they care most about.

Jessica:

I hear you, and I also look at the landscape out there, and I know there are a lot of people who are very eager for these investigations into Trump as well. I’m curious, because I’ve talked to a bunch of people who are also saying Democrats have to move straight into addressing affordability and cost of living. They cannot get distracted with investigations. And there are people who feel the opposite. Are you worried that if they take on the investigations, that Trump will turn that into a fight that becomes a political spectacle and distracts from what voters want the most?

Lis:

No, because I think Democrats can walk and chew gum at the same time. It’s possible to do these two things at the same time. But make sure that we’re going out and communicating to people that we heard them loud and clear. Because the reality is this. I live here in New York City. I live in the bluest of the dem base. My mom is one of those people who wants to see all those investigations and people on the Oversight Committee, all those people, they will do that. But let’s make sure that we are connecting with not just the base, but with the swing voters who said, “Right, I want people who care about my ability to be able to pay my energy bills, my health care bills, my mortgage, all of these things.”

People really care about corruption. By the way, corruption isn’t a one party thing in politics. Yes, what we’re seeing from Donald Trump and the Republicans is a historical grift. He and his family have made billions of dollars in the White House, and we see his former personal lawyer, Alina Habba charging $50 million to get a pardon. That’s disgusting. We’ve never seen anything like that. I mean, $50 million.

But we also need to make sure that we’re doing something that Donald Trump didn’t do. And that’s why he’s being punished right now, because he went into the White House and said, I’m going to lower your costs. I’m going to stop dumb wars. So let’s make sure we’re focused on those things.

And at the same time, we can hold all these people who did these unconscionable things, this historic grift, accountable — but put the people and the voters first.

Jessica:

Let’s shift focus from what’s going on with the Democrats versus Republicans, and talk for a minute about Democrats who may be united against Trump but deeply divided about the party’s own direction.

We’ve seen some very bitter falling out in the last few days. It got quite ugly. Do you think a big win helped settle that debate, or do you worry that the infighting is about to get worse?

Lis:

I don’t think the debate is settled. I don’t think the infighting is going to get worse either.

This is the reality. For years we have been talking about building a big tent again. Jessica, how long have we known each other? How long have we been doing this?

Jessica:

Too old to say.

Lis:

You know what? I was at MS NOW earlier in a green room now with Claire McCaskill. We talked about how 20 years ago today, I was on her campaign when she won her race for U.S. Senate. But back then, right, the Democratic Party was more of a big tent party. We won U.S. Senate races in Missouri, in North Dakota, in South Dakota, in Kentucky, all across the country.

We’ve gotten away from that. I think we’ve got to realize that we need a big tent. But if you want to get a big tent of voters, you need a big tent of candidates. And that sometimes is going to cause a little bit of tension. But a little tension is okay. I will take a little bit of tension any day of the week over what we’re seeing on the Republican side of the aisle, which is this complete and total fealty to Donald Trump, to the point that they’re willing to walk off the cliff with him. Support him on the war in Iran. Support him on these tariffs in states like Texas, Iowa, Kansas — three of the biggest agricultural states in the country, where both the war and these tariffs are absolutely killing the economy.

Jessica:

Point taken on Donald Trump. I would disagree with you that it’s a little disagreement internally, but we will litigate that another day. You’re doing your work as a Democrat there, I appreciate it.

I want to ask you one last question, which is, We all know that the ‘28 race basically starts after the last vote is called. Not long ago, you helped introduce Pete Buttigieg to a national audience when he was first looking to run nationally. Looking ahead to ‘28, do you think Democrats should be searching for the best fighter against Trump and Trumpism, the best messenger on affordability, or someone who can change the conversation entirely?

Lis:

The third thing. There is this view that Donald Trump has been so harmful to the Republican Party. My hot take is that he may have been even worse for the Democratic Party, because ever since he went down that golden escalator in in Manhattan, it feels like everything that we’ve done in the Democratic Party has oriented around him and galvanizing people against Donald Trump, so much so that we almost forgot what we stand for as Democrats. We forgot to give people, sometimes, a reason to vote for us.

Politics isn’t just about being against stuff. It’s about inspiring people, making them believe in you, making them see themselves in the future that you’re leading. I would really like to see that. I do think that that’s what a lot of people are looking for.

You brought up the internal division in the Democratic Party, which is real. I do a lot of things — I’m not here to spin, and to hide any of the things that are going on in the Democratic Party. I just think that the big story of tonight isn’t necessarily the conflict. It’s the fact that we’re competitive in Iowa, the fact that we’re competitive in Kansas, the fact that we’re competitive in Texas. This is going to come back to your question. Why are we competitive in those places? It’s not because these candidates were going out and saying Donald Trump is a fascist. It’s not because they were going out and saying Trump voters are the enemy. It’s not because they were going out and just doing constant warfare. It’s because they were going out and saying things like: James Talarico, when he was asked about Ken Paxton, saying, I love Ken Paxton because he is a child of God. It’s like Adam Hamilton, Scott Colom in Mississippi, going and shaking hands with Trump voters outside of a Trump rally.

There is so much decency in our Democratic candidates right now and in the American people that we frankly need to channel again. The races that we’re going to win back, the majority makers in the House, potentially in the Senate, are going to come from that approach, not just the constant warfare, anti-Trump approach. Yes, your point, a lot of it’s going to be talking about affordability. But we’ve just got to put the American people first, and center them. Don’t center the personalities. Don’t center Donald Trump. Don’t center whoever the Democratic candidates are. Make sure that the American people are at the center of this conversation. We are at our best when we channel people’s better angels.

Jessica:

I was just going to say that. I’m so hopeful you’re right. This is the first time I’ve heard somebody say this, and it does make sense, because it would be the inverse of what we’ve been enduring for the last basically six, eight years. So maybe the pendulum will swing back that way.

Lis:

Well, it should, because we’ve got to look at what actually worked this election cycle. Again, you and I have been doing this for a long time. It’s really frustrating as a Democrat to think, we lost to Donald Trump in 2016. We won in 2020, which was a year of a global pandemic. Then we lost again in 2024. So like, what happened in all of that time?

A lot of it was that we focus so much on Donald Trump — not articulating what we stood for, not trying to make people feel better about themselves. This constant warfare that we have in politics, this constant need to turn anyone who thinks differently from us, votes differently from us, into the enemy is really exhausting to a lot of the American people. I really do think that someone who can channel something different, something fresh, that makes people hopeful about the future again is going to be a winning ticket in 2028.

Jessica:

And may it be. Thank you so much. It’s always good to get your perspective and it’s really nice to see you tonight. Thank you.

Lis:

Yes, thank you so much Jessica.

Could This Finally Be Democrats’ Year In Texas?

Q&A with Texas politics reporter Gromer Jeffers Jr. on the Democrat’s path to the Senate, what voters think of Gov. Greg Abbott, and whether the GOP’s redistricting backfired.

Jessica Yellin

Nov 3

READ IN APP

For daily news, updates, and analysis, be sure to follow us on Instagram. If you are able to support independent journalism, please make a tax-deductible donation to News Not Noise here.

Every election cycle, Democrats convince themselves this could be the year Texas turns blue. And every cycle, they get their hearts broken. Could this time be different?

With competitive races for Senate and governor, the question is whether Democratic enthusiasm can translate into enough votes — and whether Republicans disappointed with their own candidates will actually cross over.

Gromer Jeffers Jr., who has covered Texas politics for decades for The Dallas Morning News, joined me to explain what to watch as results arrive. We discuss James Talarico’s path to defeating Ken Paxton, whether voters are ticket splitting with Gov. Greg Abbott, and what turnout among Latino and younger voters could tell us.

We also look at whether Republicans’ redistricting gamble could backfire — and what Democrats would need to do to turn a breakthrough tonight into lasting support. As Gromer explains, winning an election would give them something they’ve long lacked in Texas: a statewide platform to show voters what they can deliver.

Watch our conversation at the top of this newsletter.

News Not Noise is a reader-supported publication. To receive exclusive content and support our work, please consider becoming a paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

Jessica:

Some of tonight’s most closely watched races are in Texas, where Democrats hope to flip a U.S. Senate seat and unseat Republican Governor Greg Abbott. I always say every election cycle that Democrats get their hearts broken in Texas. They always think they’re going to win this one, and it just doesn’t happen. But could this year be different? Joining me to discuss this is Jeffers, political writer for The Dallas Morning News, who has been covering Texas politics for decades.

Thank you for being with me on a very, very, very, very busy day for you. I’d love to ask you just at this moment — what are you watching and waiting for?

Gromer:

Well, it’s that nervous time, you know, before the results come in. We’re just waiting for a couple of hours to go by once the polls close. The secretary of state releases the early voting data and all the counties as well. From that data, you can see kind of where the race is headed.

It’s the best poll you can have because these are real voters. So you’ll see, for instance, who’s leading. And since there were 7.7 million people who voted early, a midterm election record, it would be a good indicator of whether Democrats have a chance to make history, or if it’s the same old, same old.

Now, if it’s really close, that brings into question all kinds of issues, such as what does the Election Day vote mean, right? Republicans usually vote on Election Day more than Democrats do. So all of that comes into play. But we’re just waiting for that first early vote drop that happens right after the polls close, which is 7pm central 5pm Pacific and 8pm eastern.

Jessica:

I’ve spoken with a few smart analysts who are looking at just the overall turnout in Texas and that dynamics, and they think James Talarico will win for the Senate tonight for them to be right. Can you give us a picture of who needs to turn out for him, and what has to happen to the Trump coalition in Texas?

Gromer:

Well, what he needs is a big turnout from the urban areas — Dallas, Houston, Austin, San Antonio. And then he needs to carry some of the close-in suburbs around those cities, like Collin County, which is just north of Dallas. A Republican has won that county, like, forever. It’s been like, since the 1970s since the Democrat won there.

So Talarico would have to win there and win in Tarrant County, which is the largest county in the United States under Republican control. It’s really funny. It used to be Orange County in California. Not anymore. Now it’s Texas. So he has to do well in those close-in suburbs.

This race against Paxton involves — how many disaffected, disgruntled Republicans can Talarico get to either stay home or vote for him. That’s key. Because there are more Republicans in the electorate than Democrats. And if they all stay home, if they all say: You know what? We may not like Paxton, but we can’t vote for a Democrat. Then Talarico can’t win. But the strategy is to get some crossover voters, get most of the independents, and then add a strong Democratic turnout to win.

So he needs those persuadable Republicans. And then we’re seeing early voting data based on what we know by matching voters, that younger voters are voting. Latino vote is high. And that can be unusual in Texas because Democrats have relied on Hispanic voters for decades to help them flip Texas, but it hasn’t happened.

As a matter of fact, Trump has done better over the last few cycles with Hispanic voters, with Latinos. And that’s been a problem for Democrats. Well, in this election, they hope that Talarico — and there’s evidence that Talarico has got a lot of Latino voters on his side, including some who voted for Trump in 2024. And there are all kinds of issues around that. You know, immigration enforcement, that it went too far, that they thought it would be about rounding up criminals, not going to Lowe’s or Home Depot and having raids there.

All other kinds of factors are involved. Trump’s unpopularity, but also getting back to that Democratic coalition that helped Obama, for instance, when he won in 2008, which was younger voters.

Jessica:

You brought up a lot there. I heard what you said that for Talarico to win he needs the whole coalition. And then he needs some Republicans to stay home and some willing to cross over. We’ve seen this very unusual thing where a bunch of Republicans publicly said that they will be voting for Talarico in Texas. I can’t remember seeing something like that before.

Are you seeing evidence that Republicans, at least some, are willing to cross over? And if so, is it about Paxton’s liabilities or how much of it is about Trump?

Gromer:

It’s both. The biggest part is Paxton’s liabilities. That’s part of it. But there are some Trump Republicans that accept that maybe he hasn’t kept some promises and done a bad job, that the gas prices are high, but they’re still going to support Trump.

The key for Talarico is to get those voters who would never vote Democrat. Maybe he won’t get all of them, but at least get some of them to recognize that Paxton is a problem, and vote for him or stay home. There are some Republicans who speak at rallies and they say, hey, I understand that actually voting for our Democrat may be a bridge too far for you. All right, well, skip that race, then. Just stay home. We have evidence that there are Republicans who will do that. I’ve talked to Republicans at Talarico rallies. There’s such a thing as a Talarico / Greg Abbott voter. They hate Ken Paxton.

Jessica:

That brings me to the governor’s race. There’s a Democrat. Gina Hinojosa is doing surprisingly well for a Democrat in Texas against Greg Abbott. I know that Democrats are really excited about the early vote numbers. They think it seems really promising for her. But do you think you’re seeing signs that she’s getting the support she needs or what has to happen today for her to actually win?

Gromer:

It has to be a blue wave — a real one. You know, there’s talk about a blue wave, but you know how category five hurricanes, sometimes when they hit land, they peter out and become a tropical storm. Well, that category five hurricane, when it hits Texas, it has to keep up momentum and still be a full force hurricane in order to get Gina Hinojosa over the finish line and into the victory column.

If it’s not, then Abbott probably will find a way to get close, because unlike Paxton, there aren’t a lot of — there are some Republicans who may feel like, look, you guys have embraced Trump. You’ve been in power for so long, it’s time for a change. But there are less of those voters out there than, say, anti-Paxton voters.

Jessica:

So they really hate him.

Gromer:

They’re a large segment of people. He’s a candidate with all these liabilities. What’s interesting is he’s run statewide before. But those were low-profile attorney general races. This is the first race where he’s had a national profile. And, I mean, that guy cannot campaign. I’ve never seen anything quite like that. The contrast is striking.

Having said that, there’s one thing for a Republican to tell me at a rally or to announce that they’re not going to do it — but you know how it all happens. I mean, you get into the voters booth, you see R and D, and I don’t know, I can’t do it. I can’t do it.

Jessica:

I’m curious. Have there been reports of ICE at the polls or any major problems with voter intimidation around the state so far?

Gromer:

Not in a major way so far. You know, there’s still a couple of hours, but so far it’s been relatively smooth. But we’ll see. I mean, a lot of times there’s these things percolate after folks get off work and start voting and the lines get stronger, you know?

Jessica:

When we were talking before we started live, you were saying you never know when some one voting place has an irregularity, and then they have to keep it open — and then it’s hours and hours we’re waiting for returns. So we have to keep in mind it ain’t over till it’s over.

I’m curious. The early exit polls painted a picture of a very angry national electorate. They show that about three quarters of voters are dissatisfied or angry with the direction of the country. Economic pessimism is up, and Trump’s own approval is the lowest of any president recorded in exit polls. How much of that broad anger are you seeing in Texas? And are you getting a sense that it’s depressing Republicans who are considering their vote as well?

Gromer:

That’s why these races are so close. We mentioned Paxton. But that doesn’t account for the governor’s race being close or the lieutenant governor’s race or the attorney general’s race, or all the other races, the legislative races, the state house races — they’re close, too. Democrats could gain seats in the Texas legislature later and make it close there in the House and in the Senate. I don’t think that they’ll flip them, but they will close the gap, if tonight goes the way they think it could go. That suggests that there’s this dissatisfaction.

Let me say one thing. Typically in Texas, Texas has its own brand. If the brand is separate from the national brand, that has allowed Republicans, when times get tough with the president — like in 2018, Beto O’Rourke had that close race with Ted Cruz for Senate, Trump’s last midterm election year. Usually in Texas, they have their own brand. The Republicans can say, okay, that’s the national stuff that’s going on. Everything is fine in Texas. But now with the affordability issues in Texas — and it’s not just high gas prices, it’s the high cost of homeowners insurance, the high cost of auto insurance. All of that is hitting Texas just like everywhere else.

This isn’t like you can’t say there are indicators to say that the economy is doing well. The stock market, the unemployment rate, and all of that. But every week, if you go to the gas station and you’re driving a big Texas SUV guzzler, whatever it costs to fill up for you and your wife’s big guzzler — you’re going to feel that. Not to mention the ranchers and the cattle industry, oil and gas industry, all of that, the tariffs.

Texas Republicans wrapped themselves around Trump. Now they can’t extricate themselves.

Abbott skipped a couple of the Trump rallies. He went to the midterm convention in September. But when Trump came back, he didn’t go to the rallies.

Jessica:

And they’ve been bonded.

Gromer:

Remember redistricting.

Jessica:

Oh, and some of those districts that they gerrymandered might have been dummymanders. Could they lose any of them?

Gromer:

I think that the Republicans will get two. But the districts in South Texas where they were hoping that because Trump did so well with Latino voters, that they would just vote for Republicans, they might lose all three of those.

And with California doing redistricting, it’ll be a net loss, potentially, of three, just with those two states. And that energized Democrats.

Jessica:

So let’s just say the Democrats win the Senate; they may or may not win the governors, but it at least comes close; they pick up some seats in the legislature. We could see that as Texas beginning to undergo a lasting political shift. Or it could simply be voters rejecting Trump and these particular Republican candidates. What do think Democrats would have to do to build on a victory tonight to make it a lasting political shift in Texas?

Gromer:

Well, let’s say that Talarico wins. If he does win, he’s created a path: Do well in the urban areas, do well in the suburbs, win back Latino voters, find a way to get young, new, and infrequent voters to the polls. That has always been the path for Democrats, but they’ve been unable to execute it.

The other good thing about Talarico, if he wins, is that he’s in there for six years. The problems, the problem Democrats have had: they don’t have any statewide elected officials. Here’s the problem with that. A statewide elected official can build a statewide organization. Imagine a senator with offices all across Texas, jobs to give out, money to bring back. You can go to rural Texas and say, I got you this VA hospital, or I did this, I did that. You’re interacting with voters from a position of power. The voters begin to realize, you know what? It’s not so bad electing the Democrat. We have a senator here. So you build off that.

Let’s say Talarico loses. It will be demoralizing. Very demoralizing. The narrative will be: Wow. If Texas Democrats can’t win in this environment, they can never win. But you have to shake that off, because these races, I suspect, will still be close. And so you just follow that path and take advantage of that and keep building.

But that would be hard to do if there’s a disappointment tonight. But you got to look at it as incremental steps — because, remember, they were demoralized in 2024. They got back up, dusted themselves off. And now they’re on the verge of winning. So you just got to keep building

Jessica:

Well I know that you have a very long night ahead of you. So I am so grateful to you for taking the time and helping us make sense of Texas politics. Thanks for your time.

Gromer:

This has been fantastic. Don’t be a stranger.

Jessica:

Let’s talk about it again. Let’s see what happens after tonight. We can discuss the implications.

Gromer:

I would love to. Happy election night.

Jessica:

Everybody, you can catch him every Sunday on NBC Five’s Lone Star Politics there and in The Dallas Morning News.

Want more information?

The Work Doesn’t End on Election Night

Watch now (17 mins) | Sharon McMahon joins me to talk about what the government owes us, why progress requires working with people we disagree with, and how to stay engaged without burning out.

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

The Work Doesn’t End on Election Night

Sharon McMahon joins me to talk about what the government owes us, why progress requires working with people we disagree with, and how to stay engaged without burning out.

Jessica Yellin and Sharon McMahon

Nov 3

READ IN APP

For daily news, updates, and analysis, be sure to follow us on Instagram. If you are able to support independent journalism, please make a tax-deductible donation to News Not Noise here.

Election Day can feel like the finish line. We vote, we wait, and we hope the results will change things. But even when the candidates we support win, the problems we want them to solve are still there. What should we expect from the people we elect, and what’s our role after casting a ballot?

Sharon McMahon of Sharon Says So and The Preamble on Substack joined me to talk about what comes next. We discuss what a Democratic victory would mean, what government can realistically deliver on affordability and health care, and why meaningful progress has always required working with people we disagree with.

We also talk about preparing for postelection backlash and legal challenges, and how to stay involved without burning out. Sharon brings some much-needed perspective, humor, and a reminder that ordinary people make history — and need to rest, too.

Watch our conversation at the top of this newsletter. Below is a transcript, lightly edited for clarity.

News Not Noise is a reader-supported publication. To receive exclusive content and support our work, please consider becoming a paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

Jessica:

Sharon, first of all. Hello. How are you doing on this very high stress day?

Sharon:

Merry Election Day to you. Jessica. Solidarity. It’s been a long slog. I’m doing well. How are you?

Jessica:

I’m feeling good. I actually expected there to be much more chaos around the election itself than we’re seeing — sort of ICE out or troops in the streets — and it’s really very normal. Even the messes at the election polling centers are contained to some specific areas.

Sharon:

They have been at least attempting, from my vantage point, to message: Don’t worry. We have a plan in place. Your vote still counts. Fill out the paper ballot. I guess there’s one place that has a shortage of paper ballots, but it does seem like they have been trained for redundancy. What to do if there are problems.

Jessica:

Totally. When you hear about these problems, everybody gets upset. Then one place — it’s like, there is a paper jam, and another was like a wheelchair ran over a thing. You know, stuff happens, always.

Sharon:

Yeah. But what I think is great is that we have now been trained as voters to be aware of problems. If there’s a thing, here’s what to do. The fact that voters are even like — you guys, the paper is jamming. The fact that they want to cast their ballot so much. I actually find that heartening that we’re sounding the alarm about a paper jam.

Jessica:

I totally agree, and I think you’ll agree with this — that in a way, the silver lining of this bizarre error we’re living through with all the crises is that it has made everybody vigilant and aware of what you do. It’s kind of empowered people. They’re no longer saying, who’s going to fix this? They’re like, what can I do to fix this, right?

Sharon:

I am going to make sure that my vote is counted, and if anything goes awry, I am going to let somebody know. I actually think that’s a great attitude to have. Nothing will stop me from voting.

Jessica:

Here we go.

Sharon:

Yeah.

Jessica:

You answer people’s questions about government all day, almost every day. I hope you get a few days off. I don’t see it happening. This election day, what are people asking you that tells you something about the larger mood in the country?

Sharon:

I’m still getting a ton of questions about specifics — about, like, “Okay, I forgot to change my address. What do I do?” And to me, that also speaks to exactly what I was just saying, that people very much want their voices to be heard. That they feel as though potentially things are not going the way they want them to go.

Consequently, they feel like, “Nothing will stop me from participating.” So even just the questions about things like, “I moved. Should I go back to my old precinct?” To me, that is indicative of this larger mindset of I will vote come hell or high water. Give me some specific tools that I can use to make sure that my ballot is cast.

I’m hearing a lot of that, and I am still hearing, as I would imagine you are hearing this as well, a lot of concern about what tomorrow is going to look like, what the flurry of potential lawsuits is going to look like, what kind of shenanigans we’re going to be up against later on in this week.

People very much want their voices to be heard. They feel as though things are not going the way they want them to go — consequently, they feel like, “Nothing will stop me from participating.”

Share

Jessica:

I heard something that’s very cool. And I think you and your folks will be glad to hear this too, is that you think about all those lawyers that the Trump administration purged from the DOJ. A lot of them have gone into private practice, but they have come back and been working to protect the election ever since. They are on call today and going forward to deal with all those lawsuits, in addition to legal outlets all over the country. I mean, the preparation has been intense, extensive.

Sharon:

I have heard from lawyers at a number of organizations that have gamed out dozens of potential scenarios and have paperwork ready to go for every single state in all of these dozens of scenarios so they can just be like — Ohio, scenario seven, here’s your paperwork, dude. Like they already have — It’s done.

Jessica:

That’s great. This has been a lot of work. If today goes smoothly, it’s because a lot of people spend a lot of time preparing.

There’s a lot of expectation that Democrats are going to have somewhere between a strong to a very strong to a tsunami night. If they do have any version of those, how do you think they should distinguish a mandate for their ideas from voters simply wanting to check on Trump?

Sharon:

One of the things the data is showing us is that people are overwhelmingly voting on the economy. It’s somewhere in the neighborhood of 75 to 80% of voters are most concerned about the economy.

Of course, voters always care about the economy. But this economy in particular is very problematic for people. We still have very high inflation, gas prices, these sort of kitchen table issues that actually really do matter to average Americans. I do think Democrats are going to take that as a sign of, we need to do something about the economy. Now, that is the exact same thing as reining in Donald Trump.

This is not like the Iranian hostage crisis that Jimmy Carter dealt with, where he had external forces outside of his control, couldn’t get them back, tried to negotiate. We’re not dealing with a 9/11 situation. This situation is largely of our own creation. And so when you’re talking about a democratic mandate, the mandate is to fix Trump’s problems. That’s how they are largely going to view it.

Jessica:

You know, you recently wrote that public goods don’t have to be profitable to be valuable. And I’m wondering on that note: If Democrats do gain power, how do they think about what they should deliver? Because there’s a lot they could do that is sort of investing in the economy for the future. And those goods arrive maybe ten years down the road, and then people need relief right now.

So what do you think voters should realistically expect them to be able to change over the next few months when they — if they take office?

Sharon:

Well, of course,you don’t want to make pie in the sky promises, where you’re like — everybody’s going to get free child care, free health care, free college, things that a lot of a lot of people actually would love to have. You don’t want to make these kinds of pie in the sky promises. And yet those kinds of issues are exactly the kinds of things that hit people in their pocket book.

While it is more difficult to dial back gas prices when you’re in a market economy, there are ways that you can create programs that help offset the cost of the increased cost of living. So I do think Democrats are going to potentially really work on what are becoming cost of living issues for people, particularly when it comes to health care — Obamacare.

The Republicans have always hated Obamacare. They still hate Obamacare. There are huge issues with a lot of people’s policies expiring under the Affordable Care Act, a lot of health insurance or health organizations no longer accepting exchange based programs. People who are on social media talking about their child who is sick with cancer, unable to get cancer treatment at their local children’s hospital because they are on an exchange based insurance program, and their local children’s hospital will no longer take any of them. So I do think health care is going to be top of mind for a lot of people as something that could be potentially addressed.

Some of the other issues, of course, are harder to address unless you deal with some structural things. Like bringing the cost of college down might actually be more challenging than helping people afford health care.

Jessica:

That’s a really good, clear distinction. You know, I want to switch gears to something that’s happened more recently, which is the fights within the Democratic Party. We have seen a lot of these divisions break out into some very uncomfortable stuff at the very end. They’ve become remarkably personal.

What I’m wondering is, as an historian, have Democrats lost sight of what a coalition is? Have we had times in history where people understood in America that a collection of people who disagree about important things can still work together? Or are we at a different era where disagreements now are so fundamental that expecting folks to work together sometimes is just unrealistic, even within the same party?

Human nature has not changed. We are not going to suddenly find a pool of tens of millions of people who 100% agree with that. It doesn’t exist. So we have to ask ourselves, do we want to make change, or do we want to just be right and have everything still suck? Those are the choices.

Share

Sharon:

I love that you asked this question because I very much believe that, especially the left part of the political spectrum, Democrats in the United States have developed this sense of ideological purity in the sense of if we don’t agree on the following 25 issues, then we cannot work together at all. And that is simply not how almost any important thing that has happened in the United States is legislatively. That is how almost none of them have gotten done. We would have never achieved the Civil Rights Act, the Voting Rights Act — you know, any of the things that we would look at and be like, that was important. I’m glad we did it. That always came through coalition building, as much as people hate to hear that, because we are definitely in an era where we do not want to work with people we do not like — you believe the following 25 terrible things and I do not want to work with you. You should go away. You’re wrong. You know, the social media discourse is very much like: you’re wrong, you’re wrong, you’re wrong, you’re wrong, you’re wrong. And we cannot have any kind of conversation.

Now, I’m not saying we should tolerate hate. I’m not saying people’s identities should be up for debate. I’m not saying we should be fine with antisemitism or Islamophobia. I’m not saying we need to tolerate those kinds of bad behaviors. But I am saying that if we want to move the needle legislatively for the good of the American people, we are going to have to — on both sides of the aisle — set aside some of these notions of what it actually looks like to work with people.

Where did we get the idea that we all need to be in lockstep, perfect harmony, braiding each other’s hair over a campfire to be able to do anything meaningful? It’s actually going to require us being like, “Yeah, he’s weird, but he agrees with me on the roads and bridges. So we’re doing the deal.” That’s how it has always worked.

Human nature has not changed. We are not going to suddenly find a pool of tens of millions of people who 100% agree with that. It doesn’t exist. So we have to ask ourselves, do we want to make change, or do we want to just be right and have everything still suck? Those are the choices.

Jessica:

I just love how you framed that. I think for folks listening, the real work comes in hearing what Sharon said and not thinking about the person who disagrees with you. Who needs to learn that lesson? But to think about how do I refuse to work with someone else because they’re disagreeing with me on something? And where, in my own internal barometer, do I need to recalibrate so I can do that?

Sharon:

I don’t want anyone to hear Sharon say, “You have to give all your money and your passwords to strangers who want you dead.” I’m not saying that, okay? I’m not saying share all your deepest, darkest secrets with your sworn enemies.

But this is not going to be fixed by some other entity. This is going to take all of us to be willing to commit to doing the actual work, which is sometimes working with the weird guy who also happens to agree with you on roads and bridges.

Jessica:

Right, exactly. I love how you say that. Think of the weird guy. So to that larger point, I think your work reminds us that ordinary people do change history. For somebody who’s voting today and thinks, “Great, I’ve done my part, I’m exhausted.” What is the next useful thing they can do?

We are not done yet. When tomorrow dawns, even if things go really, really swimmingly, we have more work to do. That means taking care of yourself. That means making sure that you are still ready to participate, that you’re still ready to do your part.

Sharon:

I love that question too. It is almost like you should go into journalism Jessica. You should give that some thought.

Starting tomorrow we are going to start seeing the rancor really ramp up again. It’s going to start tomorrow. There are people who will win tonight, are going to have the celebratory moments, and we are going to see a strong backlash against whatever happens. This is historically how United States politics works.

Remember when Barack Obama won and people who supported him and loved him felt like, “Oh my gosh, hope has returned to the earth. This is incredible.” The backlash against Barack Obama is actually still being felt. It is still being felt specifically when we’re talking about Obamacare. You see people on social media all the time saying there’s been no more president more divisive than Barack Obama. People on the right often feel like Obama was, you know, their biggest opp.

I do think we need to be prepared for people who feel very, very angry about the election results. That is going to be a thing that we’re going to have to contend with tomorrow, and we are going to have to work on that as a work on that together. That’s not going to go away because the side maybe we prefer wins or the side that we were hoping would win doesn’t win. We have to deal with the reality that half the country — maybe a little bit less — is going to feel upset tomorrow. And what does that look like moving forward? We have to prepare potentially for a slog when it comes to the lawsuits that we know are coming. We know the lawsuits are coming.

It’s a little bit like being a doctor in the sense that patients are always better off in almost all instances being told the truth by their physician than having the truth hidden from them by their physician. So I’m just telling everybody the truth: We are not done yet. When tomorrow dawns, even if things go really, really swimmingly, we have more work to do. That means taking care of yourself. That means making sure that you are still ready to participate, that you’re still ready to do your part. And that means like, “Okay, I have to rest, I have to eat, I have to disengage from whatever it is that is raising my anxiety.” I have to listen to trusted, trusted sources of news and not just endlessly scroll consuming doom content.

I mean, I could continue, but I just want to let people know: Do you feel like this is a celebratory moment? I love that for you. But also just know that we still have more work to do and you have to take care of yourself.

Share

Jessica:

Such good advice. It’s like, have your moment, give yourself a break and then bear down for more work ahead. I offer folks a news diet which I will share with you, you can also have a news diet to help regulate a little bit how you manage all this incoming over the next few months.

Sharon, I just love talking to you. Thank you for helping put this moment in perspective, and also for the care and the clarity you bring to these conversations.

Sharon:

My pleasure. Thanks for inviting me. And take care of yourself. We need we need you. Oh, and consider a career in journalism.

Jessica:

Thank you. I’m so flattered. I’m going to look into that. That’s very kind.

Sharon:

Thank you. All right. Bye, everyone.

Jessica:

You can find Sharon’s work, as you must know, on Instagram and across socials as Sharon Says So and on Substack at The Preamble. Check her out.
