NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 22:45

Can Trump’s Coalition Hold?

Watch now (24 mins) | Mike Madrid joins me to discuss the races he’s watching, Latino voters, and why a Democratic win wouldn’t necessarily mean Americans have embraced the party.

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

"You are the only place I trust to get my news!" - Elizabeth, paying subscriber.

Watch now

Can Trump’s Coalition Hold?

Mike Madrid joins me to discuss the races he’s watching, Latino voters, and why a Democratic win wouldn’t necessarily mean Americans have embraced the party.

Jessica Yellin and Mike Madrid

Nov 3

READ IN APP

For daily news, updates, and analysis, be sure to follow us on Instagram. If you are able to support independent journalism, please make a tax-deductible donation to News Not Noise here.

Mike Madrid is making a prediction he’s never made before: a Democrat will win Texas.

The Republican pollster, Lincoln Project co-founder, and author of The Latino Century joins me to explain why he thinks Democratic Senate candidate James Talarico can pull it off — and what the race could tell us about the coalition that returned Trump to the White House.

Where is that coalition fracturing? What do polls miss? And which early results could signal where the night is headed?

Mike also challenges a familiar assumption about Latino voters: that immigration is their defining issue. He argues that affordability and economic frustration are driving their choices, with consequences for races in Texas, Ohio and Kansas.

His warning for Democrats: rejecting Republicans isn’t the same as embracing the Democratic agenda. We discuss what it would take to earn voters’ lasting support — and how Democrats could balance economic priorities with oversight and investigations if they win Congress.

Plus, his predictions for key Senate races and the results he’ll watch first.

Watch our conversation at the top of this newsletter, and tell us what you’re watching as the votes come in.

Below is a transcript of our discussion, lightly edited for clarity.

News Not Noise is a reader-supported publication. To receive exclusive content and support our work, please consider becoming a paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

JESSICA:

Hi, everyone. One of the big questions tonight is: Can the coalition that returned Trump to the White House keep Republicans in control of Congress or will they flip Congress tonight? Joining me to discuss this is Mike Madrid, Republican pollster, co-founder of the Lincoln Project and author of The Latino Century.

Mike, hi. What are the vibes out there in your view?

MIKE:

Well, look, I think the fundamentals of this race were set months ago. You have to have campaigns, obviously. Campaigns matter. Messaging matters. Democracy matters. But I haven’t seen an anti-incumbent year — it’s really bad out.

JESSICA:

So tell us first, when the polls close tonight, where’s the first place you’ll look and what will that tell you?

MIKE:

I’m going to be looking for a couple of things. The first place I’m always looking is Virginia because it closes pretty early and they count pretty quickly. What you’re going to be looking for is really the margins in key counties. We don’t need to go through all of the counties. You’ll know right away whether or not Republicans or Democrats are overperforming. If you’re watching cable news, you’re going to hear it all day long, or if you’re on the internet watching it, you’re going to get that assessment very, very quickly.

Watch Passaic County up in New Jersey to see what the turnout numbers look like for the Hispanic votes; it’s the most Hispanic-dense county. New Jersey numbers will come in early. And then keep your eyes on Florida and look for the statewide margins between David Jolly and Brian Donalds. And then, of course, the Nixon campaign, Angie Nixon, sort of the DSA candidate, if she’s within single digits, it’s probably going to be a very good night for Democrats nationwide.

JESSICA:

So basically what you’re saying is you’re looking at these states not only for what the actual results are there, but because you can extrapolate how other states might break in the end.

MIKE:

Yeah, I’m a little nerdy. That’s what I do. I’m more interested in what else is coming afterwards — the margins are what we’re looking for because they remain consistent almost overwhelmingly across the country. I’m not looking for a particular race — I mean, look, if you want to look for a race, look at Maine. If Susan Collins goes down — and she might, but I think of all the battleground states she’s probably in the best position because of the unique political environment of Maine — but if Susan Collins loses, it’s going to be a very big blue wave.

JESSICA:

OK, that’s a good one to watch. I mean, there’s so many. So let me stay broadly on the night, and then we’re going to talk about Latino voters. Pollsters have been struggling to accurately measure Trump’s support. And I think it’s in part because MAGA voters can be especially hard to reach when you’re trying to do a poll. Can you give folks a little bit of insight into that? Is that because they’re refusing to talk to pollsters at all?

And can you help people understand how they then account for these voters who won’t answer the questions?

MIKE:

There’s something called non-response bias, which is the demographic most likely to not respond to pollsters or don’t like the media the most or don’t trust institutions. They tend to be older, white, male conservatives, which is Trump’s base. So when you’re a pollster and you’re calling this demographic, you need a certain number of responses to get a representative sample. If you keep getting hang ups or nobody responding to you, you keep calling and calling until you find the exception to that rule.

That exception is by definition not representative of the sample that you’re getting. And so oftentimes by a few percentage points, there can be an “underrepresented” or I think the real misnomer is a “shy” Trump voter. There’s nothing shy about Trump supporters. They just don’t want to respond. So there have been polls when Trump is on the ballot where Donald Trump will overperform.

But this is really important: Donald Trump is not on the ballot. In 2018, those voters did not show up. Not only did they not show up, but Democratic strength was underrepresented. It was a stronger Democratic year. 2018 was important because that was the first midterm when Trump was president. All this year, what we have been seeing is Democratic underrepresentation in the polls — dramatically overperforming in New Jersey, in Virginia, in Miami, in Texas and California, everywhere. Democrats have been dramatically overperforming.

So this bias can go both ways. My strong sense is when you look at history, this midterm election will look a lot like the 2018 election because of who is showing up. That probably suggests that if there is an undercounting or an underrepresentation, it will probably benefit the Democrats.

JESSICA:

Wow. So Democrats could have a massive night tonight.

MIKE:

They could have a massive night.

JESSICA:

Let’s talk about one of the reasons that may be the case, which is Latino voters. They helped power Trump’s victory in 2024. This is your specialty. We know they could help deliver big wins for Democrats tonight. But would you help folks understand how much of the shift is explicitly about ICE and mass deportations? And if it is that versus affordability — what’s happening? And how does what’s happening compare with what those voters expected when Trump campaigned promising mass deportations?

MIKE:

Well, this is the misconception that we have as a country. It goes back to the way we have a very elementary understanding of race and ethnicity in this country. By a three to one margin, what is motivating Latino voters is the economy and affordability. And that doesn’t mean it’s not ICE, but you’ve got to really put it in perspective. I think a lot of folks that, frankly, are not Latino will say, well, there’s got to be some racial or ethnic reason why voters need to vote or why they’re motivated.

That’s not true. The economy is what is motivating these voters. They’re lower on the economic ladder, more blue collar, far more price sensitive than any group in the country. And so again, by a three to one margin, all of the polling is saying this is about the economy, the economy, and the economy. So that really needs to be the lesson that Wednesday morning after a very historic election that Democrats have — is they’ve got to fix this problem.

There’s this obsession with saying the way you talk to Latinos is through the immigration lens. It’s not only not true now, it’s never been true. Again, I’m not saying it’s not an important issue, but it’s not nearly as important as fixing the economy. That’s what I think the Democrats are going to need to be thinking about positioning for the 2028 presidential election, is can we become a blue collar working class party again focused on building an economy for those folks.

If they can do it, they stand a very good chance of realigning the 270 map through the Sun Belt, winning back and holding on to Latino voters. If they can’t, there’s every likelihood, every expectation that Latino voters will go back to Republicans in that scenario.

JESSICA:

So which states that are considered swing states tonight, especially for the Senate, have a large share of Latino voters and the decision could ride on which way they break?

MIKE:

Well, certainly Texas is one that we’re all watching right now. And I would say North Carolina and Georgia are the next two, but those aren’t contended. The places you really need to watch are Ohio and Kansas because about five to seven percent of their electorates are Hispanic. And we don’t think of those as traditionally Hispanic states, but five to seven points is going to be larger than the margin of victory in both of those states.

What’s really fascinating about this year, after doing this for 35 years, this is the year where it’s going to be the most consequential ever. It will be the most consequential voting bloc for the next thirty years now because it’s starting to grow. I’m getting calls from Alaska and the Peltola race talking about the Latino vote because of the fishery and energy industries.

It’s happening in Maine. Wherever there’s a blue collar industry, you’re going to find Latinos are going to be occupied there. And even though they may be only five to seven percent of the entire electorate, a lot of these races are going to be decided by that margin or less. That makes the difference.

JESSICA:

Is there anything you can tell us about the Latino voters that you’ve assessed to date who are staying with Trump versus those who are breaking with him? And is it really socioeconomic?

MIKE:

That’s a great question. No, what tends to happen is there’s this kind of obsession with the social conservative nature of Latinos that describes why a lot of these voters for socially conservative reasons support the Republican party. That number has always been about a third of Hispanic voters. It ranges from about 25-26% percent to about 30-32% percent depending on the state. That won’t change. Those voters will not leave. But it’s also not accurate to say that this is a very fast growing part of the Latino community. There is erosion of Catholic affiliation to becoming more evangelical.

But for every Latino that leaves the Catholic Church to become more fundamentalist, some leave religion altogether. The fastest growing group of religion amongst Latinos is no religion at all. And those voters tend to have very progressive cultural positions, much more progressive economic positions, and tend to look more like the overall American electorate than a culturally conservative Republican voter.

JESSICA:

Are they young?

MIKE:

They’re kind of a mix of everything, but these younger voters that are happening right now, the most fascinating characteristic — and I think that’s going to redefine American politics — is the fact that they’re far, far more likely to be independent voters, non-affiliated with either party than any other group in America. They’re the least partisan by a wide stretch. They’re also the least racially polarized. So they don’t view themselves — because they’re not, generally — as either Black or White voters. They don’t respond to the arguments that I think a lot of people want to impose on them.

Back to the immigration question, they just don’t see this as a racial or ethnic issue as much as other groups might want them to or see them as. And again, those two dynamics, I think, have the promise of perhaps breaking us out of this hyper polarized era that has basically suffocated our democracy and made our work intractable and left our politics very zero sum.

I think over the course of the next decade, we’re going to see that change. The youth remains the great hope.

These younger voters … the most fascinating characteristic — and I think that’s going to redefine American politics — is the fact that they’re far, far more likely to be independent voters, non-affiliated with either party than any other group in America. They’re the least partisan by a wide stretch. They’re also the least racially polarized.

JESSICA:

Yeah. Let’s talk about Texas for a minute. I’m curious what it would take for Latino voters to put Talarico over the top and whether you think they will. What has his campaign understood about reaching them that other Democrats have missed?

MIKE:

I went to South Texas in September and I got to interview James Talarico. I wanted to ask him just this question. So I did. And I asked him why he was getting as much attraction with Hispanic voters, more so than other candidates — including, by the way, he’s out polling Gina Hinojosa. Not by much, but measurable and consequential and enough to be interesting enough for somebody like me to ask the question. And what he said was fascinating.

What he said was Latinos are part of a generation of Americans who feel that the American dream has been stolen from them. And the reason why that answer struck me was because if I had asked a Kamala Harris or a Joe Biden that question, they would have said this is about ICE raids or mass deportations, and let’s approach this as a racial question. James Talarico is not saying that. That doesn’t mean he doesn’t support reining in ICE. Of course he does. But he understands that first and foundationally, this is a class and generational voter.

And that’s what the breath of fresh air is in the Democratic Party — realizing if we approach these folks as a generation of Americans, a generation that has grown up more multicultural, more diverse, and more pluralistic than any generation of Americans before, we’re going to start getting a better conversation with them. We’re going to connect with them more. And that’s absolutely right. That’s what I think is the key — once he approaches them that way he’s getting the opportunity to have a longer conversation, which gets to the other part of what you were asking. That is, a lot of these counties we focus so much on in south Texas, if you start to see a 65-Democrat, 35-Republican vote share, that’s enough for James Talarico to win. It’s actually arguably enough for Gina Hinojosa to win. And the wave is moving in that direction, at least from early turnout numbers.

Now, we don’t know how these voters are going to vote. Texas does tend to be a little bit more conservative than a place like California. But a lot of these counties where Hispanics were voting a majority for Donald Trump — if you see that move into parity, a 15- to 20-point shift, that’s likely enough to see James Talarico win. And right now it’s looking like the margin is bigger than that. So I’m not somebody who’s ever been a believer that Texas is going to go blue. I never believed that the Beto O’Rourke phenomenon was real. I am predicting that James Talarico will win tonight.

JESSICA:

Wow. That’s huge. Sounds like he also may have studied your work.

MIKE:

Maybe. I think there’s a reason why they gave me the opportunity to sit down and talk.

JESSICA:

Right. So beyond Latino voters, Trump won in ‘24 in part by gaining ground with even more of the Democratic coalition, including younger men — strong support, as you said, from working class voters, some Black men. Which parts of that coalition still look solid to you and which are starting to fracture?

There is nothing solid about Donald Trump’s coalition anymore. Even older white male MAGA voters are peeling off.

MIKE:

There is nothing solid about Donald Trump’s coalition anymore. Even older white male MAGA voters are peeling off. At least it’s softening. I’m not suggesting that there’s going to be a thirty percent defection rate amongst MAGA voters. That’s not going to happen. But what we may see is we may see a one, two, three point percent lower turnout rate. And in a place like Texas, that’s the whole ballgame. In a place like Kansas, that’s the race. In a place like Ohio, that’s the race.

That’s why this cycle is so competitive. The fact that Georgia and North Carolina are not the competitive states show that there is sagging amongst the Republican base. There’s just no question about it. The only question is how big and how much.

All of those other demographics, — and I think this is really important, we have to remember what’s happening right now. There’s got to be some real sober, clear eyed analysis about this. There’s nothing that the Democratic Party is doing to win these voters back other than not being a Republican. I’m saying that because there’s going to be a very big historic night for Democrats. But on Wednesday morning, drinking that cup of coffee and feeling a little bit better about the world and where it’s heading, you have to realize the hard work is just beginning — to get back to that working class place, to hold these voters, that’s the real challenge. Because again, it’s easy to be against Trump, especially when things are historically bad, but that doesn’t mean that these voters won’t go back in a meaningful way to the Republicans in 2028.

JESSICA:

There’s this big debate in the Democratic Party, which is, they feel like if they win control of both houses, they have to really push on the affordability issues, health care, all of that. But how much should they also focus on things like oversight and investigations? And then there’s even some pushing for impeachment. In your analysis, what should that ratio be?

MIKE:

I don’t think that they’re either or. I think they’re going to have to do both because you can’t just walk away from justice, nor should you. Look, I believe that the best thing the Democrats could do is literally before they even take office — and it’s probably wise to do it before they’re sworn in — is to announce 10 corruption reform measures for Congress, including giving more teeth to the emoluments clause, announce what the investigative committees are going to look like, and work on passing legislation to rein in the executive branch, which has gotten completely out of control with the over-the-top corruption that’s going on.

Defy the president to veto it. Let him veto it. We all know why he will do that for his own purposes. Let the American people see that. But, and your question is the right one, they’re gonna have to walk and chew gum at the same time. They’re gonna have to do something different than what their coalition has done with the economy in the past. They can’t just go with a Build Back Better bill with a gazillion dollars of building bridges ten years from now.

The best thing the Democrats could do is before they even take office … announce 10 corruption reform measures for Congress. … Defy the president to veto it. Let him veto it. We all know why he will do that for his own purposes. Let the American people see that.

That’s not the way working class people view the economy. They’re worried about paying the rent Friday. They’re not worried about getting a job building a bridge in ten years from now. And so those types of reforms are going to have to become central again to becoming a hard hat party. They’re going to have to get back to people who are on the construction sites and people who are hauling things for a living and working with their hands.

If the Democrats can’t do that — at least begin the policy process to go down that road in a meaningful way, we’re just going to be ping pong balling back in between the parties the same way we are with working class people.

JESSICA:

OK, I want to play a quick game with you. You can participate or not. I know you’re calling Texas. Your prediction is Texas to Talarico. I’m guessing you’re Michigan for Democrats.

MIKE:

Oh, yeah. I don’t think the race is that close.

JESSICA:

OK. Ohio, Sherrod Brown.

MIKE:

Sherrod Brown wins.

JESSICA:

Really? I’ve heard that’s closer than we think.

MIKE:

It might be, but look, that’s a Trump plus-eleven state last time. Trump wins by eleven. That’s really a red state. Sherrod Brown hasn’t, except for maybe one or two polls in the aggregate, been behind. And again, the real question is going to be whether we get the turnout from these rural white Republican voters that just have not been showing up come with this Election Day miracle that they require. There has been almost a four hundred percent increase in the early vote turnout amongst Latinos. Those are not Trump voters.

JESSICA:

Wow. OK. North Carolina and Georgia, I’m sure you’re going to say Democrats. So then Maine, you’re saying, is a jump ball.

MIKE:

Yeah. And I’ve got a little trauma because I spent fourteen million dollars against Susan Collins in 2020 and we weren’t able to get rid of her. Maine is a very peculiar, unique state. Everybody there literally knows her. It is very much like a southern state in the northern part of the country. But tariffs are a real thing up there. The relationship with Canada that has spoiled so badly matters. I’m saying no. Conservatively, I think that the Democrats get to 52 in the Senate, but I would not be surprised if Collin loses.

JESSICA:

Okay, I get it. I think of her sort of like Harry Reid. Like you see no evidence for why she should win, but she wins because she’s like got the state locked up.

MIKE:

Love that analogy. I may steal that.

JESSICA:

Okay, go for it. And then Mary Peltola in Alaska.

MIKE:

Peltola wins.

JESSICA:

Really? And you think that’ll be like caught up in legislation and lawsuits forever, or she actually gets seated?

MIKE:

No, I think she gets — well, look, here’s the thing. This is where it could get really fascinating is that Alaska is the only state that takes longer than California to vote.

JESSICA:

Yeah, it’s insane.

MIKE:

Because you’ve got to bring in votes from the Klondike, and they literally fly them in and stuff like that. We love Alaska, but they’ve got better reasons why than California does. Then they’ve got ranked choice voting. Once you get the votes in, they do ranked choice. I think Peltola will win. I’m hoping it’s not a split Senate or that’s determinative because then things will get really very messy, with Alaska being the deciding vote on a majority in the Senate. My hope is that she’s the 52nd or 53rd. I think things will go smoother. I don’t believe that the court challenges will be much of an issue — there’s just too much has been litigated on it before. It is smarmy politics. It is nasty. Both sides have done it, we shouldn’t be engaged in it, but it doesn’t seem to be anything illegal’s gone on.

JESSICA:

OK, last question. I’m curious about the results tonight. If it is such a — as we’ve said before — thumping for Trump, what would that tell us about whether another Republican can carry his coalition in 2028?

MIKE:

Trump is a unique person on the national stage. I think he always has been. The problem is I don’t think that the coalition that he’s built is based on anything ideological. I think it’s built largely on two things. One is a cult of personality, and the second is the rejection of the Democratic Party. And as long as there’s that strong anti, the only thing that can really save the Republican coalition is Democrats.

The reverse is true. What is holding and animating the Democratic coalition is basically antipathy or hatred for the Republicans. That is a defining feature of our politics today. The Republican Party, though, faces a very extraordinary set of circumstances. I think the chance of the Republican wing of the spectrum balkanizing, going in five, ten different directions, is far greater than just a standard center-right far-right split.

JESSICA:

Are you worried about the same on the Democratic side?

MIKE:

Yeah. I think this is the sign of a populist era that’s emerging. The right-left spectrum really no longer exists. These are not two healthy functioning parties, which is why there’s so much dissatisfaction with them. There’s a lot of reasons as to why that is. It’s probably another episode for another show, but it is very important ,because the parties as we know them — I don’t even want to say are going to change. They have already changed. Neither of them look like what they did ten years ago, and we’re operating under the assumption that they still are.

One team’s win, like the Democrats will win tonight, is somehow a mandate of victory for what they stand for when nobody really knows what they stand for. That’s the same conundrum that the Republicans felt in 2024. It led them to this gerrymandering, dummymandering process in Texas, thinking we have this new coalition of these newfound Republicans and we’re going to have a new MAGA majority when it had nothing to do with that at all. It was simply people were upset with the Democrats.

So until both parties understand that, and they have no incentive to understand that, we’re going to keep operating in this environment where people are saying we need economic reform, we need governmental reform, and what we get back is, who can play water polo with who? Who’s allowed in which bathroom? You know, all these ridiculous performative issues that aren’t addressing anything because the government is either incapable or unwilling to address the extraordinary economic and technological changes that we’re going through.

JESSICA:

Mike, you are an absolute dream to talk to and a wealth of information. I’m so grateful for your time. Thank you.

MIKE:

Anytime. Always great to be with you, Jessica.

What Election Betting Odds Can (and Can’t) Tell Us

Q&A with investigative reporter Drey of the Drey Dossier on election betting markets, how the media reports results, and why the government wants your passport information.

Jessica Yellin

Nov 3

READ IN APP

For daily news, updates, and analysis, be sure to follow us on Instagram. If you are able to support independent journalism, please make a tax-deductible donation to News Not Noise here.

People used to call me with the latest poll numbers. Now they call to tell me what Kalshi says.

Election betting markets have become part of how Americans follow a race. But what are those odds actually telling us? And what happens when people treat them as a preview of the results?

Today I’m joined by Drey of The Drey Dossier to discuss how these platforms work, how a small number of wealthy bettors could influence the numbers, and her concerns about a feedback loop: betting odds become news, viewers place bets, and those new odds become the next headline.

We also discuss the Associated Press’s relationship with Kalshi, how media projections differ from final election results, and the questions that arise when betting payouts depend on those projections.

We also speak about passport records and voter eligibility: proposed changes to how that information is shared, privacy concerns, and what she’s tracking on government websites.

As results come in, keep this distinction in mind: betting odds reflect wagers. Projections can change. Official vote counts take time.

Watch our conversation at the top of this newsletter. Below is a transcript that has been lightly edited for clarity.

News Not Noise is a reader-supported publication. To receive exclusive content and support our work, please consider becoming a paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

Jessica Yellin:

Hi, everyone. How is your midterms day going so far? I know the big question on your mind is: Who is going to win and how do we even know when they do?

One of the odd realities of 2026 is that some people are evaluating the races in new ways. They look beyond the polls to platforms like Kalshi, where people bet on election outcomes. They can treat those bets as a forecast of what’s going to happen. And one of the weirdnesses of our era is that some people trust them more than polls. That matters because those predictions can actually shape whether Americans believe the results, if the final totals end up looking a little bit different from what they expected.

So how does all that make sense? Who makes decisions at Kalshi? Can someone with enough money move the numbers? And what goes into those analyses?

I’m excited to have a conversation about all this with somebody I have just started becoming aware of, Drey of the Drey Dossier, who has been investigating this and has also been looking into the administration’s push to use passport records to check voters’ citizenship. So, Drey, first of all, hi. Nice to really meet you.

Drey:

Nice to meet you, Jessica. Thank you so much for having me. I’m a big fan, and I appreciate all the work. You’re a real hustler out there, getting all this news out, and I really appreciate it.

Jessica:

Thank you. I appreciate the investigative work you do, too. I know it’s not easy.

I’m super interested in Kalshi because I can’t tell you how many people now call me up and they’re like, “Well, Kalshi is showing this and that.” The way they used to say, “Well, the Siena poll shows this, but the Wall Street Journal, Peter Hart poll shows that.” Now they’re looking at these betting markets as if it’s like gospel.

I’m hoping you can break it down for us a little bit. Can you start first with the basics? On platforms like Kalshi, people can put money on who wins an election. So how does that work? And when we see that a candidate’s odds are improving, does that actually reflect reality? Or can somebody with a lot of money drive that race in a way on Kalshi that doesn’t actually reflect the real world?

Drey:

I’ll start by saying that the odds on Kalshi look like the wisdom of the crowd. But in reality, that crowd is quite small, because a nonprofit called the Anti-Corruption Data Collective found that on Polymarket only 1% of the accounts were behind 68% of the money that’s bet on midterms.

Jessica:

Wait, so that. So it’s Polymarket.

Drey:

They found that 68% of the money that was bet on midterms came from 1% of accounts. It can look like the voice of the people when in fact it’s actually not. There is a lot of trouble we can have with how this sways — how people perceive these votes are going to happen.

And just to lay some groundwork for people: Kalshi is obviously an app where people can put real money on things like who will control Congress, which you would think already has enough of our money — taxpayer wise and otherwise — on the line. It’s regulated by the federal government. The government tried to stop a lot of this and the courts cleared the way before the 2024 election. So now there’s money on top of your tax money that’s at stake for who can potentially sit in Congress.

Jessica:

Wow. I want to ask you about the relationship between the Associated Press and Kalshi. For folks who somehow don’t know, the AP is the source that newsrooms rely on for election results. They’re sort of the gold standard. I’m curious, what is the AP providing to Kalshi? Is there any kind of conflict of interest or what safeguards are in place to protect the AP?

Drey:

This year the AP started selling their live counts for the race calls to Kalshi, where they’ll show up essentially right next to the bets. Kalshi’s house rules say that the house can bet and pay out early once four of its eight designated outlets call the race for one party. And as long as none of the others disagree, then bets can be paid out that night.

Some of the conflicts of interests here: Now you have the AP being the data provider for all of the leading networks, like ABC, CBS, NBC, CNN. They all switched to AP in 2025, when they used to get calls from Edison. You had data coming from different places. So with places like CNN having separate deals with Kalshi, there’s a lot of potential for that corruption.

Then, of course, you have Donald Trump’s son, Donald Trump Jr., on the board of Kalshi.

Jessica:

But you’re not suggesting that they would change the election results?

Drey:

No, I’m not suggesting that I know where that data is coming from or how they collect that data. Whatever is projected on your screen from each of these networks — they each can make their own call based on that data, but it is all coming from the same data outlet.

That’s just worth keeping in mind, because when you see five different projections on screen, sometimes you think it’s five separate data streams — when it’s all coming from one. That’s why it’s important to pause and let the counts continue. I mean, counts go through December 3rd — I believe they let all of the mail and ballots and stuff come in. So it’s important to be patient and just let those official counts come through.

Jessica:

Right. You’re making an important point that I can also underscore, which is: I know many viewers assume that when you see a vote call, like a projection by a network, it means that the votes have fully been counted and this is a final call. But in fact, it’s all a level of projection, which they do say. And the number that the media announces could change before all the votes are finally counted.

Drey:

On November 23rd in California, while we’re still counting, there are other bills that are trying to get pushed through, like making a passport part of eligibility in order for votes to be counted. So there are some things that can shift as we’re in the middle of counting. While we all may have voted in the midterms, it’s important not to exhale quite yet because there’s still a lot of people that need to be on watch for any of that to happen.

Jessica:

OK, I’ll go back to that one. But one question on Kalshi that surprised me from your reporting is that essentially, if there’s a vote call tonight and I bet on someone and that person is projected the winner, I’ll get paid out tonight. And if in the counting that changes and the person I bet on actually loses in the end. I still get the money. Kalshi doesn’t change who gets the money if the actual final total changes.

Drey:

I couldn’t find that part. That’s kind of the question, right? Because I couldn’t find that part in their document, in their contract. It’s important to remember that four out of eight have to all agree on a consensus. So long as the other four don’t disagree, then from what I read in this contract, that is how they will then allow payouts to potentially happen. I don’t know what their policy is with it if it switches. I’m sure that that will cause a whole other slew of problems. But I just think that considering that is something that we need to be on the watch for, it’s important to remember that uncertainty we need to keep in the back of our heads.

Jessica:

So you’re saying four of eight networks?

Drey:

Yeah, so four of the eight leading networks.

Jessica:

Which are all based on the AP. So it’s all like this.

Drey:

Well, CNN has their own set, and has already been using Kalshi’s numbers as a means of projecting votes because they have their own partnership with them. I’m not saying that anybody’s doing anything malicious. I think that we just need to be very, very vigilant of how this is going to affect people’s money in their wallet.

Jessica:

Right. And also, if the foundational information is wrong, then everybody’s wrong because they’re all basing it on the same foundation. It happens all the time. We saw it with Bush versus Gore. You know, it can happen. It’s a normal thing, that not having all the data could make your projection wrong. That’s a very normal thing.

I’m curious on Kalshi: Can someone with really deep pockets move the odds and make a candidate look stronger than they really are? And is there evidence that people have tried to do that?

Drey:

Like I said at the beginning, the Nonprofit Anti-Corruption Data Collective found that Polymarket had 1% of accounts behind 68% of the money that was bet on the midterms. I would say that that absolutely can happen. And I do believe that three congressional candidates were tossed out for betting on their own races. They had to face a federal judge for that in April.

Jessica:

One of the conversations in 2024 was that these betting markets are for sure being manipulated. People assumed that and that there were people who were going to bet on somebody to make their candidate look stronger. But we’ve never been able to actually definitively find somebody doing that or prove that. We assume it based on the numbers, but it’s not something that we’ve determined. Is that right?

Drey:

To my knowledge. I think the loop worries me the most. This is what I would say for anybody who’s worried about this. I think CNN using these odds for coverage and then people betting on what they just watched and then those odds going back on TV as the news for the coverage is the loop that concerns me the most.

Jessica:

I see. Let’s turn to your reporting on how the government checks whether someone is eligible to vote and what happens when its records are incomplete or even wrong. I know that you’ve looked into efforts by the Trump administration to use passport records to check people’s eligibility and citizenship. So can you explain a little bit about what’s currently used, what’s being proposed, and what concerns you?

Drey:

I don’t know about anybody else, but just because of all of the conversations around the SAVE Act and Congress trying to pass a law to show a passport for voting — which didn’t go through or anything — I was nervous enough today when I went to vote that I brought a passport just in case, even though it’s not legal for them to ask you to show that. It kind of goes to show this deep-seated doubt now that at least I know I have with what this government is trying to push through.

The important thing for people to remember is that the State Department changed its own rules in October. It published a rule that lets passport records be shared with government agencies and nonprofit groups to check voter eligibility. Now, that still has to go through the House and it’s still being talked about; I think you can give comments until November, which is, again, in the middle of when California is still counting its ballots. That is something that potentially could go through. Something that was also on California’s voting today was that if you want to pass a proposition that allows for that passport to be used for voter eligibility.

Jessica:

What kind of changes are you tracking on government websites involving passports and voter information?

Drey:

In May, I found a working copy of —well, a copy that was behind a firewall — Vote.gov being used inside the White House office, built by the National Design Studio, along with Passports.gov. I did a video about this back in May, I believe. It was all registered to the White House and has since been moved over to the State Department, Passports.gov. I worry about the data that is being collected from the information we give for our passports, our photos, our place of birth.

Jessica:

So what you’re saying is that these websites that used to be the domain of the agencies are now under the White House?

Drey:

They were built under the White House, but now I’m seeing them start to move. So they were built under the executive office of the president, the EOP, National Design Studio. Now I’m seeing them starting to move them around — like the passports.gov, which the passport state office back in May had no idea about when I called them. That was originally under the EOP, and now it’s moved under the State Department. So I don’t know if it’s being used or not, but it’s worth keeping in mind that data is starting to travel now into different agencies.

Jessica:

So It sounds like this is part of the consolidation of all of our information that this administration has said they’re doing, beginning with DOGE. Before you had your tax information here and your passport information here, and the two shall never mix. Now they’re consolidating everything so they can have one national database with all Americans.

Drey:

Yeah, I believe they call that “frictionless,” Jessica. We want to live in a frictionless world. Apparently, keeping that separation of church and state isn’t so sexy when it comes to not having your way in voting, you know?

Jessica:

Well, Drey, I love that you’re looking into all this stuff. I’m really grateful to you for delving into it and explaining, especially to a lot of people for whom all this is very new, especially Kalshi. Thank you.

Drey:

Thank you so much for having me. Grateful.

Jessica:

And you can find the Drey Dossier on all the socials, especially Substack and YouTube. Check her out.

To everyone watching, just a reminder that the only votes that count are the ones you actually cast. We will be here throughout Election Day, bringing you updates from key races, explaining what is actually going on, and bringing you the projected vote totals when they do come in. Stay tuned. Take some breaks and I’ll see you soon.

NEWSLETTER · 04 Nov 2026, 05:59

The Dawn of the Vikings

A History of Mankind (418)

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

The Dawn of the Vikings

A History of Mankind (418)

David Roman

Nov 4

READ IN APP

Germanic-language literature emerged from a combination of half-baked Christian themes and influences, as well as the steppe traditions of a simple, warlike people fond of its heroes and warlords.

Take the poet Cynewulf, who wrote in one of various dialects of Anglo-Saxon language in the 9th century: he left behind a long poem called “Christ” in which he takes the idea that Jesus was “King of the Jews” quite seriously, and surrounds the Messiah by a warband, his apostles.

A History of Mankind is a reader-supported publication. To receive new posts and support my work, consider becoming a free or paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

Homeric-style honor and bravery are this literature’s central subjects. In the “Battle of Brunanburh,” a later poem about an actual battle in 937, Danish king Anlaf enters the enemy camp before battle, disguised as a bard, and sings for the enemy English king and his companions; after Anlaf leaves, a soldier who had once served with him informs the English king about Anlaf’s real identity. When the king asks why he didn’t warn them before, the soldier responds:

“If I had betrayed the person whom I served before, would you put your trust on me, your servant now?”

The king accepts the explanation and gets his army ready; the next day he defeats Anlaf. In some pieces, this primitive directness detracts from the final product: “The Phoenix” takes “De Ave Phoenice” – an erudite, not-quite-Christian 4th century poem filled with Graeco-Roman references – and produces a clumsy combination of Latin and Anglo-Saxon.

Credit: historyanswers.com

In the “Song of Hildebrand,” the earliest poem in German, written roughly at the , this primitivism works to greater effect as the old Indo-European theme of a man who unknowingly fights and kills his son is displayed in a cruel, sparse language¹ lacking flourishes. The “Heliand,” written in Old Saxon (an antecessor of Low German) as a poetic version of the Gospels at Louis the Pious’ behest, like Cynewulf turns the Apostles into warriors, presents Christians as robust and not averse to violence, and omits to cite the famous quote about turning the other cheek.

The Beowulf, an English epic poem in the Germanic tradition that is the most famous piece of Anglo-Saxon literature, was compiled at some point after 700, possibly around 800, from a combination of old Germanic legends and some additions made by its anonymous author. This probably was a Christian priest with some classical education who intended to write a Germanic-style Aeneid².

Like other early Anglo-Saxon poetry, including The Seafarer³ and the Finnesburg Fragment⁴, the Beowulf is written in alliterative verses with few flourishes and no rhyme, where the rhythm is provided by the repetition of sounds, typically two or three per line. This old English trick – present in multiple modern idioms, like “without rhyme or reason”, “fair or foul”, “kith and kin” – was facilitated by the love of compound words inherent in Germanic languages⁵ and drove the author to use multiple metaphors for everyday objects and sights, providing Beowulf much of its punch⁶.

Such an approach remained commonplace for centuries to follow, with similar alliterations to be found all over English-language works. Indeed, Beowulf’s style, themes and characters served as inspiration for much of later English poetry⁷, and even prose: JRR Tolkien’s The Lord of the Rings, the most popular 20th century English-language book, takes its very name from a metaphor for king – “lord of rings” – used in Beowulf to allow for alliteration⁸.

FROM THE ARCHIVES:

How England Became Anglo

David Roman

May 14

Wales was the only part of the Western Roman that wasn’t conquered at any time by Germanic or Gothic invaders. A relatively small slice of rugged terrain, by the 7th century it was divided in four petty kingdoms and several tiny territories with various degrees of relationship with the larger ones.

Read full story

The Beowulf story, in which the namesake character – a Geat (Gothic or Jute) warrior probably from Sweden – helps the king of the Danes and later returns home to become king of the Geats, is typically brutal, depicting a world of honor-obsessed warlords given to seek glorious feats of arms amid what outsiders might be tempted to describe as squalid squabbles for the control of poor, dank villages.

This is also a misleadingly simple plot: a man looks for a job, succeeds, gets a promotion to become king, kills a dragon, dies and is lamented by his people⁹. Or, more properly, Beowulf is the story of a man who thinks he’s won in battle and, many years later, must fight it again and doesn’t win: the story of a man caught up by his own destiny. Borges, in “Literaturas…,” gives this masterful explanation:

“There are few possible plots; one of them is the man who comes across his own destiny; Beowulf would be this eternal plot, in a rudimentary shape… One gets the impression that the anonymous author was a Northumbrian cleric, stimulated both by Latin reading and Scandinavian traditions. As a Christian, he couldn’t name pagan divinities, but he doesn’t cite the Redeemer or the Saints. Perhaps by chance, thus, he succeeds in giving the impression of an ancient world, so ancient that it precedes mythologies and theologies.”

The epic, with close similarities with Nordic sagas and other Germanic tales, is still gripping and told with disarming directness and insight, very much in the Homeric style. Its Nordic themes and locations (the Heorot Hall in Beowulf has been identified as Lejre, seat of Danish kings) are eerily appropriate – early in the text, a funeral is described in which the body of Beowulf’s father Scyld Scefing is placed inside a boat that is left to drift offshore:

At the harbor stood a ring-prowed ship, vessel of a prince, covered

with ice and ready to set out, the dear beloved king,

dispenser of the circlet, laid down in the lap of the ship, whose

mast was glorious. The ship made ready to burn, there were many

precious valuables of decorated armor brought from distant lands.

Ship-burials soon became associated in the mind of learned Europeans with raiders from Scandinavia, even though it’s not clear that most, or even many, great Scandinavian lords set up such ceremonies for themselves¹⁰. It’s also not clear that many of these lords, or their followers, would have been happy to be called “Vikings,” the moniker they soon received.

The 9th century Oseberg ship, found in Norway, may or may have not been used for a ship-burial.

Indeed, it’s likely that most Norsemen of the late 1st millennium would have been appalled to be called Viking, a word that stands for “pirate” and “raider.” Even actual pirates and raiders would have probably objected to be described as such, much like the real-life equivalents of Odysseus and the trader-raiders of the Aegean two millennia earlier would have not enjoyed being called outlaws. In their view, their activities combining trading with some robbing, killing, kidnapping and raping were just synergic, and all coalesced towards the same objective of enriching themselves and the poor villages they came from.

This problem only got worse over time, as the word Viking appropriately came to define a lifestyle rather than a people. Eventually, non-ethnic Norsemen, like Baltic and Slavic tribesmen, became Vikings; by the 11th century, a Swedish runestone celebrates the exploits of a man called Assur, son of Jarl Hakan, who “kept the Viking watch” against such raiders targeting the Scandinavian coast itself¹¹.

A tough existence on the margins of the known world, exposed to a difficult environment, made beliefs about the half-dead popular across Scandinavia. The “draugr” – “undead” – were seen as reanimated corpses coming back to a kind of life. These were often troublemakers and witches, often pitted against heroes in the sagas; in an anticipation of the later, rich tradition about zombies, some of these revenants were helpful – for example, dead women who return to cook and clean in a farmstead, although they still caused horror among living residents¹².

In fact, the Norsemen were wary of even their own gods. Asgard, the Nordic dwelling place for them, the Scandinavian Mount Olympus, was believed to contain temples: so that Nordic gods were the only ones who practiced religion themselves, perhaps praying to the older gods of the Romans and Greeks for some guidance for their reckless existence.

At the same time, it’s not clear whether this notion was directly influenced by the fact that Nordic paganism, as later known and celebrated in multiple works of art, is a 13th century composite, mostly deriving from the works of the Icelandic Snorri Sturluson, who described the Nordic pantheon over two centuries after the Icelandic parliament announced the island’s conversion to Christianity. Thus, the story of Ragnarok, the last battle between good and evil which ends the world, may or may not be derived from the Christian story of the Apocalypse¹³.

Likewise, many spectacular bloody Viking rituals appear to be later inventions, or tall tales. For example, epic poems and later works about the Vikings delighted in describing the so-called “blood eagle ritual,” said to have involved separating a victim’s ribs from their spine to symbolize an eagle’s wings and hanging their lungs out through the wounds while they are still alive. As described, this ritual was very hard to implement, and would result in the victim’s death early in the process – in contrast with many of its popular depictions¹⁴.

A blood eagle ritual? No, a person thrown into a snakepit sometime in the Viking era. Oh, you tricky Vikings.

Share

To read previous posts in the History of Mankind, which is pretty long, you can click here.

So cruel, indeed, that later versions of the story of Hildebrand have father and son reconcile before they fight.

This is, for example, Borges’ opinion in his “Literaturas Germanicas Medievales.” The first Beowulf translation (into Latin) was destroyed in, of all places, Denmark when an English fleet, of all fleets, attacked Copenhagen during the Napoleonic Wars, which Borges finds delightful.

Much translated in following centuries, from an original found in the 10th century Exeter Book, one of just four surviving manuscripts of Old English poetry.

A tantalizing part of a longer poem depicting the last stand of sixty warriors surrounded by enemies. The poem’s themes and style were later repeated in “The Battle of Maldon,” a poem celebrating a similar clash, of that name, in which an English army failed to repulse a Viking raid in 991.

Modern German keeps “Fingerbut” for “thimble” and “handshuh” for “glove,” while both English and German maintain “Rain-bow” (“Regenbogen”) for something described in less poetic terms in most languages.

As Borges observes in “Literaturas…,” not specifically about Beowulf, some metaphors were long admired because of their barbarian flavor, even though they probably were cliches at the time.

Borges, in “Literaturas…,” quotes the example of Samuel Coleridge’s Rime of the Ancient Mariner (1834): “The fair breeze blew,/The white foam flew,/And the forrow followed free./We were the first to ever burst into the silent sea.” Tennyson’s translation of the Battle of Brunanburh has the same pace: “All the field with blood of the fighters/Flowed, from when first the great/Sun-star of morning-tide.”

Tolkien took much more from Beowulf for his Middle Earth universe, from the names of races to characters and monsters.

The summary is W.P. Ker’s, in “Epic and Romance: Essays on Medieval Literature” (1897).

“The boats may have been convenient coffins; they may have been symbols of secular power, with the ship-settings thus being imitations by those of lower social status of the elaborate graves of the powerful; they may have been seen as a ferry taking the man or woman to Valhalla (though there is no evidence for this and, from what we know of Valhalla, no place there for the many women found in boat-burials); or they may be an offering to the gods, perhaps specifically the god and/or goddess of fertility.” Edward James, Op. Cit.

See “The Children of Ash and Elm: A History of the Vikings,” by Neil Price (Penguin, 2022).

Neil Price, Op. Cit.

The first Nordic “Saga” or “Stories” were only written down in Iceland during the late 1100s, more than a hundred years after the nominal end of the Viking Age. Similar problems arise with ‘Celtic paganism’: the stories of the Irish gods are likewise preserved in manuscripts of around 1200 or later.

See “An Anatomy of the Blood Eagle: The Practicalities of Viking Torture,” by Luke John Murphy et al, Speculum, University of Chicago Press, 2021.

-USA: So sind die Midterms gelaufen

-Auto: BMW kappt Prognose – schon wieder

-Bundesregierung: Strenger gegen Huawei

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNTAsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACMsaGNZX8o0_ZRNFUDpkKEEJVKmQczT2hZaPDfzhLxtFWmhiBY-8U_DSFHgdEw9VPZx8w59IuQF_e49df_lK_eXEDoDEU08Jj1-xA3k

Wahlparty gestern Abend in Ohio: Blau führt. (Foto: Reuters)

Guten Morgen, liebe Leserin, lieber Leser,die Wahlerinnen und Wahler in den USA haben Prasident Donald Trump für die zweite Halfte seiner Amtszeit in der Nacht einen dicken Denkzettel geschickt. Trump konnte seine bisherige Mehrheit in beiden Hausern des Kongresses an die Demokraten verloren haben. In den frühen Morgenstunden dieses Mittwochs deutet sich eine demokratische Mehrheit im Reprasentantenhaus an, auch im Senat scheint sie moglich.

Wie ernst Trump selbst die Lage nimmt, lasst sich wohl auf seiner Plattform Truth Social sehen. Dort schimpfte Trump die ganze Nacht vor allem über seinen bisherigen Haus- und Hofsender Fox News. Der verbringe „zu viel Zeit damit“, über die Demokraten zu berichten. Und er fügte trotzig an:

Meine Zahlen sind besser denn je.

Unser Chefautor Jens Münchrath, der die Wahl in Florida für Sie verfolgt, sieht das etwas anders. Er kommentiert heute Morgen:

Trump war es, der die Wahl zu einer Abstimmung über sich selbst erklarte. Es ist seine Niederlage, obwohl er nicht auf dem Wahlzettel stand.

Tatsachlich zeigen die Zahlen schon früh in dieser Wahlnacht einige empfindliche Niederlagen für Trumps Republikaner: So hat der Demokrat Roy Cooper die Senatswahl im Bundesstaat North Carolina gewonnen – einer der wichtigen, sogenannten „Flips“, bei denen die Demokraten einen bisherigen Senatssitz von den Republikanern gewonnen haben. Die Demokraten benotigen netto vier solcher Flips, um die Kontrolle im Senat zu übernehmen. Auch im historisch republikanisch dominierten Texas scheint ein solcher Flip moglich, wo der Demokrat James Talarico in ersten Hochrechnungen führt – das ware historisch.

Auch wenn viele Ergebnisse noch ausstehen, gibt es drei Lehren aus dieser Zwischenwahl:

- Die US-Demokratie lebt. Zwar steht der Hartetest, wie Trump auf die Ergebnisse reagieren wird, noch aus. Bisher aber lasst sich festhalten: Die US-Demokratie scheint stabiler, als nach zwei Jahren fortdauernder Attacken Trumps auf die Institutionen befürchtet. Diesen Befund stützt auch, dass selbst für den reichsten Mann der Welt die US-Wahlerinnen und -Wahler nicht kauflich sind. Elon Musk investierte 366 Millionen Dollar in diesen Wahlkampf – bisher bleibt das gewünschte Ergebnis aus. - Trumps Wahlerkoalition von 2024 brockelt. Laut Nachwahlbefragungen verzeichneten die Republikaner deutliche Rückgange bei mannlichen Schwarzen, Latinos, Jüngeren, evangelikalen Christen und Wahlern ohne Hochschulabschluss. - Außenpolitisch bleiben die USA unberechenbar. Dass ein moglicher Mehrheitswechsel im Kongress nicht das Ende der Trump’schen Chaos-Herrschaft bedeutet, bekam die Welt noch in der Wahlnacht vor Augen geführt. Da gab Trump den Kollegen der „Financial Times“ ein Interview, in dem er unmittelbare Angriffe auf Iran nach den Wahlen ausschloss, andererseits aber drohte: „Wir sind vorbereitet, unsere Waffen geladen, wenn wir losschlagen müssen.“

Am frühen Morgen europaischer Zeit setzte Trump über den US-Sender CBS eine weitere Botschaft ab:

Wir werden uns nicht blamieren.

Wobei die Chancen gut stehen, dass das eher für das Land als seinen Prasidenten gilt.

BMW iX3 bei der Autoshow in Busan: Das Auto als Verlustbringer? (Foto: Bloomberg)

Auto: BMW kappt Prognose – schon wieder

BMW hat gestern Abend seine Prognose für dieses Jahr korrigiert – und zwar zum zweiten Mal. Im Kerngeschaft rechnet der Münchener Autokonzern nur noch mit einer Ebit-Marge von minus 0,5 bis plus 0,5 Prozent. Das heißt übersetzt: BMW halt auch Verluste für moglich.

Der Konzern führt zwei Gründe an: Wegen des schnellen Anlaufs seiner neuen Generation Elektrofahrzeuge schreibt er Teile seiner vorherigen Fahrzeug- und Technologiegenerationen ab. Zum anderen dürften im Zuge eines Restrukturierungsprogramms zusatzliche Rückstellungen für ein Abfindungsprogramm notig werden. Zum Jahresbeginn hatte BMW noch vier bis sechs Prozent Marge für moglich gehalten.

In der Gruppe der krisengeplagten deutschen Autohersteller galt BMW bisher wegen seiner umsichtigen Antriebsstrategie noch eher als Lichtblick – doch dessen Strahlkraft scheint nun auch begrenzt. Die Aktie gab nachborslich um drei Prozent nach.

Großbritannien: Exit vom Brexit rückt naher

Die Europaische Union und Großbritannien wollen im großen Stil Handelsbarrieren abbauen – und einen gemeinsamen Freihandelsraum für den Agrar- und Lebensmittelbereich schaffen. Zudem wollen beide Seiten ihre Emissionshandelssysteme miteinander verbinden und jungen Menschen ermoglichen, bis zu drei Jahre im jeweils anderen Land zu leben und zu arbeiten. Auch ihre Handelsschutzmaßnahmen gegen China und andere Drittstaaten wollen Brüssel und London künftig enger aufeinander abstimmen.

Das alles steht im vertraulichen Entwurf einer gemeinsamen Erklarung für den europaisch-britischen Gipfel am 20. November, den meine Kollegen Jakob Hanke Vela und Martin Greive an Land gezogen haben. Ich mag den Briten ja nicht zu nahe treten, aber ich hatte auch schon einen Namen für das Konstrukt: gemeinsamer Binnenmarkt. Dafür hatte man allerdings nicht erst aus der EU austreten und dann fast sieben Jahre lang teuer sein eigenes Ding versuchen müssen.

Schriftzug von Huawei: Einsatz kritischer Komponenten vermeiden. (Foto: Bloomberg)

Bundesregierung: Strenger gegen Huawei

Nach Jahren der nahezu wohlwollenden Partnerschaft lasst sich nun wie im Zeitraffer die wochentliche Verschlechterung des deutsch-chinesischen Verhaltnisses beobachten. Jetzt trifft es erneut Huawei: Das Bundesinnenministerium bereitet einen weitreichenden Bann chinesischer Netzwerktechnik aus den deutschen Mobilfunknetzen vor.

Der Hebel ist eine neue Liste der Bundesnetzagentur, die erstmals auch die 5G-Basisstationen als kritische Komponenten einstuft. Weil Netzausrüster Paketlosungen verkaufen, würde das faktisch auf das Aus für Huawei hinauslaufen – von dem Experten zufolge rund 60 Prozent der deutschen Basisstationen stammen. Telekom, Vodafone und Telefonica sind dagegen.

Wann ein Verbot kommt, lasst die Regierung offen. Der danische Experte John Strand glaubt, Berlin versuche, „sich hinter Brüssel zu verstecken“, wo gerade ein „Cybersecurity Act 2.0“ entsteht. In einem Positionspapier fordert die Bundesregierung allerdings zugleich, dass „weiter gehende nationale Regelungen“ jederzeit zulassig bleiben müssen. Wenn sich Berlin also hinter Brüssel versteckt, dann nur mit einem Notausgang.

Bauministerin Hubertz: Vorwarts, wenn auch in kleinen Schritten. (Foto: Katrin Luxenburger/dpa)

Wohnen: Turbo mit Traufhohe

Ein Jahr nach dem Start zieht Bauministerin Verena Hubertz (SPD) Bilanz ihres Bau-Turbos. Kommunen konnten Bauvorhaben jetzt „bis zu 20 Mal schneller“ zustimmen. In den 393 Kommunen, die freiwillig am Monitoring ihres Ministeriums teilnahmen, gab es von Januar bis Juni Genehmigungen für 7892 Wohnungen. Klingt viel. Nur leider fehlen bundesweit 1,35 Millionen Wohnungen. Es braucht rechnerisch also hunderte weiterer Bau-Turbos, bis Hubertz am Ziel ist.

Ihr Lieblingsbeispiel stammt aus Berlin. Dort verhinderte bislang eine Traufhohe aus der Kaiserzeit mehr Wohnraum, nun werden auf demselben Grundstück aus 250 Wohnungen 305. Das ist der eigentliche Fortschritt: Deutschland baut nicht nur neu, es baut endlich auch alte Regeln ab.

Leergeraumtes Kühlregal: Hacker heizen dem Handel ein. (Foto: AWO Ennepe-Ruhr/dpa)

Handel: Hacker attackieren Kühlregale

Die Angriffswelle auf vernetzte Kühlanlagen hat nach Frankreich nun auch deutsche Supermarkte erreicht. In mehreren Markten von Rewe und Edeka heizten die Kühltheken über Nacht auf 50 Grad plus. Alle Lebensmittel landeten im Müll.

Betroffen sind Handler in mindestens zehn Landern Europas. Oft traf es altere Steuerungen des Herstellers Danfoss, deren Software bislang nicht aktualisiert worden war. Dabei ware Abhilfe kein Hexenwerk. Schon nach den Attacken in Frankreich vor drei Wochen hatte das Bundesamt für Sicherheit in der Informationstechnik geraten, solche Steuerungen nicht direkt aus dem Internet erreichbar zu machen. Und auch ein Software-Update dürfte helfen – günstiger als eine Kühltruhe voller verdorbener Ware ware es allemal.

Start-ups: Weniger als Null und Eins

Zum Schluss schauen wir nach München, wo das Quanten-Start-up Quint 9,1 Millionen Euro eingesammelt hat – eine „Rekordfinanzierung“ für das Unternehmen, wie meine Kollegin Nadine Schimroszik schreibt. Quint will mit einer neuen Chiparchitektur das großte Problem der Branche losen: die vielen Fehler, die Quantencomputer machen.

Die Herausforderung: Die Konkurrenz heißt Google und IBM und investiert Milliarden. Doch Quint-Gründer Michael Hartmann ficht der Großenunterschied nicht an: „Es gab seit der Gründung noch keinen einzigen Tag, an dem ich das Gefühl hatte, zu zweifeln.“ Bemerkenswert für einen Quantenphysiker. Seine Qubits dürfen gleichzeitig 0 und 1 sein – er selbst kennt offenbar nur einen Zustand.

NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 23:30

News, here's your South Korea briefing for Wednesday

Visit Website

Your daily briefing onSOUTH KOREA

Executive Briefing

Lee-Erdogan talks expose the difficult economics of South Korea’s nuclear push

John Lee

South Korean President Lee Jae Myung and Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan agreed to expand cooperation in nuclear energy, defense and industrial development on Tuesday, signing four agreements aimed at strengthening economic ties. The Export-Import Bank of Korea and Turkiye Nuclear Energy Company signed a financing cooperation agreement to assess potential financial support for Korean companies seeking to participate in Turkish nuclear projects, including the planned Sinop power plant. The agreement did not include a financing commitment or construction contract.

The two leaders confirmed progress on Sinop site assessments and a joint working group established following their Nov. 2025 summit. The countries also signed agreements covering energy, science and technology and critical minerals, while pledging to expand joint defense production and exports to third countries. They agreed to elevate their bilateral free trade agreement committee to ministerial level to address trade imbalances, import restrictions and customs delays.

Opinion & Analysis

Multicultural policy needs a broader vision

JoongAng Daily

"Discrimination against multicultural families remains a reality in Korean society, with 13 percent reporting discriminatory experiences. Their economic circumstances remain relatively disadvantaged, and many work in manual occupations. Their net enrollment rate in higher education stands at 61.9 percent, 13 percentage points below the national average. Against this background, repeated presidential criticism of preferential treatment could deepen existing prejudice and discrimination. It could also provide ammunition to groups already hostile toward multicultural families."

South Korea must turn growth into jobs

Dong-a Ilbo

"Government spending can help spread the gains from growth, but there is no reason to assume it can do so more efficiently than the private sector. During this year’s parliamentary audits, lawmakers noted that the government had spent more than 7 trillion won on youth employment programs over the past three years, yet the employment rate for people ages 15 to 29 fell to 45% last year from 46.1% in 2024."

NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 23:23

What A Day: The Mog of War

The midterms are FINALLY here. So is the anxiety.

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

What A Day: The Mog of War

The midterms are FINALLY here. So is the anxiety.

Matt Berg

Nov 3

READ IN APP

THE PHANTOM POLLBOOTH

The midterms have finally arrived. Here’s why Democrats shouldn’t worry about polling error this year — or, at least, not worry too much.

Somebody’s getting mogged tonight. And if the polls turn out to be right — always a colossal “if!” — then that somebody is President Donald Trump and the entire Republican Party.

For those unfamiliar with the term, “mogging” (which Collins Dictionary named word of the year today) means to outshine someone to the point of embarrassment. Seemingly every election indicator available — polls, models and prediction markets — suggest a historic blue wave is upon us, in which Democrats mog the GOP so hard it might just generate some new dictionary-worthy terms to commemorate the moment.

Yet the auguries are so uniformly good for the left, that many Democrats literally just refuse to believe them. Polls have let them down before, most notoriously during Trump’s bizarre political rise in 2016 and resurrection in 2024. So, there exists the possibility that the pollsters will be the ones getting, shall we say, outperformed… by reality. As a headline in The Onion today put it: “Nation Lines Up In Droves To Humiliate Nate Silver,” in reference to the polling guru who recently launched the excellent Still Counting podcast with Crooked Media.

The polling skeptics should keep two things in mind. First, pollsters are painfully aware of their past failures. Many have been tweaking their methodology to account for their past bloopers as if their jobs depended on it. Second, even a massive correction in favor of the GOP… would still mean a pretty good night for Dems, because their current polling lead really is that tremendous.

“If the polls are as wrong as they were in the 2018 and 2022 midterms, Democrats will walk away tonight with 54 seats in the U.S. Senate,” data expert G. Elliott Morris wrote today. “If the polls miss as they did in the recent presidential years, they’ll likely fall short of a majority with just 50.”

In other words: Even if pollsters are off, Democrats have good reason to feel confident that millions of voters will deliver a rebuke of the Republican Party and the Trump administration today.

But will Trump’s intimidation tactics deter people from voting?

State election officials have feared for months that Trump’s team will send federal agents to the polls, potentially scaring voters away. Those concerns remain valid: The Department of Justice deployed a record-breaking 1,000 election monitors to every state today.

Nevada Secretary of State Cisco Aguilar, however, isn’t sweating the deployments, because the “the incompetence is so high” among the president’s team.

“The federal government can’t do anything well,” Aguilar told What a Day. “And the fact that they’re going to be able to coordinate 1,000 observers across the country to do anything impactful is a pipe dream.”

Of course, federal agents could still pull some funny business. “The wild card here is again the administration,” Aguilar added. “You never know what people do in desperation. But we’re prepared for all of it. I’m not fearful of any of it.”

Data shows that Americans want to have their voices heard in this election. Several states set early voting records, including several battlegrounds. Latinos also have reportedly voted in record numbers, driven by opposition to ICE’s immigration campaign.

“We’re not going to be bullied by them,” Aguilar said. “They can go fuck themselves.”

Read: Message Box: An Hour-by-Hour Guide to Staying Sane on Election Night by Crooked’s Dan Pfeiffer

WHAT ELSE? 👀

Donald Trump said that he plans to cut off roughly $4 billion in funding to Israel by the end of the year, admitting that he’s tired of Democrats talking about the issue on the campaign trail. “Why should we suffer the slings from lunatics like an El-Sayed when [Israel doesn’t] need it,” Trump told Fox News. “They don’t need it.” Classic Trump to wake up to a red-hot political issue on [checks notes] the morning of the election.

Think climate change isn’t having an impact on our politics? The climate would beg to differ. Today, more than a dozen polling locations in Florida were forced to relocate as parts of the state saw half a year’s worth of rain in just 36 hours. (“I’ve NEVER seen anything like this,” a local meteorologist tweeted.) A severe flooding emergency was also declared in the Utah desert today. State officials allowed Utahns in the area to vote electronically if they couldn’t make it to the ballot box. Turns out, Mother Nature gets a vote.

A child died after contracting measles in upstate New York, marking the state’s first death related to the disease in three decades, state officials announced. The death is “a devastating reminder of what’s at stake when Washington deliberately erodes decades of progress in protecting Americans from preventable illnesses,” said Gov. Kathy Hochul (D-NY).

The former FBI chief in Chicago accused top Trump officials of pressuring him to investigate the president’s perceived political foes and using him as a “scapegoat” when a case against anti-ICE protestors fell apart. The DOJ and FBI didn’t respond to What A Day’s request for comment.

Schools that restrict access to Google’s AI tools will also lose access to Google Search, the company announced today. “Search and A.I. are deeply integrated,” said Google’s VP for education. There are valid concerns about how AI affects kids’ learning, and how schools can use the technology for downright odd purposes. One recent example: “A 6-year-old imagined her future self. Then her school gave her an AI version.”

LIGHT AT THE END… ☀️

The Pod Save America guys will be on Jimmy Kimmel’s late night show to break down the election results tonight. Tune in!

Three U.S. astronauts on the International Space Station voted today using electronic ballots. What’s your excuse?!

How does NBC’s Steve Kornacki prepare for big election nights? Based on his Spotify listening habits, there’s a good chance Kornacki throws on The Temptations before the country relies on him to guide them through the poll results. “It says my all-time top song is ‘My Girl,’” Kornacki told Wired. “Is Spotify the one that did your listening age or something a couple years ago? Mine was 87 or something.”

Speaking of memes, New York’s government encouraged people to vote today by posting an image of John Pork at the polls. Not familiar with John Pork? He’s an anthropomorphic pigman who wears a red flannel and a sly smile. And for some reason, he’s usually calling you.

The New Yorker published a satirical piece about how outsiders view the lives of D.C. insiders, and I can’t stop laughing at this bit: “Breakfast is scrambled eggs and toast, prepared and hand-fed to me by Mitt Romney,” the author writes. And that’s not the only encounter with the former senator. “Somebody walks by me. It’s Mitt Romney. I wonder why he serves me breakfast by hand every morning. It’s not something we’ve discussed.”

Rock bands, from Metallica to the Fray, have started noticing an influx of parents bringing their kids to shows. “When we were kids and we went to see rock ‘n’ roll, our parents would be like, ‘what the fuck is that shit you’re listening to?’” the manager of Weezer and Green Day told the Wall Street Journal. New father-daughter bonding idea: Headbanging to “Enter Sandman” in the mosh pit.

Edward Freytag, a 66-year-old middle school teacher, mysteriously developed perfect pitch, the ability to immediately identify musical notes by ear, after a motorcycle crash five years ago. Only about 1 in 10,000 people are believed to have perfect pitch, which is thought to be genetic. “The lucky few who have it — a group that has included Mozart, Ella Fitzgerald and Charlie Puth — tend to be musical prodigies,” the New York Times writes. “Edward, however, was the first person known to gain perfect pitch from a brain injury.”

Heidi Klum dressed as her own severed hand for Halloween, continuing her legacy of being the weirdest-dressed person every year. Which costume was her favorite? “The worm in 2022,” she said, referring to this monstrosity. “Because it was not just about painting the worm and building the worm, but it needed to be lubed to make it look like a rain worm. They look slimy, those worms. So I had like five people lubing me down.”
