NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 23:23

America Votes, the World Watches

Is this a rare foreign-policy election? Plus: the Global Briefing news quiz, watch Fareed’s take, United States of deadlock, will the Iran war escalate after the midterms?, and corruption as a voting issue …

ADVERTISEMENT

Tuesday, November 3, 2026 View in browser

America Votes, the World Watches

Foreign policy doesn’t usually determine the outcome of US elections—except, of course, when it does. In 2006, Democrats won congressional majorities by lambasting then-President George W. Bush’s war in Iraq. Consequentially for future elections, a young state senator named Barack Obama drew attention beyond his Chicago district by delivering an incisive speech in 2002 against starting that war.

As Americans vote today, to what extent are these elections about US foreign policy? And how will the outcome affect it?

Inflation and the economy might top voters’ minds, but Foreign Policy’s Emma Ashford reminds us those are foreign-policy issues too, albeit indirectly. President Donald Trump’s war with Iran has made fuel and fertilizer more expensive, raising the cost of living.

Nonetheless, Ashford writes: “[T]he Iran war has been a hot-button topic for voters because Trump himself made it one. One of his primary campaign pledges to voters in 2024 was that there would be ‘no new wars.’ He also committed to improving the cost of living … The war with Iran is an open and clear betrayal of these promises.” If Democrats win big, Ashford writes, that might signify a public rejection of the Iran war, but it would also indicate more general displeasure with “a president who promised one thing and did another.”

The midtermresults could weigh on Trump’s foreign-policy apparatus itself, Bloomberg columnist Andreas Kluth argues. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has been the most outspoken champion of Trump’s war with Iran. As a “cartoon” of Trump’s aggressive foreign policies, Kluth writes, Hegseth could be cast aside by Trump if Democrats win big; at the very least, a Democrat-majority Congress would likely call him to testify. The same could go for envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner.

Different world regions could experience different effects, depending on how Americans vote. Nikkei Asia Editor-in-Chief Akito Tanaka writes: “If the midterm results strengthen the backlash against data center construction in the U.S., major technology companies in Taiwan and South Korea [seeking to build data centers in the US or supply the technological hardware] could feel the consequences, potentially weighing on earnings and share prices.” Then again, even as the Iran war looms large over these midterms, Brian Katulis of the DC-based, Gulf-backed Middle East Institute argues the results might not change US policy in the Middle East. The expanded authority of the executive branch means Congress will have a harder time wresting control of US foreign policy, even if Democrats win majorities, Katulis writes. At the same time, Democrats “have not been laser focused on offering an alternative to the Iran war in the way that they were about the Iraq war in 2006,” and domestic issues will probably end up overshadowing foreign policy. That all makes the vote less of a clear choice between two contrasting foreign-policy visions.

A Democratic Congress might strengthen America’s touchy relationship with European allies, and the midterms could set the stage for an intra-Democratic debate over Israel policy, Célia Belin and Majda Ruge write for the European Council on Foreign Relations. But an unconstrained Trump might be the thing to watch after voting finishes.

“Even if a Democratic win allows the party to use hearings and investigations to make the president’s decision-making more transparent … Donald Trump’s approach to US foreign policy will likely be unchanged,” they write. “He has almost entirely disregarded his own Republican majority and will feel even less constrained by a Democratic one.” As for what GOP midterm losses could change, they write: “With greater leverage, Democrats could force public debates on Trump’s ‘business diplomacy’. This matters because some of the administration’s worst instincts on Europe, including hostility towards the EU and an interest in reopening commercial relations with Russia at the expense of Ukrainian territory, stem from transactional business diplomacy” featuring the possibility of economic or investment deals. Election gains would also sharpen Democrats’ debates over their own ideas and “enable the party to begin shaping the future of American foreign policy.”

Watch Fareed’s Take

Follow the link below to watch

America is a country in flux, with large swathes of voters uneasy about the future and uncertain about any political loyalties, Fareed said on Sunday’s GPS—which gives the center an opportunity to advance a clear agenda and win support now and into the future.

United States of Deadlock

Follow the link below to watch

We are in an “unusual” period in American politics where the two major parties have been evenly matched for a long time, Princeton professor of politics and public affairs Frances E. Lee told Fareed on Sunday’s GPS.

Will the Iran War Heat Up After the Midterms?

Having signaled an aversion to striking Iran before the midterms, while promising a swift conclusion to the war after Americans vote, Trump seems poised to escalate his military campaign against Iran after ballots are cast.

That’s not a certainty, of course. Really, the president’s public comments could foreshadow almost anything: a resumption of intense bombing; an immediate peace agreement (i.e., the opposite of escalation); or something more ambitious, although none of the options for more serious escalation look very appealing. All that said, commentators—and Gulf Arab states—are bracing for impact.

“While Mr. Trump has said he would not attack [Iran] before the midterm elections, he has hinted that all bets are off after that,” writes The New York Times’ Vivian Nereim. “As a result, the elections have become a grim marker of time for people across the Gulf Arab states … [where residents] know that before the midterms are over, they can reasonably expect their lives to continue as normal. … After then, nobody knows what will happen.”

“While there have been renewed diplomatic efforts to reach a [US–Iran peace] deal over the past week, the terms Iran has put on the table—including lifting all U.S. sanctions—would be difficult for the United States to accept,” Foreign Policy’s John Haltiwanger writes. “Meanwhile, Trump has offered mixed signals on what he plans to do, indicating that he hasn’t yet made a decision. Over the weekend, Trump said the war could be over soon, but he also said that the U.S. still had to ‘take care’ of one or two nuclear sites and would have to give Iran ‘the hammer’ at some point.” The US military has been spending down its stocks of interceptor missiles, which “raises questions about … readiness and capabilities if Trump chooses to return to war post-election.”

“As always with Trump, it is impossible to know what is bluster and what is serious,” Richard Haass wrote recently in his Substack newsletter Home & Away. “But it is quite possible he will want to strike, either in an effort to strengthen his hand heading into negotiations or to be able to declare ‘mission accomplished’ and walk away. Meanwhile, caretaker Israeli Prime Minister Bibi Netanyahu would like nothing more … since he would likely see a November surprise as improving his chances of cobbling together a new governing coalition or of making a stronger showing in a new election … The problem with all this, of course, is that Iran gets a say. It is already unclear what any new U.S. use of force against Iran would accomplish. Neither regime change nor capitulation is in the cards. Then there is the near certainty that Iran would retaliate against the energy infrastructure or airports or capitals of neighboring countries. … In short, we would be back where we are now, and where we have been for eight months.”

Have Corruption Politics Spread to US?

“Do you think that the Trump administration has a corruption problem?” CBS’ Ross Douthat asked Michigan GOP Senate candidate Mike Rogers on Sunday’s episode of “60 Minutes.” Rogers’ response: “Well, I can tell you this: I’m an FBI agent by training. … So I’m gonna act as a United States senator with the best interest of Michigan, I can tell you that right now.”

It was a telling non-answer from Rogers, a former chairman of the House Intelligence Committee. Some observers expect concerns about government corruption will weigh on today’s results. For some of the most powerful people in Trump’s America, private business abounds. Trump has vastly enlarged his net worth while in office. A cryptocurrency venture affiliated with his sons and the Witkoff family took in massive investments from the UAE, and a Jared Kushner-affiliated resort investment project has destabilized the politics of Albania. (Kushner has argued it’s a net positive to mix private business with his unofficial government work, saying the relationships he develops in business can help US taxpayers.) Trump’s two eldest sons are working on a mining deal in Kazakhstan. The list goes on.

“Little wonder, then, that Democrats have jumped on the issue,” Ruth Marcus writes for The New Yorker. “According to the tracking firm AdImpact, Democrats have spent a hundred and eighty-two million dollars this year on corruption-focussed advertising in House and Senate races—far more than Republicans have and more than Democrats did during the previous four elections combined.”

If Democrats do make gains in today’s elections, and corruption concerns play a role in that, the US will have followed a global trend, The Atlantic’s Isaac Stanley-Becker writes. Anger at perceived corruption has driven youth protests around the world. In Hungary and Serbia, it appeared to play a role in ousting long-tenured leaders. “If MAGA meets the same fate in the midterms as other entrenched right-wing parties have around the world, it would suggest that history is moving in a new, and unpredictable, direction,” Stanley-Becker writes, adding: “It’s not hard to understand why the ostentatious behavior of political elites would be more galling during a cost-of-living crisis.”

News Quiz: The Answer Is …

Question: The leader of which Gulf Arab country visited China this week? Answer: Oman

Al-Monitor’s Beatrice Farhat reports: “Omani Sultan Haitham bin Tarik Al Said concluded a landmark visit to Beijing on Monday where he met with Chinese President Xi Jinping. The visit is the first by an Omani head of state since the two countries established diplomatic ties in 1978. … Oman, a longtime US partner, appears to be seeking to diversify its international relations … Trump threatened to bomb Oman twice, in May and August, over its talks with Iran. Oman has long acted as a mediator between Washington and Tehran and facilitated negotiations over Iran’s nuclear program in the run-up to the war that erupted on Feb. 28.”

POLITICO Forecast

NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 22:51

What happens next

Global power is in flux. Your daily guide to what comes next. By JACK BLANCHARD Welcome to POLITICO Forecast. This is Jack Blanchard, dosing up on caf

Global power is in flux. Your daily guide to what comes next.

By JACK BLANCHARD

Send tips here | View in your browser

Welcome to POLITICO Forecast. This is Jack Blanchard, dosing up on caffeine for the long night ahead. An election is “a moral horror,” the playwright George Bernard Shaw wrote in 1921, “a mud bath for every soul concerned in it.” Your author has covered quite a few, and is in no place to argue.

But look beyond the posturing and chicanery; the false accusations and the bad faith arguments; the endless TV ads and the endless Dan Sullivans on the ballots. The sense of national renewal after a big election can be truly invigorating. And watching it all play out is a whole lot of fun. Enjoy the night.

5 THINGS THAT MATTERED TODAY

America went to the polls for midterm Congressional elections, with results due later tonight.

U.S. President Donald Trump said he will end America’s $4 billion-a-year military support for Israel, stating: “They don’t need it.”

King Charles III told a summit of Commonwealth nations that transatlantic slavery was a “shameful chapter of history” that “fills my heart with horror” — but stopped short of a formal apology.

Russia confirmed three-way talks between Trump, Russia’s Vladimir Putin and China’s Xi Jinping could happen at a summit in Shenzhen later this month.

The U.S. carried out air strikes on Iranian targets in the Strait of Hormuz, raising fresh questions about a resumption of military hostilities.

THE NEXT NEWS CYCLE

U.S. President Donald Trump delivers remarks during a campaign rally on Oct. 30, 2026 in Daytona Beach, Florida. | Andrew Harnik/Getty Images

GAME ON: Americans are casting their votes right now in what President Trump has called the most important midterm election in his lifetime. NFL stars are voting. American billionaires are voting. Even NASA astronauts are voting. Results will start rolling in over the next few hours.

Pre-election predictions are clearly a fool’s errand. The detail of what plays out across America tonight will mainly come down to relative turnout among different voter groups, something pollsters find notoriously hard to predict.

But we already know plenty about what happens next. Today we have 11 things for you to brace for once the results start rolling in.

The angry reaction from the White House. Trump is hardly known for his grace in defeat, and cries of voter fraud seem likely if Republican losses are significant. How hard the president pushes the “rigged election” angle will likely depend on the scale of those losses, and on how close the U.S. Senate proves to be. Most of what the president says will be noise; some may yet prove consequential.

But the policy response should be much more interesting. Any political leader worth their salt would be sitting on a post-election plan to seize back control of the narrative and show their party, their supporters and the country who’s (still) boss. That could mean a flurry of executive orders. It could mean doubling-down on tariffs.

Or it could mean Iran. If there’s one issue certain to return to the fore after the midterms it’s Trump’s Middle East war, with the president having paused plans for fresh military strikes as the election loomed. Once voting is over — and with Trump never needing to face the electorate again — all bets are off. Much of the Middle East is braced for further bloodshed. It could come any day.

The Republican blame game will be brutal. Trump will point fingers at Senate Republicans; at failed GOP candidates; at current and former advisers. And this could have consequences, given big-name personnel changes frequently follow midterm elections. Hill Republicans will in turn blame Trump and his war, though those who still have jobs will mostly be too cowardly to say so. But the anonymous briefings should be quite something.

The 2028 presidential primaries will start immediately — and that means a Democratic civil war playing out across traditional and social media. Expect to see plenty of 2028 wannabes on your TV screens these next few days, and to hear progressives and moderates alike explaining why this result or that result exactly proves their argument. This will be, as POLITICO’s John Harris memorably put it this morning, the “told-you-so” primary. That conversation will dominate the next 18 months of Dem politics.

A Republican power struggle will also begin, albeit deeper below the surface. Get ready for the hot takes on what these results mean for JD Vance, for Marco Rubio, for Ted Cruz, and for the future of the MAGA movement in 2028. And get ready too for an immediate battle to succeed Speaker Mike Johnson as the top House Republican, if Dems take control of the lower chamber.

And yes — divided government is almost certainly coming back to Washington. If Democrats can’t even take the House in this climate, with the smallest of majorities to overcome and every historical trend and political wind at their backs, they might as well pack up and go home.

That means more legislative deadlock next year. It means Dem legislation as political theater, set up for Senate Republicans or Trump himself to knock down. It means budget standoffs, constitutional tug-of-war, angry confrontations between the Hill and the White House over healthcare spending, trade tariffs and Iran. It means even more Washington noise, and even less Washington action.

But it also means back-room deals. Trump himself said this weekend that he will be “Mr. Veto” if Democrats take back both chambers, but also that he’d expect to cut deals with Dem leadership in the end. Senior Dems are already preparing for that eventuality. Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer has been here before, after all.

More than anything, it means inquiries. Lots and lots of inquiries. Democratic control of one or both sets of Congressional committees will be put to immediate use, and we’ll see a flurry of investigations into Trump and his family. Watch out too for investigations into Elon Musk and DOGE; into Trump’s tariff powers; and on relations with Russia and overseas allies.

And impeachment is going to be a live Democratic conversation, whether of Trump himself or of allies like Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. Democratic leadership was badly burned by the experiences of 2019 and 2021, and will resist all efforts to go nuclear on Trump again. But the pressure from the Democratic base will be intense — and who knows what Trump might do in his final two years to provoke fresh outrage? There will be nothing calm or inconsequential about the period ahead.

FUTURE INTELLIGENCE

THE BLACK DEATH: Which has been the world’s deadliest shipping route in 2026?

Not, as you might have expected, the Strait of Hormuz — though there’s eight weeks of the year still to run. Look instead toward the Black Sea, at the northeastern tip of the Mediterranean, where Russia and Ukraine have relentlessly traded blows since the summer.

Throughout the past few months Ukrainian drones have rained down on Russia oil and grain carriers. Russia has in turn bombarded merchant ships carrying Ukrainian grain toward African ports in an effort to choke off a key revenue stream. Hundreds of ships have been hit; dozens of sailors killed.

“The conflict is expanding because the Russians are trying to hit all the ships that contain Ukrainian grain,” POLITICO’s shipping reporter Martina Sapio told Forecast. “The ships they attack are normal commercial ships. Most of them, I think, have been Turkish — but the interesting thing is that because most of the seafarers that work on these ships are not European citizens, they come from India and the Philippines and other parts of Asia — there hasn’t yet been a victim who’s a citizen of a NATO country.”

That threshold could be crossed at any time, however. And NATO is anyway being sucked into the conflict, given many of the Russian attacks have been carried out in Black Sea waters of NATO countries like Bulgaria and Romania.

**Every political capital. One destination. The new POLITICO app brings our reporting from 14 power centers together in one place, with every newsletter you follow, every podcast we make and breaking alerts on the stories that matter to your work. Search nearly twenty years of our journalism in seconds. And every morning, the stories your day turns on are waiting the moment you open it. It's free to download, and Pro subscribers can log in for the people pages and their full Pro coverage. Get the new app.**

That in turn raises fears of a dangerous escalation. Both governments are furious and want support from NATO allies. But as with Russia’s wave of hybrid attacks on Europe, the Black Sea attacks are deemed below the threshold of NATO’s Article 5 treaty requirements, which — in theory — would trigger an all-alliance military response against Moscow.

“Of course it’s not the same as a direct attack on Romania,” Martina says. “But under international law, these maritime zones are part of the territory of the country.”

Discussions are now underway at NATO level about offering better military protection for Europe’s Black Sea ports, and about providing safer routes for merchant vessels across the waterway. Not for many years has the global international shipping trade been so fraught with peril.

QUOTE OF THE DAY

“The idea that we can build anything meaningful without owning the foundation of it, which are the reasoning models, the brains that can operate the factories, that can help design better systems … is, I think, a complete trap.”

— Arthur Mensch, head of Europe’s biggest AI firm Mistral, tells POLITICO why the continent urgently needs its own compute capacity and advanced AI models.

HARD POWER: ISRAEL’S ELECTION DEFENDER

Central Elections Committee chairman Justice Noam Sohlberg speaks to the media on Oct. 14, 2026 in Shoham, Israel. | Amir Levy/Getty Images

Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu has been claiming voter fraud after failing to secure a majority in last week’s Israeli elections. Israel’s elections chief is having none of it.

The Israeli election results will be formally presented to President Isaac Herzog tomorrow after election officials rejected Netanyahu’s claim the election was “stolen” by Arab parties. “Even if the suspicions are substantiated,” Central Elections Committee chairman Justice Noam Sohlberg said, “this does not detract from the integrity of the election process — or the election results as a whole.”

The process of forming a new government thus continues, although at present there’s no clear way out of the deadlock.

Tomorrow marks the start of a short formal consultation period before Herzog asks one of the party leaders to try to form a government. Opposition leaders have lined up behind former IDF chief Gadi Eisenkot as prime minister, though neither his coalition nor Netanyahu’s have the numbers for a majority without the support of Arab parties, which both have previously ruled out.

SOFT POWER: D.C. PARTY NIGHT

Who said politics is not a spectator sport?

Washington will be teeming with private and public election watch parties tonight, with bars and restaurants already filling up with political operatives, officials, lobbyists and Hill staffers nervously awaiting the midterm returns.

There are plenty of options to choose from, if you’re so inclined. You’ll find one crowd at the public transit-themed Metrobar, where Democratic organizers and lobbyists are throwing a “Bicameral Blackout” watch party. Others are at the work-dinner hotspot Teddy & The Bully Bar, which features its own Teddy Roosevelt impersonator.

One popular Capitol Hill pub will feature a slew of state-themed drinks, including Ohio’s “Buckeye Bloodbath” and Texas’ “The Dead Man’s Draw” — heralding two states that will be home to high-stakes Senate races today.

The stakes are even higher for D.C.’s waterfowl population. Olivia Gray — the five-pound duck that animal rights advocates have adopted as their mascot in the city’s battle over banning foie gras — will make an appearance at a campaign drinks party at another local tavern.

WEDNESDAY’S AGENDA

Final results expected in the U.S. midterm elections … give or take Alaska.

Commonwealth leaders including Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney and British Prime Minister Andy Burnham hold a second day of talks in Antigua.

Consultation period begins in Israel to form a new government.

Uber releases Q3 financial results.

NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 21:44

Elon Is Doing It Again: SpaceX Will Be The World’s Biggest AI Cloud

Making 13GW in 1.5 Years A Reality

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

Elon Is Doing It Again: SpaceX Will Be The World’s Biggest AI Cloud

Making 13GW in 1.5 Years A Reality

Reyk Knuhtsen, Jeremie Eliahou Ontiveros, and Dylan Patel

Nov 3

Preview

READ IN APP

We wrote at length about how SpaceX’s plans for 10GW in 2027 was feasible, paving the way for +300b+ ARR. We received a lot of pushback from folks calling it impossible. Now, just two months later, we’re already seeing 3.6GW under construction across multiple sites, both existing and new. SpaceX has even revised its estimate up to ~13GW in 2027. This would be the largest amount of datacenter capacity ever built by any one company in that timeframe, roughly equivalent to all of Google’s datacenter capacity from 1999-2026 in just 1.5 years. We think it’s possible and have already identified >9GW of capacity.

We’ve since written multiple update notes to our Datacenter Industry Model subscribers showing off all the sites that can make this possible. We now believe that SpaceX may be the fastest datacenter builder in the world, even more so than Chinese peers. Watch how Colossus 2 adds nearly two Stargate Abilenes (2GW) in just under one year!

These sites now build so fast that the power supply is often identified mid-flight. Looking at our Energy Model, we see that power under Elon’s management (>8GW) is less than our total datacenter power coming online (>9GW).

Datacenter Industry Model subscribers knew all of this before anyone else. On June 23, we sent our customers information on the infamous Elon “10GW in 2027” statement on SpaceX’s first earnings call by six weeks, pointing out a 5-10GW incremental order for 2027 was floating around the supply chain and that a new building was already under construction at Colossus 2, now publicly known as “MiniHard.” On September 14, before a single datahall went vertical, we identified >2GW of fresh sites, both at Colossus 2 and SpaceX’s Starbase campus.

Starbase, Texas, October 29th, 2026; Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model

The Elon locations have a pioneering track record with us. We called Colossus 2 the first gigawatt datacenter in the world back in September 2025, when onsite gas was called a science experiment. We showed exactly how xAI bypassed the grid (and permitting!) with truck-mounted turbines, why the behind-the-meter playbook was going to spread to 40GW+ of behind-the-meter datacenters by 2028, and how the Anthropic and Google deals turned SpaceX into the most profitable neocloud on earth. Now, Colossus 2 could soon house the largest BESS deployment in the world, with 5.9GWh already being installed, and more to come.

This report unveils our analysis of SpaceX’s “proprietary plan.” From repurposing Starship’s rocket building and launching campuses into datacenter sites, to the gargantuan BESS deployment, buying out homeowners to house construction workers, and relocating mobile turbines, this report covers it all.

For example, did you know TeraFab, Elon’s 100M sqft, 10GW+ chip fab project in Texas, will likely also have a datacenter? See the clip below of SpaceX representatives talking to Grimes County officials.

Source: Local Filings

Keep reading to find out why we think the TeraFab datacenter will be for third party renting!

Upgrade to paid

And beyond the paywall, we dive deeper into the likely SpaceX power solutions, another site that may house a datacenter, and potential pricing for future SpaceX contracts, build off our proprietary SpaceX Funding Model as part of our Capital, Compute and Markets model.

Fastest Build Times In The World

What does building two Stargate Abilenes in one year look like? Let’s talk about the Colossus expansions, where Elon is already trying to buy hundreds of nearby homes to both soothe noise complaints and provide construction worker housing. We’ve already spoken about MiniHard in Memphis finishing construction in just ~5 months, but the newly purchased “Gun Range” plot just south of the state line in Mississippi will take ~4 months!

“Gun Range,” Colossus 2, October 28th, 2026; Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model

To our knowledge, this would be the fastest greenfield datacenter ever built at scale. While most are fighting for 8-12-month build times, SpaceX has cut them in half.

Looking closer at Colossus 2, we see SpaceX trying to break its own record again. SpaceX’s first retrofit at Colossus 1 took 122 days for 150MW, and Colossus 2 took just 91 days for 210MW. Now, SpaceX has taken the building (codenamed Project SPAM) directly in the middle of Colossus 2, and targets December to bring its first tranches online. With a construction start date of October 5, 2026, this could be shorter than the 91-day record, which itself broke the 122-day record; both are held by SpaceX.

“Project SPAM,” Colossus 2, Mississippi, October 28th, 2026; Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model; Note the Megapacks and Chillers already being deployed!

Colossus expansions were to be expected, but repurposing sites from Elon’s other ventures was not. For example, many are familiar with Elon’s Starbase in Texas, traditionally used for rocket building and launching. However, as flagged two months ago to Datacenter Industry Model subscribers, this site will now house two datacenters too!

The site went from simply clearing ground at the end of August to full vertical construction by the end of September, and we now believe it will actively fit out datahalls in the coming weeks.

Starbase, Texas, October 29th, 2026; Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model

At this size, and with the knowledge of a 2027 timeline (meaning Vera Rubins), this site could be well over 1GW! Looking at the datacenter on the right, you can see that the steel mainframe is being built, while the roofing is actively following behind it. And on the left, you can see the transition from dirt to full preconstruction. These are multiple construction processes performed simultaneously, a visual representation of speed.

Lastly, we have medium-high confidence that TeraFab will have a datacenter too. That’s right, the 10GW, 100M sq.ft chip fab venture from SpaceX will likely have a datacenter. We flagged this possibility ~3 months ago, and again 1.5 months, to Datacenter Industry Model subscribers, solely by examining preconstruction work and filings.

Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model

The project actually initially started as a datacenter and then quietly pivoted to TeraFab.

Source: Local Filings

We showed the clip at the beginning (August 2026) of the site housing compute; furthermore, the representatives do not confirm that the datacenter will be for SpaceX’s use only, leaving room for third parties to rent the capacity too.

Source: Local Filings

The main remaining question is whether the current work on the ground is for one or more datacenters or for supporting facilities for TeraFab (like onsite gas, Central Utility Plant, etc.). We lean toward a datacenter for now, but suspect the vertical construction is moving slowly to ensure their JETI agreements/permits are fully compliant; otherwise, they may be subject to the TCEQ pauses or other regulatory scrutiny. Preconstruction work is so early that these pads can be pivoted a number of ways if datacenter work is not granted.

TeraFab, October 2026; Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model

How Are They Building So Fast?

One thing often overlooked is that the Elon companies are some of the best hardware companies in the world. We mentioned in our previous newsletter that their labor can run at 3k workers per GW thanks to their top-tier engineers. However, we also believe that Tesla is manufacturing modular MEP equipment, allowing SpaceX to deploy so rapidly.

If SpaceX and Tesla can manage their global supply chains appropriately, their partnership can easily construct GWs with far fewer constraints than others. This is like Crusoe manufacturing their own Switchgear to skip lead times, but on a massive scale. Tesla will likely become more than a BESS merchant to SpaceX soon.

Largest USA BESS Deployment, Turbines, and A Grid Connection?

Now that we’ve walked through GWs worth of sites, how will they power them? So far, we track 8GW+ of likely behind-the-meter capacity for SpaceX. This includes deals such as FTAI, Solaris, and others that only our Energy Model subscribers get access to. We have discussed how SpaceX decides the power on the fly, and one amazing example is the new Starbase, Texas site, where APRs are being deployed just one month after vertical construction started!

Starbase, Texas, October 29, 2026; Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model

The site may use >1GW of APRs, but judging by building size, this will not be enough. And looking at the (two!) pipelines underway, the site will likely have multiple GWs of power, and more turbines on the way. According to a TikTok with the Brownsville mayor, the site is building a fiber optic channel through the Port of Brownsville, as well as looking at leasing waterfront space (potentially for its own port). At such a massive scale, we suspect most of the temporary turbines from Colossus 2 will be relocated here.

Next, let’s move on to Colossus 2, where the opposite is happening: a grid connection. According to TVA (the region’s utility), Macrohard (700MW) and MiniHard (450MW) have both requested service for their facilities. For now, we assume this grid connection services Macrohard and MiniHard in full.

Source: Local Filings

We’ve already seen SpaceX building out the 500kV gas-insulated substation (GIS) to the left of MiniHard, which is scheduled to finish construction by December 31, 2026, and connect to the Freeport substation.

SpaceX GIS Substation, October, 2026; Source: SemiAnalysis Datacenter Industry Model

This comes at the perfect time, as the turbines at the Southaven power plant must be shut down. The site can legally operate 2.7GW nameplate right now, but only 1.24GW nameplate by July 2027, due to the binding requirement signed July 30, 2026.

But the eagle-eyed reader may notice: after subtracting the ~1.2GW grid connection, we’re left with 1.3GW of compute… and 1.24GW of nameplate power generation… Recall, nameplate generation still takes a haircut on efficiency and derate, and IT MW doesn’t represent the full facility load. There could be a gap upwards of 300MW. How does this get solved?

We think there are a few ways, and the Energy Model team will explore this power system in depth soon. But first: we think SpaceX has built the largest BESS field in the USA for a reason. With the BESS nearing 6GWh, we think the site will orchestrate gas and BESS to increase baseload, while the turbines cycle through charging them. If the datacenters are designed right (and our IT MW assumptions are correct), this could work. Given some clusters are customer-facing while others are for internal training, there could be an SLA uptime tiering to increase total capacity.

We suspect that with an ultralow PUE of ~1.10-1.15, variance in datacenter types (inference vs training, customer-facing vs internal usage), rack designs using less power (Project SPAM using half the fiber of others), and the oversized BESS, the project may still be able to cover a ~300MW shortfall.

Beyond the paywall, we will further discuss the power situation, and how we think SpaceX economics could play out...

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

The progressive congresswoman is in prime position for a 2028 presidential run if she wants it.

Good afternoon, and welcome back to Press Pass. Election Day is finally here, but we’re just getting started. Regardless of the outcomes in the House and Senate, there will be a whole set of new challenges in the coming days, weeks, and months. I know you want to stay in the loop about what’s going on behind the scenes and at The Bulwark, and we’re committed to making sure you do. That’s the power of a truly independent media. But we can’t do it without the generous support of our Bulwark+ members. We’d love to have you join our community if you haven’t already.

Join now

Today’s edition isn’t horse-race coverage always looking ahead to the next campaign, but it does show you the ways in which candidates help each other out while possibly gaining something in the future. To see what the final days of this election cycle portend for the future, I traveled down to AOC’s final stop on her multi-state tour before Election Day.

In addition, this election cycle brought back a familiar theme that seemed a bit lost after a decade of Donald Trump’s disregard for decency: the return of the sex scandal. Candidates in both parties have seen their chances go up in flames—or hang by a thread—because of their actions and voters’ disgust. The only difference is that the standards seem to be set differently for each party: Democrats shamefully dropped out, while GOP candidates sustained their campaigns. At least voters will have a say for the Republicans who stayed in their races despite the allegations.

Lastly, The Bulwark will be providing LIVE election coverage all night starting at 7 p.m. ET, which you can watch at this link. I’ve spiced it up for you by adding some easy recipes for patriotic cocktails while you take in the returns. All that and more, below.

(Photo illustration by Bill Kuchman/The Bulwark | Photos: Getty, Shutterstock)

The future is not set

Dumfries, Virginia

Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-N.Y.) has been campaigning furiously in the final stretch of this midterm election cycle, visiting states across the union to boost a range of candidates who hope that the charismatic congresswoman’s considerable name I.D. will help them run up the numbers against their Republican opponents. It’s usually helpful for a party star to campaign with others, especially in this attention economy, and Ocasio-Cortez is being a good team player here. But campaigning hard now also positions her well for a potential presidential bid in 2028.

It’s not unusual for a lawmaker with higher ambitions to travel as much as Ocasio-Cortez has done this year. Arizona’s Sen. Mark Kelly, a widely discussed potential presidential candidate from the other side of the Democratic spectrum, has ventured to twenty-three different states this cycle to promote candidates up and down the ballot. For all the talk of factionalism in the Democratic party, most senior Dems are doing a good job maintaining a big tent approach, and Ocasio-Cortez’s final stop bridged the gap between the Democratic Socialists of America and the centrist New Democrat Coalition.

On Friday evening, Ocasio-Cortez showed up to a crowded auditorium in Virginia’s 7th Congressional District—which, according to nonpartisan ratings, is not in much jeopardy—to boost incumbent Democratic Rep. Eugene Vindman.

“We cannot simply hope that voters will return their ballots or get to the polls,” AOC said. “We gotta bring them there. And that’s why all of us have been knocking doors. It’s why I myself have been touring the country. And this is my last stop on the swing.”

Virginia State Delegate Rozia Henson told me Ocasio-Cortez’s presence would likely juice Vindman’s turnout, while also shoring up the congresswoman’s favorability in the state for a theoretical primary campaign in two years. The DNC has scheduled Virginia to vote sixth in the 2028 Democratic presidential primaries, just before Super Tuesday.

“I think it is important [that AOC is in Virginia], because in my district, I represent Route 1 all the way up to Fort Belvoir. There was some concerns with progressive voters and how they felt with [Vindman],” Henson said. “So to have that additional voice just like AOC here, it helps shorten that narrative relating to his votes [in Congress].”

“I thought that from the perception—her being super progressive, socialist, et cetera, et cetera—and him more on the moderate side, to see that collaboration here today makes a difference,” Henson commented. Acknowledging Virginia’s newfound status as an early presidential primary state, he added, “There might be possibilities of what happens next, but we’ll see when that bridge gets here.”¹

The fact that Ocasio-Cortez is campaigning in locales home to centrist or moderate voters is a sign of the importance of progressives to the Democratic coalition, said Usamah Andrabi, communications director for Justice Democrats, a leftist organizing group.

“I think the left and progressives are leading this party forward with bold and visionary ideas that voters actually want to see us fight for,” Andrabi told me. “It makes sense that more establishment-aligned candidates are trying to align themselves with those campaigns, candidates, and issues, because they are simply what the people are demanding.”

Part of being in a broad coalition is making a heads-I-win-tails-you-lose bargain with the other factions. If Democrats underperform today, progressives will blame moderates for failing to deliver the radical change that people want, while moderates will blame progressives for poisoning the party’s brand with more moderate voters. If Democrats have lots to celebrate, the credit will shift in equal and opposite directions. And in that case, you can expect to see more candidates like Ocasio-Cortez barnstorming these purple-ish districts in the future.

“We cannot simply exist where our base already exists, right?” Andrabi added. “We’ve had a really successful cycle on the left, and now we have an opportunity to continue growing and organizing working-class communities in these purple and red districts in the South and across the country, because that is the future of building power for working-class communities and for the left.”

Maybe so, but during Friday’s event, Ocasio-Cortez wasn’t spiking the ball for the party’s progressive wing, and actually emphasized the need for a broader appeal and even drew comparisons between herself and Abigail Spanberger, Virginia’s more centrist governor.

“In 2018, your great Governor Abigail Spanberger flipped Virginia’s 7th district. Governor Spanberger and I were elected in the same class, and I had the absolute honor of serving with her,” she said. “It is such a testament to the hard work of your community and everything you did to deliver that victory on a razor-thin margin.”

Things weren’t always so collegial, however. During their time in Congress, Spanberger was part of a bloc of early-career House Democrats who distanced themselves from Ocasio-Cortez and the so-called “Squad” composed of staunch progressives.

After the 2020 elections, Spanberger continued to deride politicians who brand themselves as far-left, an apparent shot at Ocasio-Cortez and her allies in Congress.

“We need to not ever use the word ‘socialist’ or ‘socialism’ ever again,” Spanberger said in 2020. “We lost good members because of that.”

Five years later, though, Ocasio-Cortez and Spanberger found common ground. AOC sent a fundraising appeal for Spanberger’s 2025 gubernatorial campaign, soliciting her large and powerful base to support Spanberger, whom she called “a brawler for the working class.”

Join now

Virginia is a funny place for a high-profile Dem to be campaigning this late in the game—but maybe not for someone who travels as much as AOC does. Ocasio-Cortez released a campaign memo Tuesday, highlighting her campaign stops in pivotal primary states, fundraising prowess, and heavyweight status as a coveted endorser for candidates across the country.

“In the coming days and weeks, much will be discussed about the future of the Democratic Party and 2028. You may be asked whether you support her running for President or for Senate in New York or staying in the House of Representatives,” the memo read. “We’d recommend you rely on the details of this memo and answer that Rep. Ocasio-Cortez will continue to do what she’s always done: look for ways to do the most good for the most people. But, over the course of the last two years, she has proven herself to be one of the most effective voices in the country for America’s working class.”

With the exception of former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg (who travels more than virtually everyone else in the potential 2028 field) and nearby Maryland Gov. Wes Moore, few outside Dems have stumped in the Old Dominion, primarily because Democrats feel confident about their prospects in the state and their exertions are needed elsewhere. The Northern Virginia suburbs were hit hard by the Trump administration’s DOGE agenda, turning some of the state’s metropolitan areas sharply against Republicans—a shift felt in the state’s off-year elections in 2025 in which Democrats significantly overperformed. Polls indicate Virginia is almost certainly poised to continue on that blue trend today.

Looking forward to 2028, Virginia has become especially important for prospective Democratic presidential candidates. Not only is it now an early primary state, but it represents a useful cross-section of the new Democratic coalition: highly educated voters in the sprawling D.C. suburbs, a diverse immigrant community, college students, and black voters. Added to this is the fact that Virginia is close to D.C., making it easy for Washington-based candidates to hop over and campaign while Congress is in session. Virginia will be a busy destination for anyone looking to lock up support early in the next election cycle.

There will no doubt be many Democrats making trips down to Virginia to announce their presidential bids in the near future. But if she decides to jump in, Ocasio-Cortez is already one step ahead.

Share

Hasta la vista, baby

This election cycle brought many surprises. Among them was the return of the sex scandal—or at least sex scandals with real consequences.

After the bipartisan effort to force the Trump administration to make good on its promise to reveal the contents of its files on Jeffrey Epstein, various candidates and public officials found themselves tethered to criminal or otherwise slimy behavior. Candidates across the country have been the target of campaign ads highlighting their ties to the many individuals named in the files, even when there’s been no confirmed wrongdoing.

Beyond that, several candidates in key races have been dogged by allegations of sexual impropriety. After allegations of sexual misconduct against Maine Democratic Senate candidate Graham Platner and California gubernatorial candidate Eric Swalwell emerged, the pressure became so great that each had no choice but to abandon their campaign.

The dynamic is different in the Republican party, where candidates marred by sex-related allegations have maintained a fighting chance, perhaps because of their party leader’s seemingly unequivocal support for accused perverts and philanderers. (And also . . . because of the party leader himself.) Voters, however, may not be as forgiving as the institutional GOP. Candidates with messy personal lives include Texas Senate candidate Ken Paxton, whose affairs and divorce have been well documented; and Rep. Lauren Boebert (R-Colo.), who was kicked out of a performance of the Beetlejuice musical for disruptive behavior that included aggressively sexual PDA with a man, denied rumors of sexual relationships with male and female staffers, and had to address her son Tyler’s child abuse conviction and the separate charges against him of producing and distributing sexually explicit material depicting a minor. On October 23, the Cook Political Report shifted Boebert’s race from “Solid Republican” to “Lean Republican.”

In a similar vein, Rep. Max Miller (R-Ohio) has stayed in his race for Ohio’s 7th Congressional District despite credible allegations of physical and domestic abuse against his ex-wife and daughter. Top Republicans, including House Speaker Mike Johnson and his potential replacement, Rep. Jim Jordan (R-Ohio), have continued to support Miller despite the allegations.

The sex scandals circling over these GOP candidates have not come without some electoral consequence, however. Paxton is at risk of becoming the first Republican to lose a U.S. Senate race in Texas since 1988, despite his and his surrogates’ efforts to paint his Democratic opponent, state Rep. James Talarico, as another kind of deviant.

There’s an obvious difference between the two parties here. The Democratic candidates embroiled in these scandals have been forced from their respective races. The Republican candidates have maintained support despite their personal issues. But voters don’t seem very keen to lend their support. We’ll know in a few days.

Leave a comment

No problemo

All right. It’s Election Day. Once you’ve voted, there’s not much more to do other than to stay vigilant and watch the returns. You can do that by tuning into The Bulwark’s livestream tonight starting at 7 p.m. ET.

But I’d like to make it a little more fun for you. So here are two cocktail recipes to keep yourself refreshed this evening with some patriotic flair.

For the blue cocktail, make a classic negroni, but replace the Campari with some blue curaçao. This is one of the easiest cocktails out there, and it’ll be a bright blue to celebrate a blue wave.

1 oz gin

1 oz sweet vermouth

1 oz blue curaçao

A dash of orange bitters

Garnish with an orange peel

For the red one, I’d recommend a Kir Royale. It’s an easy twist on a mimosa made by replacing orange juice with a splash of crème de cassis, which adds some bitter flavor turning the standard glass of sparkling white wine a bright red.

4–5 oz of champagne or prosecco

1 splash of crème de cassis

If you end up making either of these cocktails, I’d love to see the results. Send me a picture and a toast over on X or in an Instagram story. I’m @JoePerticone on both platforms.

Bridges aren’t known for traveling to specific destinations. But there will certainly be many to cross when the 2028 cycle gets started, however soon that may be.

Europe’s seat at the table

Also, in tonight’s edition: Exclusive insight into the Commission’s 2027 work programme

VIEW IN BROWSER

By Victoria Becker

Supported by

“There can be no lasting peace unless Europe is at the negotiating table,” Volodymyr Zelenskyy, the Ukrainian president, said on a surprise visit to Berlin on Monday.

He stressed that continued European support was crucial in Ukraine’s four-and-a-half-year battle against Russia, speaking alongside Friedrich Merz, the German chancellor, one of Ukraine’s strongest allies.

The visit to Germany comes ahead of US-brokered talks with Russia – the first since February – set to be held in Abu Dhabi next week. Zelenskyy hopes the talks could soon pave the way for a meeting with Vladimir Putin, the Russian president.

“Our working teams will meet, and we hope these and further meetings will pave the way for a trilateral format at the level of heads of state and government,” he said. “Europe is part of this process and of the path to peace. It is important that we share a common view of all the security challenges and of how this war must end.”

On Wednesday, Steve Witkoff, Donald Trump’s special envoy, will come to Brussels for talks with Ukraine, France, Germany, Britain, NATO and the EU. He is expected to brief on the upcoming talks in Abu Dhabi.

With Trump’s focus previously dominated by campaigning on the US mid-term elections, key European allies are hopeful that Washington will be more open to boosting political and practical support for Ukraine.

Talks with the Europeans – led by national security advisers from the ‘E3’ of France, Germany and Britain – are expected to focus on security guarantees for Ukraine if there is a peace agreement.

European negotiators stress that any question of territorial concessions made to Russia by Ukraine is a matter for Kyiv to decide, noting Zelenskyy’s past comments that a ceasefire and the current frontline can be an initial starting point for negotiations.

Supported by Cement Europe

Rebuilding Europe out of itself

Construction and demolition waste is about a third of all EU waste. Cement plants can take it back as raw material. What it needs: sorting, standards and rules that let recovered material move across borders. case studies from across Europe. Learn more here.

Round-up

EXCLUSIVE: The European Commission will refocus its attention on implementing existing legislation and slimming down the burden on national governments, rather than pumping out new laws in 2027, according to an eight-page Commission work programme set to be unveiled on Wednesday and obtained by Rapporteur. The EU executive further urges countries to ban conversion practices, floats an ‘anti-China’ deterrence plan, warns the 2030 defence readiness target is at risk and eyes withdrawing key elements of the Green Deal.

COSTS OF CLOSURE: Europe has spent €127 billion more due to higher fossil fuel prices caused by the de facto closure of the Strait of Hormuz, Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, said on Monday. Roughly a fifth of the world’s seaborne LNG and oil passed through the strait before the war on Iran. Her comments come amid fears of a re-escalation of conflict in the region.

AI RULES: AI companies may freely use copyrighted data for training models, according to a non-binding opinion of the legal advisor to the EU’s top court – but restrictions apply to using press publications in AI answers. This is the first time a discussion about how far AI labs are allowed to go in their data use has made it to the Court of Justice of the European Union.

ECB SUCCESSION: EU countries have been asked to submit their nominations for the next European Central Bank president by Friday. Christine Lagarde announced her February 2027 departure from the ECB last week, giving French President Emmanuel Macron room to influence the choice of her successor. Eurogroup ministers meet next Monday to discuss potential candidates.

LONELY SÁNCHEZ: Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez's Latin American allies will not make their way to Madrid for the 30th Ibero-American summit later this week. The absence of Mexico’s Claudia Sheinbaum and Brazil’s newly re-elected Lula is a setback for the socialist ahead of snap elections later this month.

Snapshot of the day

November 3, 2026

Sidharth Bhatia, Kalrav Joshi, Anirudh S.K.

In a shocking but hardly surprising move, the Delhi police has asked social media platform X to take down the entire accounts of the Internet Freedom Foundation, the Software Freedom Law Centre and Geo Blocked in India – not because they posted anything illegal but because they responded to a ‘legal notice’ purportedly issued to them by the Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh. The cops also asked for 37 posts to be taken down – which commented on the ‘notice’ – including one by The Wire‘s editor Seema Chishti. In its latest of two communications to X, the police has invoked ‘hate speech’, ‘defamation’, ‘safety/security’ and ‘enforcement of court order’, though it neglects to mention who issued this order or what it says.

Ironically, the purported RSS notice to the IFF, SFLC and Geo Blocked demanded that the organisations stop criticising and documenting the Modi government’s censorship orders. The IFF has issued a statement arguing that the police communications to X fall foul of the law. “A takedown request that accomplishes the demand of an anonymous private notice is not law enforcement. It is the use of state power for a private end,” it said.

Elsewhere, in the government’s ongoing business of censoring Instagram, its latest target is particularly noteworthy: the Congress’s West Bengal handle.

The pattern is becoming harder to dismiss: even terms such as “SIR”, “ECI” and “protest” appear to be enough to invite takedowns on Instagram. Government requests may be triggering automated censorship without any human review, while Meta and the Ministry of Electronics and IT remain conspicuously silent...

What Is Needed for Tokenization to Deliver

Tokenization, the representation of assets and liabilities on programmable distributed ledgers, has the potential to transform financial markets by streamlining the asset life cycle, reducing frictions, and improving transparency. Yet tokenized markets remain small and fragmented, and their growth depends on legal certainty, regulatory clarity, interoperability, and the availability of settlement assets. Policymakers should adopt a technology-neutral approach that removes unnecessary barriers while safeguarding financial stability.

Headlines & Launches

GPT-6.2 Astra appears in Codex usage ahead of possible launch (3 minute read)

GPT-6.2-Astra has started appearing in Codex usage breakdowns. The requests are likely being quietly routed to the new model as part of an A/B test or a staged rollout. A public launch could be days away. OpenAI reportedly decided to skip GPT-6.1 Astra due to concerns about deception and scope.

Claude Introduces Message Threads (2 minute read)

Anthropic released message threads in public beta, allowing agents to send only new messages instead of resending their entire conversation history. The feature reduced median time to first token by approximately 35% for requests with 500K–1M tokens, without changing token usage.

Anthropic Warns It May Be Close to Self-Improving AI (3 minute read)

Anthropic disclosed in its updated IPO filing that it may soon reach its internal definition of recursive self-improvement (RSI), where AI systems help develop increasingly capable AI models.

Deep Dives & Analysis

The Hitchhiker's Guide to Training on Compute Islands (18 minute read)

Being able to train reliably on multiple smaller clusters widens the usable training pool for frontier training. A single run could mix accelerator generations and vendors as each island runs on its own schedule. Older generations or less common chips could also participate in a frontier run. One risk with this model is that integrity and safety may become harder when models are trained across providers, as one faulty or compromised site can quietly degrade or tamper with models in ways that are harder to trace.

How to Train a Swarm (19 minute read)

Swarms are AI systems where multiple agents cooperate towards a shared goal. This post discusses a technique called collective inference-time scaling, where agents scale the amount of useful work performed in parallel and the communication needed to achieve it. It also explains how to design incentives for parallel work, and covers the general topic of designing incentives for reinforcement learning.

Lessons from building Copilot Cowork (21 minute read)

Microsoft's Copilot Cowork, an AI-driven agentic execution layer, achieved global availability in four and a half months, switching its runtime from Python to Rust while live. Key practices included writing clear specs before coding, running narrow agents with clear boundaries, and ensuring automated checks genuinely verify code changes. The transition to Rust improved efficiency and highlighted the importance of clear testing, boundary setting, and human oversight in the development process.

🧑‍💻

Engineering & Research

🆕 Docusign MCP is GA! Ask your agents to send, track, search, & analyze agreements (Sponsor)

Put your agents to work on your business-critical agreements with the Docusign MCP Server. Track agreements waiting on signatures, manage select CLM workflows, or build integrations between Docusign and your AI platform. Actions stay within user permissions, with human approval for critical steps. Learn how to get started.

GPU-accelerated Weight Teleportation (17 minute read)

GPU-accelerated Weight Teleportation optimizes RL training by improving weight syncing between trainers and samplers, reducing pause times to under 0.23 seconds. This is achieved using a co-designed compression/decompression pipeline with GPU-accelerated parallel processing, utilizing efficient codecs like LZ4 and Snappy, combined with a Zstd layer.

Harness 2: executing agent code as it streams (6 minute read)

Framer's Harness 2 significantly upgrades the agent's execution process, allowing entire workflows in one program, achieving up to 33% cost reduction and 20% faster execution. This is done by executing JavaScript as it streams in, avoiding multiple model calls and using eager execution and early cancellation to enhance efficiency. The redesign also replaces DSL with method calls for real-time updates, making task execution streamlined for both in-app and external agents, reducing tool steps by 43% and cached input tokens by 39% per message.

NVIDIA's Cosmos Framework (6 minute read)

NVIDIA introduced new Cosmos Framework capabilities for training, fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and optimizing world models for physical AI. Developers can generate synchronized video and LiDAR simulations for robotics and autonomous vehicles, then deploy models through GPU-accelerated Cosmos NIM microservices.

Miscellaneous

OpenAI is finally merging ChatGPT's Chat and Work tabs (2 minute read)

OpenAI plans to merge the Chat and Work tabs within the ChatGPT experience after an initial testing period with select users. Codex will remain a separate experience in the ChatGPT desktop app. The change will enable users to move from exploring ideas to having the chatbot complete work within the same space. Users can import files and plugins. Both ChatGPT and Codex will soon draw from the same allowance pool.

LLMs are not superhuman at reading and writing neural network weights (4 minute read)

A recent paper demonstrates GPT-6 Astra's ability to interact with neural network weights directly, surpassing human or conventional algorithm capabilities. Astra significantly improved a 0.8B Qwen LLM's mathematical skills by editing weights natively. While impressive, these capabilities reflect advanced problem-solving akin to skilled human researchers, not radically new AI capabilities.

Anthropic releases amended IPO prospectus with third-quarter financials (1 minute read)

Anthropic's amended IPO prospectus reveals a 1,200% increase in revenue to $33.24 billion for the first nine months of 2026. The company reported a net loss of $104 billion for the same period. Anthropic's commitments for compute and infrastructure total approximately $895.4 billion.

Limit what agents learn about private repos with enclaves (17 minute read)

Enclaves are a new security primitive for cross-repository GitHub Agentic Workflows that lets agents work on different repositories while limiting what they say to each other.

OpenAI Introduces LifeVal to Measure AI Performance on Everyday Tasks (4 minute read)

LifeVal is a benchmark designed to evaluate AI performance on practical everyday tasks such as budgeting, finding childcare, and accessing government services.

Elon Musk Limits Grok Bot Usage Over Rising Costs (2 minute read)

Elon Musk confirmed that Grok Bot usage limits were reduced after operating costs became unsustainably high relative to revenue.

Teaching Qwen Why (47 minute read)

The research extends Anthropic's "Teaching Claude Why" methods to new models, focusing on reducing agentic misalignment through synthetic document finetuning (SDF) and supervised finetuning (SFT) with fictional stories and ethical dilemmas.

Trump’s Downfall Started in Minneapolis

by Andrew Egger

So here we are at last. After two years of Donald Trump’s Washington—the idiocy, the cruelty, the malice, the bullying, the bigotry, the hubris, the state violence, the monomania, the cowardice—it’s finally time for the president to make the first payment on the bill. Barring a catastrophic polling error (and when has that ever happened?), Americans seem set to deliver a colossal rebuke to Trump, who stormed into office last year ready to crack heads and is now doddering out the last days of his trifecta ranking asses.

The big political killer was Iran. Trump’s disastrous Middle East war fractured his coalition and doomed the country to a year (or more!) of terrible economic vibes. A week into the war, Trump’s approval rating was sitting north of 42 percent; it’s been slumping lower ever since. Nothing makes price-conscious Americans reach for their pitchforks like spiking energy prices.

But I think that the turning point in Trump 2.0 actually came a little earlier. The thing that convinced Trump he needed to beat a retreat on a core part of his domestic agenda was his fiasco in Minneapolis.

There’s no better time than Election Day to join our pro-democracy community. We’d love to have you.

Join

It’s only been a year, but it’s already easy to forget just how big a part large-scale state violence against Democratic protesters played in Trump’s plans in 2025. Over and over, the White House launched remarkable provocations against blue cities and states, from Los Angeles to Portland to Washington, D.C. These actions weren’t just intended to cow Democratic leaders. They were intended to stoke deliberate conflict between militarized agents of the state and people on the ground—conflict that Trump and MAGA could then repackage as content for their base.

Amid Trump’s many simultaneous assaults on the norms and laws that restrain the presidency, this content served a specific rhetorical purpose. In videos of riots and clashes with protesters, Trump’s government represented the forces of order, while those who opposed him were the forces of chaos. The law, Trump’s violence argued, wasn’t some bunch of buzzkill rules dorks and eggheads used to rein in great men like him. The law was pepper spray in an angry protester’s face.

As I wrote last October:

Trump goes out of his way to start conflicts. He wildly exaggerates the horrors of daily life in blue cities. He sends the National Guard in a shock-and-awe show of force into those same places. In the protests and clashes that result, he tells them the only way they can err is by pulling their punches. You’ve got to teach these criminals, he says, the only way they understand. They spit, you hit. And “criminals” are whoever Trump says they are.

When he ordered ICE into Minneapolis, Trump was just following the natural progression of this plan. But the plan quickly met disaster. ICE’s clownishly brutal occupation of the city spurred ever-increasing organized resistance from locals, who tracked ICE vehicles and made their job consistently irritating and difficult—blowing their cover by following them with blaring horns and shrieking blowing whistles, organizing deliveries of groceries and other materials to terrified immigrants wouldn’t have to risk leaving their homes, and so on. And videos of irritated agents lashing out at annoying but law-abiding and peaceful protesters started to turn public sentiment against the occupiers—even before things went catastrophically wrong.

And then came the deaths of Renée Good and Alex Pretti. More than anything else, these two instances of shocking police violence against peaceful protesters turned the tide. Trump and Co. wanted a constant feed of videos of police administering street justice to hooligans; instead, Americans’ social platforms were suddenly full of videos of deadly violence dealt against—in Pretti’s case—a man who had literally tried to peacefully place his body between an agent of the state and the woman he was roughing up.

MAGA’s propaganda machine tried feverishly to smear these two dead Americans as domestic terrorists and would-be cop killers. But no one was buying it, and soon even they had to admit it. Not long after, the White House announced an about-face: They were heading out of Minneapolis. From now on, federal immigration enforcement would focus on the red cities and states that would cooperate with less fuss. The blue-city occupation strategy was at an end.

I don’t mean to oversell this. It isn’t as though Trump et al. have gotten significantly more careful about the violence the state wields: Others have died at the hands of DHS agents this year, including Lorenzo Salgado Araujo in Houston and Johan Sebastián Durán Guerrero in Maine this summer. And, as our Adrian Carrasquillo reported last week, Good and Pretti’s families have never gotten accountability from the officers who killed them.

But Minneapolis’s resistance still achieved something extraordinary: At a time when Trump seemed at his strongest and they seemed at their weakest, they managed to throw him off his violent agenda simply by standing up nobly as individuals and as a community against his designs. Maybe—probably?—voters will put Democrats back in power today, and Trump will never again achieve the level of unilateral state authority that he enjoyed last year. But even if they don’t—even if Republicans beat the odds and give Trump back more or less united control of Washington—Minneapolis still gives us the model: You resist with whatever you’ve got.

One Last Round with Ramaswamy

by Jim Swift

West Chester Township, Ohio

The prevailing sentiment online is that a blue wave is going to wash Republicans out of the majority in both chambers today. But out here in the Cincinnati exurbs Monday night, prognostications were a bit different.

“It is a Ramaswamy tsunami,” a man at a bar declared. He paused, then added, “and a Husted high tide.”

The prognosticator in this case was the Republican candidate for attorney general of the state of Ohio, Keith Faber. He and a small battalion of quarter-zipped Republican pols were taking turns warming up Lori’s Roadhouse for gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy, who was in town to make the closing argument of his beleaguered, often-bizarre campaign.

The spacious music venue had arranged rows of four-tops to seat around 300 people in the dim, red-lit hall. Other early attendees—staff, mostly—adhered rigidly to that GOP quarter-zip aesthetic.

I had barely settled at the open bar when a man with his phone out approached me and demanded to know: Why was I there if The Bulwark hated Vivek so much, huh? And why hadn’t I condemned Amy Acton, the Democratic candidate in the race, as an alcoholic, huh? He briefly gained a head of steam before leaving me to my drink; he had a recording to review and (maybe) post. An Ohio State Highway Patrolman stepped in to fill the spot, and he stayed there through the rest of the program, never saying a word to me.

The candidates arrived about twenty minutes behind schedule. Ramaswamy and his running mate, Senate President Rob McColley, strode past half-empties on the tables as much of the statewide Republican ticket strode behind them. The bus contingent had, you guessed it, their quarter-zips on. The group’s tidy, techy appearance belied the nastiness of the attacks on Acton many of them were about to spout, as when the candidate for treasurer—not exactly a role that requires a dogfighter—smugly told the audience: “Not one of us is going to slur our speech.”

He was referring to a video from Acton’s Thursday appearance in Toledo that her critics have used to claim she is an alcoholic. That’d be quite a secret to keep in today’s world, but plausibility aside, it became a recurring subject throughout the evening. AG candidate Faber brought it up, too, urging supporters to send it to friends and family. It was all done in service of the same basic message: Acton was unfit to lead. She was a complete sot. What’s worse—she’d gotten involved in, like, COVID stuff, man.

Vivek’s running mate attempted to instill the central point through a bit of call-and-response.

[Y]ou gotta ask your friends, family, and neighbors, “Who do you trust? Who do you trust to deliver lower property taxes?” (Audience: Vivek!) “Who do you trust to take out Medicaid waste, fraud, and abuse?” (Audience: Vivek!) “Who do you trust to end sanctuary cities in the state of Ohio?” (Audience: Vivek!) “And who do you trust to make it so that you and your family can live, work, raise a family, and prosper here, and your kids and grandkids don’t have to leave for another economic opportunity?” Let’s hear it. Who do we trust? (Audience: Vivek!)

When Ramaswamy took the mic, he promised his next visit to Butler County would be as governor.

“That’s the good news,” he said.

Then came one of the most abrupt unforced pivots I’ve witnessed at a political event: “The bad news is communism is spreading across the West.”

He took the crowd on a quick tour of looming horrors: France was burning, New York was under socialist rule, Los Angeles was soon to follow, and Minnesota was plagued by entitlements fraud and what he called an “illegal immigration infestation.”

Then he invoked Dearborn, Michigan. “Are you gonna let that come to Ohio?” he asked.

“This is not Republican versus Democrat. This is common sense versus communism.”

Common sense, in his view, means lower electric bills, an end to sanctuary cities, and becoming “the most conservative governor this state has ever seen in our history.” It means maybe-sorta abolishing property taxes altogether.

And it means calling and texting everyone in your contact list to tell them to vote for Ramaswamy, even if it requires suspending normal phone etiquette. “Tonight’s an exception. You can do it,” Vivek said.

“This is the last night of this campaign,” he told the crowd. “I’ll remember this for the rest of my life. You will, too, when we get this right tomorrow morning.” And just like that, the quarter-zip brigade retired for the night.

BRENDAN BOYLE joins CATHERINE RAMPELL to discuss what Democrats plan to do if they take back the House, from reversing Trump’s healthcare cuts and tariffs to confronting the economic fallout from his Iran war. Plus, SAM STEIN joins to break down Trump’s closing economic message and how rising costs and voters’ frustrations over the economy could shape decisions at the polls.

QUICK HITS

BOMBS AWAY?: Will Trump hit Iran again after the midterms? There’s been growing chatter that he’s considering it, and America’s regional allies are glumly bracing for impact, assuming they’ll bear the brunt of Iran’s retaliation if America starts dropping bombs again. But the president hasn’t made up his mind, the Wall Street Journal reports:

The president told Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu in a call Saturday that he wanted to first give continuing diplomatic efforts a chance to succeed, people familiar with the call said.

Netanyahu pressed for strikes immediately after the U.S. midterms, the people said. Israel’s military has prepared attack plans and on high alert, one of the people said. . . .

“The only reason why I’m not finished with Iran is I want to be nice. I want to try and save all those lives if I can,” Trump said Sunday at a rally in Iowa ahead of Tuesday’s midterm elections. “But at some point we’re going to have to give them the hammer.”

Yesterday, the Journal also reported that the United States had bombed Iran’s Qeshm Island—apparently without any relation to the short, sharp escalation Trump is mulling after the election. This all from the man who said he could win the Vietnam War in a week.

AMERICA’S SMALLEST DONOR: Trump has made a couple billion off the presidency since last year, but he doesn’t seem particularly interested in giving back to the people who helped make it possible. The Washington Sun reports that Trump didn’t contribute a dime of his own money to a single candidate, PAC, or super PAC this cycle, despite plenty of promises to do so: “I’m going to be making contributions to various campaigns and people that I think are good,” he told the Sun in September. “I’m going to spend whatever amount of money necessary to try and help us.”

Instead, he’s been treating other people’s money like his own—in open violation of federal law. Trump isn’t supposed to direct the spending of his super PAC, MAGA Inc. But he’s made no secret that the PAC’s hundreds of millions—funded by the likes of Miram Adelson, Jeff Yass, and the Winklevoss twins—are, in his words, “money that I control.”

“For months voters have watched donors to Trump’s roughly $400 million war chest land posts in the administration and receive presidential pardons, among other favorable treatment,” former FEC Chairs Trevor Potter and Ellen Weintraub wrote in a Washington Post op-ed last week. “These results, coupled with Trump’s assertion of control, fly in the face of the basic legal bargain that allows organizations such as MAGA Inc. to raise and spend funds.”

HERE COMES CHUCK: Democrats may be poised to retake power in Washington, but voters still don’t like the party much: Surveys consistently find the Democrats’ national approval rating significantly lower even than the GOP’s. But despite widespread disapproval even among their own voters, Washington Dems aren’t in any big hurry to make leadership changes. In fact, Politico reports, the mood among Senate Democrats is that, should they retake the chamber, Sen. Chuck Schumer will walk back in basically unopposed as majority leader.

If the wave predictions fizzle, things might look different:

“This isn’t last year,” said a Schumer-neutral Senate Democratic aide granted anonymity to speak about internal caucus dynamics, referring back to government funding fights with Trump that had many Democratic senators publicly fuming over how Schumer handled them.

But if Democrats are in the minority, the 75-year-old leader could find himself dealing with members who publicly break with him, questions from colleagues about whether he will retire in 2028 or even an unlikely longshot challenger, according to five people interviewed about Schumer’s standing.
