November 03, 2026 | Read online

Welcome back, Superhuman. Any AI tool can build a website these days. The Chinese lab DeepSeek just went one step further by letting you describe custom plugins that customize the platform itself. Plus, a Y Combinator-backed startup revealed an early look at its AI cell phone.

Today: Grok Bot’s finances, how to create a daily brand pulse from X with Grok Bot, and get the latest prompts and trending social posts.

TODAY IN AI

Watch how DeepSeek’s Creator Mode lets you build custom plugins. Source: DeepSeek.

1. DeepSeek launches Creator Mode for building custom plugins: The new feature lets you create your own tools and add them directly to DeepSeek Harness, allowing you to customize the tool itself. Or, as the lab puts it, “turn ideas into plugins you can use.” The demo video shows use cases ranging from simple productivity tools, like a notes app, to more advanced projects like design consoles and LLM visualizers. To help you get started, the Chinese lab posted 7 creator mode starter prompts.

2. Cyclon opens pre-orders for the beta version of its AI cell phone: Cyclon runs on its own AI operating system (OS), which lets the phone use your existing apps, build new ones on demand, and run tasks in the background. As part of its closed beta, the startup opened pre-orders for a Google Pixel 8a running Cyclon’s OS. Cyclon’s own hardware is expected in Q2 2027, pending FCC approval. See how it works.

3. Elon Musk says Grok Bot’s costs are “unsustainably high” relative to its revenue: Two weeks after calling Grok Bot the fastest-growing product he’s ever seen, Musk admitted the cost of serving the tool is unsustainable, though he gave no specifics. It’s a reminder that always-on personal agents are expensive to run, and labs may be subsidizing costs. SpaceXAI isn’t slowing down though: its data science team built a Grok Bot that handled an estimated 70 hours of work last week.

TUTORIAL

How to create a daily brand pulse from X with Grok Bot

Photo: Grok Bot

Open Grok Bot and create a new Bot dedicated to monitoring your brand on X.

Connect the Bot to X and add your brand name, product names, competitor names, executive names, and any keywords or hashtags you want tracked.

Sample Prompt: “Review X activity from the last 24 hours about [brand], [products], and [competitors]. Identify important mentions, recurring customer reactions, emerging conversations, complaints, praise, competitor activity, and relevant trends. Ignore spam and duplicate posts. Return a concise brief with: What People Are Saying, Emerging Trends, Competitor Moves, Risks, Opportunities, and links to the most important posts.”

Run the Bot once manually and remove noisy keywords, irrelevant accounts, or topics that create false positives.

Save the tested workflow as a Skill so the same monitoring and filtering rules are reused each time.

Create a Routine to run the Skill every morning and deliver the finished brand pulse on your preferred schedule.

FROM THE FRONTIER

The companies winning with AI are the ones not cutting jobs, says Palantir’s CTO. Here’s what they’re doing instead.

Made with Midjourney

Companies are feeling layoff remorse. 55% of employers that cut staff citing AI now regret those decisions, a sentiment that’s showing up across multiple studies. Palantir CTO Shyam Sankar has seen it firsthand.

In a recent Stratechery interview, Sankar says “almost all” of the customers he’s worked with who wanted to use AI to cut headcount failed to even hit that goal. The ones pulling ahead? They’re leveraging AI to take on work that was previously too expensive or time-consuming. This is allowing smaller teams to punch above their weight:

Eve, an AI platform for plaintiff law firms, says it has enabled customers to double their capacity with minimal new headcount.

Mithril Medicine, a one-person biotech, was recently acquired by Anthropic after founder Cody Tranbarger used Claude to take a rare-disease treatment from idea to promising results in under 18 months.

Cisco is giving a 27-year-old engineer unlimited AI tokens to rewrite the company’s legacy code, after the engineer pitched the project to president Jeetu Patel directly. The president’s response: “Go nuts.”

Doing more with less. This doesn’t mean the AI layoff wave is over: Microsoft just offered buyouts to 60,000 employees last week. But it’s a sign that sentiment may be starting to shift in boardrooms around the world.

Finding my thing

Nobody knows anything

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

Finding my thing

Nobody knows anything

Nov 3

READ IN APP

How do I follow up my last post…I’ve never had such a response before, I’m still working my way through every reply.

And I wasn’t actually going to post today.

Since last week I’ve been having great conversations and spent a lot of time thinking, without my agent. I’m back to actually jotting stuff down in my notes rather than agents.

Inertia is my problem. Just because this newsletter is sent tues/thurs in a specific format, it’s easier to keep that going. I feel obliged.

But really I’m trying to find something. My thing. And if I do what I’ve done, I won’t find it. I need to change it up. So from now until the end of the year, the formats will vary, the content, even the days + frequency of when I post. I’m in experimental mode. The digital equivalent of going to Thailand, slinging on those awful trousers and finding myself.

I need to post what I’m interested in. For me to continue doing this for a long time, I have to make these for me. So what am I interested in?

I’m following my nose. It’s already led me to have convos with Stripe, Granola, Zed and Raycast about how they’re building in this new world, while staying sane and nurturing a considered culture. I think there’s a ton of interesting stuff here I’ll write and build on top of. Coming soon-ish.

I said I wasn’t going to post today, but you’re reading this…so, what gives? Well in my deliberation of to post or not to post, I felt like I wanted to bring you along in my thinking. I want to bare all whilst we see where we end up.

I’ve stopped using Grok Bot for everything, except to ping me when Carlos Alcaraz is playing. I’m reading more long-form. I’m consuming less slop (and spending a little less time on X). I’m recording a voice-note every morning for a minimum of 10 minutes, and not doing anything with the transcript. I’m letting things percolate, noticing what I come back to.

Agents give you answers to everything. But if we’re ‘soul-searching’ or trying to connect the dots, an answer presented to us may not be the right path. Even if it’s a reasonable one. All I’m ‘optimising’ for here is - what do I really want to do? And noticing what I keep coming back to when I take the time to think on it.

This is not an ‘anti-ai’ stance at all. I love AI. I can do so much more than I ever could. It’s a tool though, not a person, and I use it as such. You probably need a grand total of one tool (an agent). Instead of looking at everything as an opportunity to optimise, I want to use AI for fun.

Quick bites

Try doubleAI’s Maxwell, a code execution model that speeds up software as it runs. Results: 38x faster data pipelines with no code changes.*

Patrick Collison posted about adding a feedback box to what you build as a quick way to keep iterating.

A few links to get your agents to make good UI: Stageshow for making product demos, BoardUI for great-looking components and Looseleaf for a hand-drawn feel.

I enjoyed this post on how Jakub built a personal health app for just himself. He puts out a lot of great visual work.

Nobody knows anything - ‘if you feel behind right now don’t worry you aren’t.’ Fun site, but if it’s hard to read, hit the grid icon in the top right.

Kino - edit video with your agents.

Hex - A free voice dictation app for Mac, Windows and Linux. The landing page is pretty good!

I like this… a Chrome extension that swaps clickbait thumbnails for real stills from the video.

Top NK stories from around the web

Korea launches 3,600-ton submarine, boosting naval deterrence, defense export capacity Korea Times

Measuring 89 meters in length, the 3,600-ton Seo Hui is the second vessel under the KSS-III (Dosan Ahn Changho-class) Batch-II program, following the lead ship Jang Yeong-sil (SS-087). It represents a significant upgrade over the earlier 3,000-ton Batch-I submarines (Dosan Ahn Changho, An Mu and Shin Chae-ho). Equipped with an advanced combat management system, enhanced sonar suites and vertical launch system cells capable of firing both submarine-launched ballistic missiles and cruise missiles, the Seo Hui offers expanded multitarget precision attack capabilities and improved underwater survivability.

North Korean Hackers Move $6 Billion in Stolen Binance Funds Through 12,000 Wallets Business Korea

According to blockchain analytics firm Elliptic on Nov. 2, the hackers have moved assets previously held in temporary wallets onto the Solana (SOL) network and other blockchains, marking the formal start of a full-scale second-stage laundering operation. The hackers are employing approximately 12,000 cryptocurrency wallets and conducting more than 40,000 transactions in an effort to evade detection. They are distributing the stolen funds across multiple addresses and moving them between different blockchain networks, making the funds more difficult to trace. Initially, the assets were distributed mainly through the Tron network across approximately 7,700 addresses. Since Nov. 1, transfers have also been detected on other networks, including Ethereum (ETH), Solana (SOL), NEAR and XRP.

S. Korea’s defenses may fall short against low-cost drone swarms: report Korea Herald

South Korea’s military may be ill-equipped to defend against swarms of low-cost attack drones designed to exhaust conventional air defenses, a state-run defense research institute said Tuesday. The Korea Research Institute for Defense Technology Planning and Advancement said existing counter-drone systems have clear limitations against large numbers of medium-sized attack drones, particularly if cheap uncrewed aircraft force defenders to expend far more costly interceptors.

Unification minister calls for inter-Korean cooperation on border crossings by diseased animals Joongang Ilbo

Unification Minister Chung Dong-young on Tuesday called for inter-Korean cooperation to control animal diseases that wild animals could carry across the border. “There is a need for phased cooperation in animal quarantines to control diseases, such as African swine fever, foot-and-mouth disease and bird flu,” Chung told a Cabinet meeting chaired by President Lee Jae Myung earlier in the day. “We are preparing for it,” Chung added. “It is one of the reasons why inter-Korean ties should improve.”

U.N. releases S. Korea's letter decrying N.K. mine blasts, defending border warning shots Yonhap News

The United Nations has released a letter that South Korea sent to the president of the U.N. Security Council (UNSC) to decry land mine blasts in the Demilitarized Zone (DMZ) in September and urge North Korea to apologize for the incident that left three South Korean soldiers injured.

NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 13:45

IRAN. The feeling ain’t mutual

As Washington says one thing, Tehran says another.

Donald Trump said oil was “dropping like a rock” as “people feel that there will not be a war with Iran.” JD Vance said he and Trump “feel pretty good” about a deal. The US hit Qeshm Island after Iran claimed to have downed three US aircraft.

INTELLIGENCE. Trump has claimed to be close to a deal for eight months. Oil falls each time, then quickly rebounds. If there is a silver lining, it’s that Tehran has reportedly amended its offer to allow Hormuz to reopen within two days, not seven, but the conditions for doing so are presumably the same (the US leaving the region; economic reparations; no nuclear limits). This may be better than Trump nuking Pickaxe Mountain, but it doesn’t mean it’s any more likely.

FOR BUSINESS. Iran is not incentivised to give Trump a pre-midterms gift. If it did, the price would be great. More likely, it will continue to make the US economy suffer until he capitulates or leaves office. And while Iran’s economy is also suffering, it has decades of experience. Lifelines via Russia continue. Some of its oil escapes via Pakistan. China continues to provide political cover. As Oman’s sultan visited Monday, Beijing said it would soon send an envoy.

UKRAINE. RUSSIA. At Witkoff’s end

Moscow remobilises for war and peace.

Kyiv said Moscow had used Melania Trump’s humanitarian ceasefire in southern Kherson to regroup and was plotting a “massive strike” during the midterms. Steve Witkoff said he would host trilateral peace talks in Abu Dhabi next Tuesday...

Big Tech & Startups

SpaceX is reportedly looking at buying Boost Mobile or using its cell network (2 minute read)

SpaceX has held talks with EchoStar to potentially acquire Boost Mobile outright or gain access to its cell network, which is leased from AT&T and T-Mobile. Starlink recently confirmed plans to become a major mobile carrier in the US. The deal will need approval from AT&T and T-Mobile, both of which have already turned down SpaceX for access to their cell towers. SpaceX last year acquired $2.6 billion worth of spectrum licenses from EchoStar to improve its Starlink network's 5G connections.

Coming in 2027: Apple's $1,000 home robot for FaceTime and switching on lights (2 minute read)

Apple is reportedly working on an AI-driven home robot potentially named the HomeView Ultra. It plans to launch the robotic product with a possible price tag of $1,000 or more by 2027. The earliest prototypes of the home robot first came out in 2018. The robot, which will support FaceTime calls, will be able to autonomously roam around and switch lights on whenever a person enters the room.

Science & Futuristic Technology

China publicly discloses plan for a lunar landing within three years (3 minute read)

Chinese space officials have confirmed the country's plans to land astronauts on the Moon within three years. The landings will involve a lunar-orbit rendezvous similar to what NASA did with the Apollo program. China and the US are racing to land astronauts on the lunar surface, as it would be a major geopolitical win. Both countries are also building capabilities to establish a long-term presence at the Moon's South Pole.

What Is Quantum Advantage Actually Worth? (9 minute read)

Nearly all roadmaps point to a utility-scale quantum computer being developed around 2030. Despite this, there is little confidence or clarity on how these machines will actually be useful. The consensus is now that they'll first be used for breaking cryptography. The second most concrete answer is to use them to generate simulation data for AI labs' science models. This post looks at how else quantum computing could be applied and where all the revenue will come from.

Programming, Design & Data Science

Automate a software factory that works for humans (Sponsor)

How can AI do more while engineers stay in control? Join LaunchDarkly and Ramp virtually and learn:

How they've automated their AI software factory.

Why new features like automatic flag creation, autonomous releases, and intelligent flag cleanup make it possible.

The benefits of moving control to production.

Save my seat

Why Agents Are Soon Only Going to Have 1 Tool (9 minute read)

Agents are collapsing their entire tool catalog into one 'exec' tool. This allows models to write short scripts that chain, combine, and await actions inside isolated sandboxes. Models can now reliably combine and chain dependent actions in a single pass. In the previous standard tool-calling model, agents could only perform single actions, but the exec tool allows them to perform multiple actions.

Scaling Git at OpenAI (31 minute read)

OpenAI's monorepo's size and demands increased at a staggering pace over the last year, necessitating a revamp of the AI lab's source control system. Its technical staff built Pando, a new backend for Git repositories optimized for concurrent pushes to many branches and to maximize read throughput. The team implemented the backend without interrupting any engineering work or changing how the team uses Git. Pando cut median push latency in production by nearly 5x, and it now delivers nearly a petabyte of Git data per day.

Miscellaneous

Our organizations are being DDoSed from the inside (6 minute read)

Organizations are losing attention as everyone within is doing something they think is useful, but all that work draws on the same limited capacity to understand, review, and decide. Just because work exists doesn't mean it's the right thing to do. Output is not proof of value. A working implementation only means that something can be built, not that it should be - some changes have negative value.

The value of open source (16 minute read)

People open source projects for various reasons. Some do it to build a company or product, others do it performatively, and some ship code just for the love of the game. Open source was never free to copy, and the code was never the moat. AI can remake a product, but it can't provide the rest of the value a company generates. The technology is helping maintainers ship and fix problems faster.

Quick Links

Fern Docs sites are now static, and much faster (Sponsor)

Fern Docs sites are now prebuilt as static pages. On customer sites like Cash App, average mobile Lighthouse scores rose from 60 to 98. Make your docs fast.

Prototypes are not that helpful anymore (12 minute read)

A prototype is no longer an indicator that the builder is competent.

Anthropic warns that it's very close to RSI capability, aka self-building AI (4 minute read)

Anthropic recently updated its S-1 to say it may soon meet its own internal definition of recursive self-improvement.

LLMs are not superhuman at reading and writing neural network weights (4 minute read)

GPT-6 Astra's ability to make direct weight updates is cool, but the best way to update the weights of a model is still to train them normally.

Resisting Cognitive Surrender (16 minute read)

Cognitive surrender is when someone uncritically accepts whatever agents output without actually thinking, exploring, or considering alternatives.

Chip Maker Marvell Agrees to Buy Lumentum in $80 Billion Deal (5 minute read)

Marvell designs custom AI chips and other technology that connects processors and moves data around data centers, and Lumentum has technology that uses light to move data between chips and servers.

The Coming Replication Revolution (16 minute read)

Soon, almost all researchers will engage in AI-assisted replication of their own work before submitting to a journal, which will improve the quality of research going forward.

BEYOND THE HEADLINES

Why Indonesia's toxic haze is giving its neighbours a political headache

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDksInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABsqbaXML7IY3d7liG-Q_IdUUSVzsrVh3of08NAccKJE4fbQ1Uq-bxxl5pUNc-frikowNkDm_Be7JGzrzE5kKvwiv4ipXjoiQh-zYwt0H The haze stemming from wildfires in Indonesia has drifted into Singapore and Malaysia. Credit: Reuters

For weeks, residents of maritime South East Asia have been living in a toxic smog caused by wildfires burning in Indonesia, which is triggering respiratory illnesses and headaches in the population. But it's causing a different type of headache for the region's governments - the leaders of Singapore and Malaysia are split between their anxious citizens and an irascible neighbour which does not like criticism. Can Indonesia's government be pressured to act, or will the region have to wait for the problem to blow over?

NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 12:31

The Morning: Results, please

We’re following the midterms today.

View in browser|nytimes.com

November 3, 2026

By Sam Sifton

Good morning. It’s Election Day. See when the polls close where you are.

Yesterday, we told you how today’s midterm elections could play out, based on the latest polling. This morning, let’s look at what that might mean for the Senate.

(All our politics coverage is free to read, by the way, even if you’re not a subscriber. Follow our live updates all day.)

Alex Kent/The New York Times

Majority rules

Whichever party wins, the Senate majority is likely to be narrow. Maybe neither party will have a majority!

Carl Hulse, our chief Washington correspondent, lays out the possibilities and the consequences. There are a number of them that could change how the Senate functions during the second half of President Trump’s term in office.

A slim majority empowers senators in the middle.

Democrats have 47 seats, and they’re likely to pick up more. With a majority, Democrats would make legislation impossible for the Republican leadership, but anything close to 50 could embolden Republicans who are willing to break with Trump and the party at large.

That’s Senators Lisa Murkowski of Alaska and Susan Collins of Maine (should Collins win her re-election race). A slim majority for the Republicans could also put pressure on a potential party-switcher like Senator John Fetterman, Democrat of Pennsylvania, who has quarreled with Democratic leadership but who has said that he does not intend to switch.

A 50-50 tie is bad news for JD Vance.

A split down the middle means a Republican Senate, given the vice president’s power to break deadlocks with a vote as the chamber’s “presiding officer.” But a tied Senate also leaves both parties in a situation where even a single defection could alter the outcome of a vote. And a single-member absence could make it impossible to move ahead with a bill. It would also mean that Vice President JD Vance, who is expected to be a leading Republican candidate for president in 2028, couldn’t leave Washington to campaign while the Senate is in session. He’d have to be nearby to break tie votes.

In that scenario, Democrats would likely lobby Murkowski to flip her party affiliation, though she’s unlikely to do so because she’d lose her powerful committee positions by changing sides. But it has happened before, Carl reminds. In 2001, Jim Jeffords, a Republican senator from Vermont, became an independent and started caucusing with the Democrats. That gave them a Senate majority.

Independents could scramble the picture.

There are two independents currently in the Senate: Bernie Sanders of Vermont and Angus King of Maine. Both caucus with Democrats. But at least three more are running, and they say they wouldn’t support the leadership of either party. The idea is, their independence would give them leverage over both parties.

You have to get into the way-back machine to see the last time that happened, Carl reports:

The Senate has not faced such a situation since 1952, when Senator Wayne Morse, Republican of Oregon, declared himself an independent, later moving a folding chair into the center aisle of the Senate. He was denied committee seats and ultimately joined the Democratic Party.

This could all take a while.

Challenges to election results could mean it takes time to seat new members. For example, Republicans may contest the results of the race in Alaska, where the Democrats recruited a candidate to siphon votes from the Republican incumbent. If the seat remains open during litigation, it would leave the balance of power in the Senate in question.

For how long? In 2008, it took more than eight months for Senator Al Franken, the Minnesota Democrat, to gain his seat amid a protracted recount and court battle.

When will we have results?

These charts show what time we had the answers in 2024:

Vote count data from The Associated Press. Alicia Parlapiano/The New York Times

Source: Race calls from The Associated Press. Alicia Parlapiano/The New York Times

Don’t forget to vote today!

More midterm news

The Supreme Court rejected a Trump administration request to require California to set aside 20,000 mail-in ballots that the administration claims went to noncitizens.

Trump said he was willing to issue pre-emptive pardons to aides and allies, including Elon Musk, if Democrats won back control of Congress.

See the top donors pumping $2 billion into federal races in these elections.

Top Justice Department officials have refused to acknowledge that Trump lost the 2020 election. They’re now part of an intense effort to intercede in states’ election administration.

The campaign is ending, as it began, with Democrats fighting about Israel. One feud in Michigan may be a preview of more conflict to come.

THE LATEST NEWS

Politics

The Trump administration is preparing to scale back workplace accommodations for women related to pregnancy and reproductive health.

A judge threw out charges against two former F.B.I. officials, saying the case was a rushed pre-election political prosecution ordered by the White House.

The administration is dismantling America’s scientific research machine.

Immigration

Immigration agents have arrested Ukrainian truck drivers who fled the war, Ukrainian officials said. Some remain in detention.

On TikTok, people deported by the United States are finding an audience. Watch one video.

Iran

The U.S. military struck Iran’s Qeshm Island in what American officials described as a small-scale defensive measure. They said it was a limited action, with no immediate plans to escalate.

Iran said it was prepared to reopen the Strait of Hormuz soon if the United States agreed to demands including the lifting of sanctions and the release of billions of dollars in frozen Iranian assets.

Plane Crash

One of the pilots of a private jet that crashed in Florida, killing all nine people on board, was a 20-year-old who received her pilot’s license at 17, her mother said in an interview. Read more about the pilots and passengers who died.

Investigators believe ice and severe weather were key factors in the crash. (A story about the crash was the most clicked in The Morning yesterday.)

Other Big Stories

Russia, which has long reviled the prime minister of Poland for staunchly supporting Ukraine, is backing hard-right Polish protesters.

El Niño has triggered devastating flash floods across the Southwest.

A child in upstate New York died from measles, the state’s first death from the disease in 30 years.

William Nordhaus, the Nobel-winning economist who pioneered the study of climate change’s economic impact, has died at 85.

THE MORNING QUIZ

The New York Times

This question comes from a recent edition of the newsletter. Click an answer to see if you’re right. (The link will be free.)

Pinyo Pukpinyo, a fireman in Bangkok, catches about 20 snakes a day when people call the force to report sightings of them. What does he do with his quarry?

He wok-fries the meat and sells it at a hawker stand.

He trains the snakes to perform at children’s birthday parties.

He spends $300 of his own money every month to feed them.

He wears them on walks around the city’s parks.

OPINIONS

Insurance premiums are going up, but a major market overhaul might take years. A Yale economist recommends a few changes that can help people today.

As voters head to the polls today, America is in its fourth great crisis, Ken Burns writes.

RECIPE OF THE DAY

Armando Rafael for The New York Times

A version of this soup recipe — navy beans simmered with ham hocks, butter and onion — has been served in the dining room of the United States Senate for more than 100 years. As voters across the country choose who will get a chance to order Senate Bean Soup in Washington, make it yourself at home.

RISING SPEED

IShowSpeed in Las Vegas. Malike Sidibe for The New York Times

Jon Caramanica, one of our pop critics, spent the last 18 months following IShowSpeed, real name Darren Watkins Jr., one of the most influential livestreamers in the world, who has 62 million subscribers on YouTube.

Folks aren’t watching Speed play video games or hang out at home. Each of his streams, Jon writes, “is a one-man play, with improvisational work contributed by umpteen secondary characters who flock from their homes to see him, sometimes by the thousands, or watch from home, sometimes up to a million at a time.”

And he’s everywhere, traveling the world to find adventures and spread chaos while livestreaming on YouTube 24 hours a day. Jon told me he thinks Speed is the person most likely to change the shape of media in the next decade. Get to know the guy.

More on culture

The John F. Kennedy Center for the Performing Arts announced this year’s recipients of the Kennedy Center Honors yesterday. They include Paul Anka, Andy Garcia, David Mamet, Barbara Mandrell and David Foster. The ceremony won’t be at the center.

Late-night hosts covered Trump’s speech at the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool.

Buy or borrow Annie Proulx’s new, career-spanning collection of short fiction. “Reading it is like opening the hall closet and having the contents of an antique junk shop, laced with spiky and rusted farm implements, spill out and maim your dog,” writes Dwight Garner in his review. “I mean this as praise.”

Scrub your stainless steel with help from the studious kitchen porters at Wirecutter, who have found the very best cleaning powder.

NEWSLETTER · 03 Nov 2026, 12:09

😸 Anthropic’s $895B receipts

PLUS: Proton drops AI re-interviews + Anthropic’s $895B tab ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

November 03, 2026 | Listen online | Read online

Welcome, humans.

So apparently Microsoft wants to put words in people's mouths. Literally. Futurism reported that Microsoft filed a patent for AI that rewrites what a speaker says during a live video, then reshapes their lips and face so the edit looks natural.

Here's the setup. AI agents watch the live feed and check each word against rules you set or a viewer's profile. When a word trips a rule, the system swaps or deletes it, then patches the audio and video around the change so there's no visible jump. Replacement words can even come from an internet search, and the original feed may be stored as a reference copy of what was really said. The patent's examples include:

Live translation, so you see and hear a speaker in your own language.

Cutting filler words like um and ah, plus stutters.

Removing slang such as y'all, along with words the system flags as offensive or mumbled.

One caveat: a patent is a claim on an idea, not a product you can use, and Microsoft didn't respond to Futurism. But the worry is easy to picture. Everyone watching the same live event could get a slightly different version of what was said. Somewhere, a Texan just felt a disturbance in the Force.

😸 Proton Scrapped Plans to Re-Interview Staff About Their AI Use After Backlash

Picture your boss announcing that nearly everyone at the company will sit for another round of interviews. The topic is how much you use AI. Cool cool cool, no pressure.

That was the plan at Proton, the privacy company behind Proton Mail and Proton VPN, until CEO Andy Yen canceled it, 404 Media reported. The story rests on six current employees and audio from a roughly two-hour all-hands meeting.

Here's what happened:

Yen planned re-interviews with nearly the whole company, where employees would explain how they use AI.

Employees told 404 Media they believed people who used AI too little could be fired.

A 404 Media investigation the day before the cancellation showed Yen pushing AI hard across Proton, sometimes over security concerns.

Staff and Proton users pushed back, and Yen dropped the interviews.

The detail from that earlier investigation is the one we can't stop thinking about. In late August, an employee in Proton's security Slack channel suggested spending a few days on penetration testing before releasing new code. That means attacking your own software the way a hacker would, so you find the holes first. Yen reportedly brushed the idea off as a loser's mindset and told staff to stop handcrafting code and use AI more.

That lands hard at this company. People pay Proton to keep their private stuff private, so a CEO pushing speed over a security check hits the one thing customers care about most. Users have been vocal about exactly that, and some called for Yen to resign. Not the brand moment you plan for.

Here's our own interpretation. Counting AI use measures activity. It can't tell the engineer who used AI to ship clean work from the one who used it to look compliant. And once usage gets graded, the careful person who asks for a security test starts to look like the slow one.

What we don't know: the report doesn't say how the interviews would have worked, how many employees were involved beyond nearly everyone, or whether Proton is changing its approach to AI. Proton didn't provide a comment in the article.

Watch two things. At Proton, whether the company says anything about how it reviews code before release, since that's what its users keep asking. At your own job, what your manager counts as using AI: hours spent in the tool, or what you shipped with it.

🎓 AI Skill of the Day: Keep a weekly log of what AI actually did for you

If the Proton story has you wondering what you'd say when your own boss asks how you use AI, here's a fix that takes about five minutes on a Friday.

The problem with answering "a lot" is that hours in a chat window say nothing about whether your work got better. A log of results does. Instead of counting how often you used AI, you record what you asked it to do, what you checked or changed yourself, and what came out the other end. That last part is what holds up in a review, because it puts your judgment right next to the tool's output.

Here's the workflow:

Gather the week's raw material: your task list, meeting notes, or a list of what you sent. Skip anything your employer says can't go into an AI tool.

Paste it into your AI tool of choice with the prompt below.

Fix any guessed numbers, then save the one-page result somewhere you'll find it.

Below are my task list and notes from this week: [paste here] List every task where I used AI. For each one, give me: - What I asked AI to do - What I checked, edited, or decided by hand - The result, with a number only if I gave you one (time saved, errors caught, things shipped) If you would have to guess a number, don't. Ask me for the real one instead. Keep it to one page.

Future you, staring down review season, will be very grateful.

Have a specific skill you want to learn? Request it here.

Analytics on Live Data Without Leaving Postgres

When analytics on Postgres slows down, most teams add a second database. Then they manage pipelines, sync lag, and drift forever. TimescaleDB extends Postgres instead. Analytics run on live data, in the database you already have. No pipeline. No migration. No new query language.

🛠️ Tuesday Tool Tip: Stop fixing the same video three times

You finish editing a video for YouTube. Now you need versions for TikTok, Instagram, and LinkedIn, each with different dimensions. And suddenly, you're doing the same editing job all over again.

Here's a trick: Google Flow lets you build your own reusable editing tool with a plain-English instruction.

Open a Flow project, then select Tools → Create Tool.

Enter: "Build a video-resizing tool with 16:9, 9:16, and 1:1 presets. Include a preview and controls for adjusting the crop before exporting."

Generate the tool, review how it works, and save it for your next project.

You can also start with Google's existing Video Resizer and explore remixing it for your workflow.

The useful part? You can keep refining your mini-app as your editing needs change, without having to code it yourself.

See how Google's custom Tools work.

Note: Creating custom Tools requires an eligible paid Google AI subscription. Existing prebuilt Tools are available to all Flow users.

📶 MIT shows AI improving AI, no training required

PLUS: Turn one interview into two clips with Riverside ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

Good morning, AI enthusiasts, and welcome to our 4,563 new readers. Every few weeks, it seems another leading AI lab says that an autonomous AI researcher is closer to reality. New MIT research just gave a peek at how much of that capability may be sitting inside models already available to the public.

GPT-6 Astra went inside a smaller AI with poor math scores and improved it directly, skipping the training that normally does that work. Once improving a model is as simple as pointing another one at it, AI’s already frantic pace could hit another gear.

In today’s AI rundown:

OpenAI’s Astra rewrites other AI from the inside

OpenAI grades its AI on everyday tasks

Turn one interview into two clips with Riverside

OpenAI joins Abu Dhabi’s AI-native government push

LATEST DEVELOPMENTS

AI RESEARCH

🔬 OpenAI’s Astra rewrites other AI from the inside

Image source: MIT CSAIL

The Rundown: MIT researchers just published research showing that OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra can improve some AI models by directly rewriting their weights — the numbers that decide how a model behaves — rather than putting them through more training.

The details:

Weights are normally set through training, since even small AI models have far too many to read or adjust by hand.

Alibaba’s small Qwen model scored 7.9% in math until Astra changed just 0.016% of those weights to cut its habit of repeating itself, hitting 48.1% after the fix.

The same approach did little for Google’s smaller Gemma 3, which lacked a single flaw like Qwen’s repetition, barely moving its math score.

Smaller tests also had Astra name the game an AI had learned from its weights alone, and write a simple Breakout player from scratch without training.

The authors say this capability could one day let AI upgrade itself, add or remove skills in other models on request, or check them for hidden problems.

Why it matters: The top AI labs are racing toward self-improving, automated AI researchers, and while this research is just one small example, it may be a sign of where things are headed. Once models are strong enough to alter and improve their weights, we’re in major “takeoff” territory, where AI starts speeding up its own progress.

TOGETHER WITH OPTIMIZELY

🔎 Finding AI use cases in your workflows

The Rundown: When it comes to AI planning, the highest-value use cases are already living inside your workflows — you just have to know where to look. Optimizely’s template helps you find AI use cases that really matter in just a few minutes.

The template gives you:

A 3-step framework to build the foundation of your next AI agent

The exact process that turned a 2-3 hour GA4 reporting task into a 5-minute one

Real use cases mapped to how your team actually works

OPENAI

📋 OpenAI grades its AI on everyday tasks

Image source: OpenAI

The Rundown: OpenAI just introduced LifeVal, a new test grading how well AI gets through everyday errands like booking appointments, buying groceries, and filling out government forms, with tasks chosen based on what people say they want help with.

The details:

The selected task areas come from a poll of 4K people on where they want help, and global time-use surveys on what everyday tasks consume time.

LifeVal has been OpenAI’s internal measurement for the past year, as it trained models to browse the web, operate a computer, and work in connected apps.

OpenAI’s models have gone from an average score of 49.3% (GPT-5.2) to 80.6% (GPT-6 Astra), though just 0.6 points of that gain came after July’s GPT-5.6.

Astra’s “best recorded” scores hit 100% on 10 of 16 tasks OpenAI listed, like replacing a lost engagement ring, with LSAT help scoring the lowest at 88.7%.

Why it matters: We look at benchmark results on everything from coding and math to business tasks, but few have measured how models handle the everyday tasks that a more general audience is asking AI to do. Seeing this eval tested across other models would be an interesting data point for the personal agent meta seen across the industry.

AI TRAINING

🎬 Turn one interview into two clips with Riverside

The Rundown: In this guide, you’ll use Riverside’s new agents to turn a licensed interview into two captioned videos. You’ll choose useful moments, check the edits, and prepare post drafts without filming anything new.

Step-by-step:

Download the guide’s openly licensed interview and save its credit. Then, make an MP4 copy and upload it to a Riverside practice project

Ask Clipper for short passages that make sense on their own. Make sure to watch the surrounding interview before choosing your two clips

Ask Editor for a matching vertical layout, keeping the full picture visible and leaving space for captions and the source label

Check the captions against the speech, then ask Marketer for two short post drafts. Keep the interview’s date and full credit with each one

Finally, export both clips and watch the saved files. Check the framing, sound, words, and ending before handing them off

Pro tip: A shorter clip can change what someone seems to mean. Keep the question or qualifying sentence when the answer depends on it. Visit The Rundown University to get the full guide, materials, files, and more.

AI & THE WORLD

🤝 OpenAI joins Abu Dhabi’s AI-native government push

Image source: Abu Dhabi Media Office

The Rundown: OpenAI just sealed four agreements in Abu Dhabi, bringing its AI to state oil giant ADNOC, the emirate’s government, and state investor Mubadala, while a UAE group is reportedly set to be the top investor in the AI giant’s raise of up to $20B.

The details:

ADNOC’s deal, the largest of the four, aims to cut oil field planning from months to hours by running decades of well data through OpenAI’s models.

Food imports come first in “Machine-Readable Government,” a project turning some Abu Dhabi rules into text AI can read to help streamline permit processes.

Abu Dhabi already has a $3.5B plan for AI across all public services, and a separate OpenAI and Microsoft program aims to train 20K staff and the public.

OpenAI’s models will help Mubadala weigh risk and spot new bets, while a five-year pact with AI firm G42’s Inception42 targets UAE businesses and schools.

Why it matters: Albania gained headlines last year by naming a digital AI “minister,” but Abu Dhabi’s version is more practical — giving AI better access to existing data and rules to improve efficiency. The circular AI dealmaking also continues, with Abu Dhabi acting as both a major deployer of OpenAI’s tech and potentially the biggest investor.

QUICK HITS

COMMUNITY AI WORKFLOW OF THE DAY

▸ Judd built an AI research team that fact-checks itself

Today’s workflow comes from reader Judd Slivka:

“I use a four-step system built on Grok Bot to get AI-generated research reports with fewer errors. It’s for anyone who relies on AI research and needs to trust the numbers and quotes in it.

I start with a research bot that has specific instructions. They cover the types of sources it should use, separating known facts from speculative claims, and cross-checking claims. The report goes to a skeptic. The skeptic is meant to be contrarian but not a roadblock. It asks for evidence, looks for logical fallacies, and creates a short, ranked list of problems. The problem list goes back to the researcher, who then passes the report to my fact-checker. The fact-checker double-checks every number and every quote or paraphrase against the actual document it was found in.

The result is a report with fewer errors, but also flags on what’s verified and what isn’t. Far and away the most valuable workflow that I’ve built. It costs more in terms of usage, but it’s been working great.”

See the workflow on our Community Workflow Hub →

United States

-President Donald Trump delivered a “closing argument” at the recently reopened Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool in Washington, D.C., yesterday.

The event marked his last before today’s elections.

View his full remarks here:

According to the latest Reuters/Ipsos poll published yesterday, Democrats hold an eight-point lead over Republicans in the generic ballot.

Meanwhile, Trump’s approval rating stands at 32%.

Rasmussen shows Trump’s approval at 41%.

In a rare post to X yesterday, Trump said, “I am going to give all adult citizens in the United States of America $5,000” if Republicans keep control of Congress following today’s elections.

@realDonaldTrump

-Sabato’s Crystal Ball final ratings show a likely Democratic majority in the Senate after today’s vote, with the party holding the edge in the toss-up states of Alaska, Kansas, Maine, Michigan, and Texas.

-A New York Times/Siena poll shows Democrats leading in Michigan, Texas, Maine, Ohio, Kansas, Alaska, and Iowa.

Africa

-U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said yesterday that South Africa and Rwanda would be excluded from a major U.S.-Africa free trade deal, accusing the countries of engaging in practices “inconsistent” with the pact’s eligibility criteria.

The exclusion will take effect in January.

The U.S. trade law, formally known as the African Growth and Opportunity Act (AGOA), was enacted in May 2000. The law gives duty-free access to the U.S. market for eligible countries in sub-Saharan Africa.

Upon signing the law, President Bill Clinton said: “The legislation I sign today is about more than development and trade. It's about transforming our relationship with two regions full of good people trying to build good futures who are very important to our own future.”

Fighting between the countries has largely been paused for roughly three months.

In Kyiv, hackers sent fake alerts about a nuclear apocalypse. We hardly noticed. Diana Berg. As Europe erupts in furious protests, we prep for a cold, dark winter. Rage and fear require energy we simply don’t have Explosions woke me at 3am the other night. Sitting on the bathroom floor behind two load-bearing walls, scrolling through Telegram to see where Russian missiles were heading, I noticed the light was on. Power had returned while we slept. On pure autopilot, I stood up, stuffed laundry into the washing machine, set a quick cycle hoping it would finish spinning before the next blackout, and sat back down to wait out the attack. In our fifth year of full-scale war, air raid apps have introduced a new bureaucratic absurdity: threats are colour-coded into yellow for drones and red for missiles, as if a “yellow” drone alert were merely a light autumn drizzle. This one was red. But honestly, ballistic missiles are almost convenient. Unlike the Russian drones that buzz monotonously for an hour and under which we now simply roll over and keep sleeping, hoping for the best, ballistic missiles take just minutes to arrive. A five-minute break on the bathroom floor barely interrupts your sleep, or your laundry.
